Unifying (Quantum) Statistical and Parametrized (Quantum) Algorithms
Alexander Nietner

Abstract
Kearns’ statistical query (SQ) oracle (STOC’93) lends a unifying perspective for most classical machine learning algorithms. This ceases to be true in quantum learning, where many settings do not admit, neither an SQ analog nor a quantum statistical query (QSQ) analog. In this work, we take inspiration from Kearns’ SQ oracle and Valiant’s weak evaluation oracle (TOCT’14) and establish a unified perspective bridging the statistical and parametrized learning paradigms in a novel way. We explore the problem of learning from an evaluation oracle, which provides an estimate of function values, and introduce an extensive yet intuitive framework that yields unconditional lower bounds for learning from evaluation queries and characterizes the query complexity for learning linear function classes. The framework is directly applicable to the QSQ setting and virtually all algorithms based on loss function optimization. Our first application is to extend prior results on the learnability of output distributions of quantum circuits and Clifford unitaries from the SQ to the (multi-copy) QSQ setting, implying exponential separations between learning stabilizer states from (multi-copy) QSQs versus from quantum samples. Our second application is to analyze some popular quantum machine learning (QML) settings. We gain an intuitive picture of the hardness of many QML tasks which goes beyond existing methods such as barren plateaus and the statistical dimension, and contains crucial setting-dependent implications. Our framework not only unifies the perspective of cost concentration with that of the statistical dimension in a unified language but exposes their connectedness and similarity.



1
Introduction

Modern machine learning algorithms are predominantly understood within two paradigms: statistical and parametrized algorithms.
Statistical algorithms interact with the data through empirical averages and their formal study dates back to Kearns’ seminal work on statistical query (SQ) learning
[
Kea93
]
.
Since then, the SQ toolbox has become a crucial technique in computational and statistical learning theory
[
FGR+17
,
Rey20
,
AK21
,
AAK21
]
.
In the paradigm of parametrized learning the algorithm is separated into a parametrized model and an optimization routine that fits the model regarding a loss function, where the loss function models the access to the data.
Examples of parametrized algorithms range from classics like linear regression and support vector machines to state-of-the-art methods like neural networks, deep learning and backpropagation.

Two of the main driving forces behind the success of the SQ toolbox undeniably are its ability to prove
unconditional
lower bounds on the complexity of learning problems together with its near universal ability to capture most practical and theoretical learning techniques.
Indeed, with the famous exception of Gaussian elimination there exist SQ analogs for most learning algorithms ranging from differential privacy, evolvable algorithms, gradient descent, convex optimization, Markov-chain Monte Carlo, neural networks, backpropagation and “
pretty much everything else
”
[
Rey20
]
.
Thus, the framework of statistical queries deals as a unified framework for the statistical and the parametrized paradigm in classical machine learning.

Arunachalam et al. have generalized the SQ to the quantum statistical query oracle (QSQ), which models empirical expectation values of observables
[
AGY20
]
.
However, not only does the SQ framework no longer contain many parametrized algorithms in the quantum sphere,
but even the QSQ oracle fails to revive this property:
A typical classical parametrized loss function is

l(\vartheta)=\frac{1}{2}\Ex_{x\sim P}[(f(x)-g(\vartheta,x))^{2}]=1-\Ex_{(x,y)\sim P_{f}}[yg(\vartheta,x)]
which identifies the parametrized problem of learning an unknown
f
with SQ learning an unknown distribution
P_{f}
.
In contrast, a common quantum loss function is

l(\vartheta)=\tr[\rho(\vartheta)O]
, where the unknown observable
O
takes the role of
f
and which does not correspond to a state learning problem of an unknown
\rho_{O}
.
As such there is no unified framework for (Q)SQ and parametrized learning algorithms in the general context of quantum learning
[
AA23
]
and in particular for quantum machine learning (QML).

In this work, we introduce the
evaluation oracle
to establish a novel unified perspective bridging parametrized and statistical learning algorithms.
The
evaluation oracle for a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to M
returns, when queried with some
x\in\mathcal{X}
an approximation
to
f(x)
within some tolerance
and is inspired by Kearns’ SQ oracle
[
Kea93
]
and closely related to Valiant’s
weak evaluation oracle

[
Val14
]
.
Within this new perspective, we introduce a comprehensive framework for unconditional lower bounds that brings the strengths of the SQ framework to quantum learning:

1.

When applied to classical statistical problems our results reduce to Feldman’s well-established characterization of learning from statistical queries
[
Fel17
]
.

2.

It applies directly to parametrized learning problems without the need to rewrite the problem in a specific form, while the bounds are in general identical to statistical query bounds if there exists a statistical query analogue to the problem.

3.

The framework immediately applies to quantum statistical queries and generalizations thereof and generalizes Feldman’s characterization to the quantum statistical query setting, and many
linear learning problems
.

4.

Within our framework we can analyze, extend and unite so far unconnected approaches in QML such as barren plateaus
[
MBS+18
]
, narrow gorges
[
AHC+22
]
and cost concentration
[
Nap22
]
with quantum correlational (QCSQ) and quantum unitary statistical queries (QUSQ)
[
AK22a
]
.

Drawing from George Box’s widely acknowledged quote, “All models are wrong, but some are useful”, we believe that the evaluation oracle serves as a valuable solution, filling an essential gap in QML and enriching the current toolbox for statistical learning analysis.

1.1
Overview of Formalism

This section gives an overview of our formalism. See
Part

I
for a more detailed presentation.
We start by introducing general learning problems in
Section

1.1.1

and then introduce the evaluation oracle as a natural generalization of the SQ oracle in
Section

1.1.2
.
The actual formalism is then presented in
Sections

1.1.3
,
1.1.4
,
1.1.5
,
1.1.6
and
1.1.7
.

1.1.1
General Learning Problems

We define general learning problems, which are at the core of our formalism, similar to distributional search problems as defined by
[
FGR+17
]
.

Definition 1.1:
(General learning Problems)

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of objects called
source objects

and let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of objects called
target objects
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a map from source objects
s\in\mathcal{S}
to a subset of targets
\mathcal{Z}(s)\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, defined to be the valid solutions to
s
.
The problem of learning
\mathcal{Z}
over
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
from
q
queries to an oracle
\mathcal{O}
is to find a valid solution
t\in\mathcal{Z}(s)
from
q
queries to an oracle
\mathcal{O}(s)
representing the access to the unknown
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

To gain some intuition let us consider the following learning problem called heavy Fourier search (HFS).
For
\theta>0
the task of HFS is to, given access to an unknown function
f:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\to[-1,1]
, return a vector
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
with Fourier coefficient
\hat{f}(z)
of weight at least
\theta
, i.e.
\absolutevalue{\Ex_{x}[\chi_z(x)f(x)]}_{x}[\chi_{z}(x)f(x)]\geq\theta
, where
\chi_{z}(x)=(-1)^{x\cdot z}
denotes the parity function corresponding to
z
1
1

1

Throughout
\mathbb{F}_{2}=\{0,1\}
denotes the finite field with two elements with modulo two addition and multiplication, respectively.
.

The corresponding general learning problem is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{HFS}}^{\theta}:[-1,1]^{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\to\mathcal{P}\quantity(\mb F_2^n)::f\mapsto z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\;\middle|\;\quantity{z\in\mb F_2^n\midvert\abs{\Ex_{x\sim\mb F_2^n}\qty[\chi_z(x)f(x)]}\geq\theta}\geq\theta\,.

(1.1)

Another important learning problem that we investigate closely following
[
Fel17
]
is the generic many-to-one decision problem (see
3.1
).
For sets
\mathcal{S}
and
\quantity{s^*}
the decision problem
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
asks to decide whether an unknown object
s\in\mathcal{S}\cup\quantity{s^*}
is in
\mathcal{S}
. In particular, the problem is defined by the map

\displaystyle\text{$\mathtt{Dec}$}(\mathcal{S},s^{*}):\mathcal{S}\cup\quantity{s^*}\to\quantity{\set{\mc S}, \set{\set{s^*}}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\quantity{\mc S, \set{s^*}})::s\mapsto\begin{cases}\quantity{\mc S}&,\quad s\in\mathcal{S}\\
\quantity{\set{s^*}}&,\quad s\notin\mathcal{S}\,.\end{cases}

(1.2)

Thus,
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
is a learning problem over sources
\mathcal{S}\cup\quantity{s^*}
and targets
\quantity{\mc S,\set{s^*}}
.

1.1.2
Evaluation Oracle

General learning problems capture virtually any learning problem.
Here, we are interested in settings, where the data access is of a specific structure, as formalized by the oracle
\mathcal{O}
.
Recall, that the SQ oracle
\mathtt{Stat}_{\tau}(P)
of a distribution
P
over
\mathcal{Y}
and tolerance
\tau>0
returns, when queried with a function
\phi:\mathcal{Y}\to[-1,1]

a value
v
such that

v-\absolutevalue{v-\Ex_{x\sim P}\qty[\phi(x)]}_{x\sim P}\quantity[\phi(x)]\leq\tau
.
In other words,
statistical algorithms as in
[
FGR+17
]
correspond to algorithms that access the data distribution only via coarse expectation values.
This leads us to the following perspective.

Perspective0:

Statistical algorithms treat the distribution
P
as a function

\displaystyle P:[-1,1]^{\mathcal{Y}}\to[-1,1]::\phi\mapsto P[\phi]=\Ex_{x\sim P}[\phi(x)]\,,

and the SQ oracle implements a point-wise
\tau
-accurate approximation of this function.

This reading leads to a natural generalization from statistical to evaluation queries.

Definition 1.2:
(Evaluation Oracle)

Let
\tau>0
,
\mathcal{X}
be a set and
(M,\mathrm{d})
be a metric space.
For any
s\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
we denote by
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(s)
the
Evaluation Oracle
of
s
with tolerance
\tau
.
When queried with some
x\in\mathcal{X}
it returns some
v\in M
such that
\mathrm{d}(v,s(x))\leq\tau
.

Let us list a few examples to highlight the different use cases.
We first consider the quantum statistical query (QSQ) oracle.
The QSQ oracle
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\rho)
for an
N
-dimensional quantum state
\rho
returns, when queried with a bounded Hermitian operator
O\in\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
,
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
a value
v
such that
\absolutevalue{v-\tr[\rho O]}\leq\tau
.

Secondly, let
l:\Theta\to[0,1]
be a loss function of some parametrized learning problem with parameters
\vartheta\in\Theta
and where we understand
[0,1]
as equipped with the metric
\mathrm{d}(a,b)=\absolutevalue{a-b}
.
Then, the evaluation oracle
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(l)
returns, when queried with
\vartheta\in\Theta
a value
v
such that
\absolutevalue{v-l(\vartheta)}\leq\tau
.

Finally, let us mention the
\mathtt{1Bit}_{\tau}(P)
oracle. When queried with a Boolean function
h:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
it returns a probability distribution on a single bit
\tilde{p}
such that
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\tilde{p},p)\leq\tau
.
Here,
p
denotes the distribution
p(z)=\Pr_{x\sim P}[x\in h^{-1}(z)]
corresponding to first sampling
x\sim P
and then outputting
h(x)
.
As such, it corresponds to the evaluation oracle for a specific set of functions
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\to\Delta(2)
with
\Delta(N)
the
N
-dimensional simplex of discrete probability distributions endowed with the total variation distance
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}
.
The
\mathtt{1Bit}
oracle is tightly related with the
\mathtt{1\!\!-\!\!Stat}
oracle as introduced in
[
FGR+17
]
.

1.1.3
Trivial and Non-Trivial Learning

The notion of trivial and non-trivial learning will be a recurrent theme in our formalism.

Definition 1.3:
(Trivial and Non-Trivial Learning)

For a learning problem
\mathcal{Z}
over
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathcal{T}
and
\mu
a probability measure over
\mathcal{S}
we denote by

\displaystyle\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z, \mu)=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_t]

(1.3)

the
Probability of Trivial Learning
.
Similarly, for any
s^{*}\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
we refer to

\mathrm{frac}(\mu,s^{*},\tau)

as the
Maximally Distinguishable Fraction
from
s^{*}
with respect to
\mu
, where

\displaystyle\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)=\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met(s(x), s^*(x))>\tau]\,.

(1.4)

Further
q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)=\max_{f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}}(\mathrm{frac}(\mu,f,\tau)^{-1})
is referred to as the
Query Complexity of Non-Trivial Learning
from evaluation queries with respect to
\mu
and
\tau
.

It is easy to see that
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z,\mu)
is the probability of success over
\mu
of solving
\mathcal{Z}
by the trivial algorithm, which without queries always returns a fixed
t
.
Similarly,
\mathrm{frac}(\mu,s^{*},\tau)
is the largest fraction with respect to
\mu
which can be distinguished from
s^{*}
from a single query
x
to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
.
As we will later see, the complexity of non-trivial learning
q_{\mathtt{nt}}
then asymptotically lower bounds the complexity for solving non-trivial learning problems, which are learning problems for which
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z,\mu)
is sufficiently small.
It is easy to see how to bound
\mathrm{frac}
and
q_{\mathtt{nt}}
using Markov’s and Chebyshev’s inequality respectively.

Corollary 1.1:
(Bounding
\mathrm{frac}
and
q_{\mathtt{nt}}
)

The maximally distinguishable fraction is bounded by the average discrimination as

\displaystyle\mathrm{frac}(\mu,s^{*},\tau)\leq\tau^{-1}\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Ex_{s\sim\mu}[\mathrm{d}(s(x),s^{*}(x))]\,.

(1.5)

Further, if the variance of
s(x)
with
s\sim\mu
is well-defined for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, it holds

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)\geq\tau^{2}\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\quantity(\max_{x\in\mc X}\Var_{s\sim\mu}[s(x)])_{s\sim\mu}[s(x)]^{-1}\,.

(1.6)

1.1.4
Characterizing Decision Problems

We lower bound the query complexity of the many-to-one decision problem
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
for random algorithms by (see
Section

4.1
)

\displaystyle q\geq 2\cdot(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})\cdot\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)^{-1}\,,

(1.7)

where
\alpha
is the success probability with respect to the internal randomness of the algorithm.
Here, we assume that
\mathcal{S}\subset M^{\mathcal{X}}
,
s^{*}\in M^{\mathcal{X}}\setminus\mathcal{S}
2
2

2

Note that
\{\mathcal{S}\}
is always a valid solution in case
s^{*}\in\mathcal{S}
.
and
\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})
is any probability measure over
\mathcal{S}
.

Similarly to the results from
[
Fel17
]
this bound is tight in the sense that we accommodate it with the corresponding upper bound.
The many-to-one decision problem
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
can be solved with probability at least
\alpha=1-\delta
by a random algorithm using at most
q
queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
, where

\displaystyle q\leq\ln\quantity(\sfrac 1\delta)\cdot\max_{\mu}\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)^{-1}\,.

(1.8)

The intuition of the lower bound follows the minimax philosophy or, in fact, the maximin advantage of solving
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
due to a distinguishing query.
The prefactor
p=2(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})
is the probability, that an algorithm that solves
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
will correctly decide not out of luck, but due to a distinguishing query
x
such that
\mathrm{d}(s(x),s^{*}(x))>\tau
.
Thus, a successful algorithm for solving
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
must have at least an advantage
p
for the task of finding a distinguishing query between any
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
s^{*}
.
We bound this advantage for any
\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})
by

\displaystyle p\leq\mathrm{Adv}=\max_{\mathcal{A}}\min_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\Ex_{\mathcal{A}}\quantity[L(\mc A(s), \mc A(s^*))]\leq\max_{\mathcal{A}}\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim\mu\\
\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\quantity[L(\mc A(s), \mc A(s^*)) ]=\max_{\mathcal{A}}\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim\mu\\
\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\quantity[L(\mc A(s), \mc A(s^*)) = 1]\,,

where
\Ex_{\mathcal{A}}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\mathcal{A}
’s internal randomness and the loss
L(\mathcal{A}(s),\mathcal{A}(s^{*}))
equals one, if at least one of
\mathcal{A}
’s
q
-many queries distinguishes
s
from
s^{*}
and zero else.
The final bound is then obtained via the union bound and bounding the maximal fraction of
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
\mu
, which any query can distinguish from
s^{*}
.

The upper bound on the other side relies on von Neumann’s minimax theorem.
In particular, by von Neumann’s minimax theorem, we obtain a measure
\nu
over
\mathcal{X}
which, borrowing the term from Feldman
[
Fel17
]
, defines a
random cover
of the decision problem.

1.1.5
Lower Bounds for Verifiable Learning Problems

The lower bound for decision problems can be directly applied to prove lower bounds to
verifiable learning problems
, which are defined as follows.
Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a learning problem over
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=M^{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathcal{T}
and denote by
\mathcal{Z}_{t}=\quantity{s\in\mc S\midvert t\in\mc Z(s)}
.
We say that
\mathcal{Z}
is
p
-verifiable with respect to
s^{*}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{S}
from access to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
, if there exists a family of algorithms
\quantity{\mc A_t\midvert t\in\mc T}
such that for every
t\in\mathcal{T}
the algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, solves
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{Z}_{t},s^{*})
from
p
queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
.

As such, we lower bound the
average case
query complexity of learning
\mathcal{Z}
with respect to the measure
s\sim\mu
over instances by
q
, where

\displaystyle q+p\geq 2\cdot\quantity(\alpha-\sfrac 12)\cdot\beta\cdot\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)^{-1}\,.

(1.9)

Here
\alpha
is the success probability with respect to the algorithm’s internal randomness and
\beta
is the success probability with respect to
s\sim\mu
.

The intuition behind this bound is the observation that any algorithm for learning
\mathcal{S}
from
q
queries implies, based on the assumption of
p
-verifiability, an algorithm for solving
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
from
q+p
queries.
The actual bound then follows from applying this argument to an arbitrary
\beta
-fraction of
\mathcal{S}
on which the average-case learner is successful.

Similarly, we can iterate the same argument in another way.
For some
s^{*}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
let
\mathcal{S}^{*}
be any set such that the restriction of
\mathcal{Z}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}=\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{*}
, is
p
-verifiable with respect to
s^{*}
.
Then, we find that any algorithm for learning
\mathcal{Z}
with probability
\beta
over
s\sim\mu
and
\alpha
over its internal randomness requires at least
q
queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
, with

\displaystyle q+p\geq 2\cdot\quantity(\alpha-\sfrac 12)\cdot\beta-\quantity(\beta- \Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[s\in\mc S^*])_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc S^*]\cdot\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)^{-1}\,.

(1.10)

1.1.6
Lower Bounds for General Learning Problems

Let us now look at bounds for general learning problems.
As before we set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=M^{\mathcal{X}}
and let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. We consider the worst-case, as well as deterministic, joint and random average-case query complexity, definitions for which can be found in
Section

2.1.1
.

Deterministic Algorithms:

The previous lower bound is similar to the deterministic average-case lower bound we obtain for general learning problems.
Any deterministic algorithm for learning
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
with probability
\beta
over
s\sim\mu
, given access to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
, must make at least
q
queries, where for any
s^{*}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
it holds

\displaystyle q\geq\quantity(\beta- \sup_{t\in\mc T}\qty[s\in\mc Z_t])\cdot\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)^{-1}\,,

which implies

\displaystyle q\geq\quantity(\beta-\triv[\mc Z,\mu])\cdot q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)\,.

(1.11)

The proof is almost identical to that of the deterministic bound of solving
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
together with the ideas leading to
Equation

1.10
.

Random Algorithms:

The previous lower bound immediately translates to a joint average-case lower bound for random algorithms, where
\beta
denotes the joint success probability over
\mu
and the algorithm’s internal randomness.
The random average-case lower bound however is more involved.
It follows the same ideas as Feldman
[
Fel17
]
.
We first exclude all source objects
s\in\mathcal{S}
, for which there exists a trivial algorithm that, without making any query succeeds on
s
with some probability at least
\gamma
.
In the next step, we apply a similar argument as for
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
in the random setting, where we estimate the probability of correctly solving the problem due to a distinguishing query.
Finally, applying the same arguments that lead to
Equation

1.10
we then obtain for any
s^{*}

\displaystyle q\geq(\alpha-\gamma)\cdot\beta-\sup_{\vartheta}\quantity(\beta-\sup_\vartheta\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[s\in\mc Z_{\vartheta}(\gamma)])_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_{\vartheta}(\gamma)]\cdot\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)^{-1}\,.

(1.12)

Here,
\vartheta\in\Delta(\mathcal{T})
is any probability measure over
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\vartheta}(\gamma)=\quantity{s\in\mc S\midvert\vartheta(\mc Z(s))\geq\gamma}
is the excluded set of “trivially-to-solve” instances:
\mathcal{Z}
can be solved on this set with probability
\gamma
over
\vartheta
– the algorithms internal randomness – without queries, by simply returning a randomly sampled
t\sim\vartheta
.

1.1.7
Characterizing Linear Learning Problems

Finally, we consider learning problems over
linear function classes
.
We will refer to learning problems over linear function classes as linear learning problems.
Simply put, a linear function class is a class of functions that is contained in the dual space of the function domain.
To be more precise, let
\mathcal{V}
be a vector space over
\mathbb{K}
(real or complex) with the metric on
\mathbb{K}
given by the absolute value.
Further, let
\mathcal{V}
be endowed with a norm
\norm{\cdotspace}
and denote by
\mathcal{V}^{\star}
the corresponding dual space endowed with norm
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}
.
A linear function class then is a subset
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\star}
.
To have a well-defined tolerance, we identify the domain of the functions
f\in\mathcal{S}
with the closed unit ball
\overline{B}_{1}(0)\subset\mathcal{V}
with respect to
\norm{\cdotspace}

\displaystyle f:\overline{B}_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{K}::g\mapsto f(g)=\left\langle f,g\right\rangle\,,

(1.13)

where the last equality holds if
\mathcal{V}
has an inner product
\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle
and
\mathcal{V}^{\star}=\mathcal{V}
.

Linear function classes are a powerful concept.
The statistical query framework can be seen as the prototypical linear learning setup.
In
Table

1
we sketch some possible settings.
The oracle corresponds to the evaluation oracle for the given class with respect to
\overline{B}_{1}(0)
.

Oracle

\mathcal{V}

\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle

\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}

\norm{\cdotspace}

Details

\mathtt{Stat}_{\tau}(P)

\mathbb{R}^{N}
, (
\Delta(N)
)

\left\langle P,\phi\right\rangle_{\ell_{2}}=\Ex_{x\sim P}[\phi(x)]

\norm{\cdotspace}_{1}

\norm{\cdotspace}_{\infty}

3.3

\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\rho)

\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
, (
\mathcal{S}(N)
)

\left\langle\rho,O\right\rangle_{HS}=\tr[\rho O]

\norm{\cdotspace}_{\tr}

\norm{\cdotspace}_{\mathrm{op}}

3.4

\mathtt{CSQ}_{\tau}(f)

[N]\to\mathbb{R}\;(=\mathbb{R}^{N})

\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\lambda}=\Ex_{x\sim\lambda}[f(x)g(x)]

\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}

\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}

3.5

\mathtt{QCSQ}_{\tau}(M)

\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
, (
\norm{M}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
)

\left\langle M,O\right\rangle_{\lambda}=\Ex_{\rho\sim\lambda}\quantity[\tr[\rho M]\tr[\rho O]]

\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}

\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}

3.6

\mathtt{QUSQ}_{\tau}(U)

\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
, (
\mathrm{U}(N)
)

\left\langle U,V\right\rangle_{\lambda}=\Ex_{\rho\sim\lambda}\quantity[\tr[U^\dagger V \rho]]

\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}

\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}

3.7

\mathtt{MCSQ}_{\tau}(A)

\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}

\left\langle A,B\right\rangle_{\rho}=\tr[A^{\dagger}\rho B]

\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\rho)}

\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\rho)}

Table 1
:
Some examples for linear functions. The oracle corresponds to the evaluation oracle
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(s)
of the given class for the source object
s
when the domain is restricted to
\overline{B}_{1}(0)
.
The set in the brackets next to the vector space is the set in which the function class is conventionally contained.
This containing set in turn is itself contained in the dual unit sphere
\quantity{s\midvert\dualnorm{s}=1}
.
As throughout,
\Delta(N)
denotes the simplex of
N
-dimensional distributions,
\mathcal{S}(N)
denotes the set of

\mathrm{U}(N)
are the

The representation of the definition of the inner product in the first example only holds for
P\in\Delta(N)\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
and is
\sum_{x}P(x)\phi(x)
more generally. By
\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
we denote the set of
N\times N
dimensional Hermitian matrices.

Characterization of Linear Learning:

We have two main findings regarding the query complexity of linear learning problems in the evaluation oracle framework.
First, we generalize Feldman’s characterization of statistical query learning
[
Fel17
]
to linear learning problems.
Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a linear learning problem over
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{K}^{\overline{B}_{1}(0)}
and
\mathcal{T}
and define for any
s^{*}\in\mathbb{K}^{\overline{B}_{1}(0)}
the
random dimension

\displaystyle\mathtt{rd}(\mathcal{S},s^{*},\tau)=\sup_{\mu}\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)^{-1}=\sup_{\mu}\max_{x\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)}\quantity(\max_{x\in\overline{B}_1(0)}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[\abs{s(x)-s^*(x)}>\tau])_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{s(x)-s^*(x)}>\tau]^{-1}\,.

(1.14)

Note that
\mathtt{rd}(\mathcal{S},s^{*},\tau)
characterizes the complexity of solving
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
from
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
.
The
random dimension of learning
for the problem
\mathcal{Z}
is then defined via

\displaystyle\mathtt{rdl}(\mathcal{Z},\tau,\gamma)=\sup_{s^{*}}\inf_{\vartheta}\mathtt{rd}(\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\vartheta}(\gamma),s^{*},\tau)\,,

(1.15)

with
\mathcal{Z}_{\vartheta}(\gamma)
as defined in the previous section.
By the previous section,
\mathtt{rdl}(\mathcal{Z},\tau,\gamma)
lower bounds the worst-case query complexity as
q\geq(\alpha-\gamma)\mathtt{rdl}(\mathcal{Z},\tau,\gamma)
.
In fact, under some assumptions (for details see
5.5
), this characterization is tight in the sense that there is a random algorithm for learning
\mathcal{Z}
with success probability
\alpha-\delta
from
q
queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
, with

\displaystyle q=O\quantity(\frac{r\cdot\rdl(\mc Z,\tau,\alpha)}{\zeta\cdot\tau^2}\cdot\log\qty(\frac{r}{\tau\cdot\delta}))\,.

(1.16)

Here we assumed that
\mathcal{S}
as well as
\overline{B}_{1}(0)
are compact and that we can set up a mirror descent (MD) scheme.
The factor
\zeta
then is due to the
\zeta
-
regularizer

R
and
r
is the radius of
\mathcal{S}
as measured by the Bregman divergence
D_{R}
.
We note that all settings in
Table

1
admit an MD scheme.
In particular, in the first setting in
Table

1
, when setting
R(x)=\sum_{i}x_{i}\ln(x_i)
the negative entropy function, we obtain the standard multiplicative weights algorithm with
\zeta=1
and obtain the same bounds as in
[
Fel17
]
.
Alternatively, in the QSQ setting, when setting
R(X)=\tr[X\ln(X)]
, then
D_{R}
becomes the quantum relative entropy with
\zeta=\frac{1}{\ln 2}
and MD corresponds to matrix multiplicative weights.
This setting is closely related to Aaronson, Chen, Hazan, Kale and Nayak’s “Online Learning of Quantum States”
[
ACH+19
]
.
Similarly, interpreting the upper bound for the correlational statistical query setting
\mathtt{CSQ}_{\tau}
with
R(x)=\norm{x}_{2}^{2}
, with
\zeta=1
, yields gradient descent-based learning scheme for functions that is closely related to Trevisan, Tulsiani and Vadhan’s celebrated result
[
TTV09
]
.

\epsilon
-Learning:

The lower bounds for random algorithms in the general framework are cumbersome to work with.
Our second result for linear learning thus concerns a more practical lower bound that is inspired by
[
NIS+23
]
.
Denote by
\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\norm{x-y}_{\star}
the distance induced by the dual norm.
For example, for SQ’s
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}=\norm{\cdotspace}_{1}
, such that the induced distance equals the total variation distance
\mathrm{d}_{\star}=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}
, or similarly, for QSQ’s
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}=\norm{\cdotspace}_{\tr}
and thus
\mathrm{d}_{\star}=\mathrm{d}_{\tr}
.
Then, for a linear function class
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\star}
we define
\epsilon
-learning by the map

\displaystyle\mathtt{Learn}(\mathcal{S},\epsilon):\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{V}^{\star})::s\mapsto B_{\epsilon,\mathrm{d}}^{\star}(s)=\quantity{y\in\mc V^\star\midvert\met_\star(x,y)<\epsilon}\,.

(1.17)

In
5.7
we show that any random algorithm for learning
\mathtt{Learn}(\mathcal{S},\epsilon)
with probability at least
\beta
over
s\sim\mu
and
\alpha
over its internal randomness needs to make at least
q
queries, where, for any
s^{*}\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
it holds

\displaystyle q+1\geq 2\cdot\quantity(\alpha-\sfrac 12)\cdot\beta-\quantity(\beta- \Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[\met_\star(s,s^*)<2\epsilon+\tau])_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(s,s^*)<2\epsilon+\tau]\cdot\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)^{-1}\,.

(1.18)

As a side result, we show a connection between
Equations

1.11
and
1.18
.
Inspired by
[
NIS+23
]
, we show in
5.11
that

\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)

together with the mass of the
2\epsilon+\frac{\tau}{2}
ball with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{\star}
and
\mu
around
s^{*}
characterizes the mass of the largest
\epsilon
-ball with respect to
\mu
in
\mathcal{V}^{\star}
:

\displaystyle\max_{r\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(s,r)<\epsilon]\leq\max\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau),\quantity{\kfrac\qty(\mu,s^*,\tau), \Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[\met_\star(s, s^*)<2\epsilon+\frac\tau 2]}_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(s, s^*)<2\epsilon+\frac\tau 2]\,.

(1.19)

For
\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,s^*,\tau)
sufficiently small, plugging
Equation

1.19
into
Equation

1.11
almost yields
Equation

1.18
, up to the contribution of a
\frac{\tau}{2}
shell around the
2\epsilon+\frac{\tau}{2}
-ball around
s^{*}
.

1.2
Overview of Applications

We give an overview about our framework applied to quantum statistical query learning in
Section

1.2.1
and to parametrized learning problems in
Section

1.2.2
.

1.2.1
Quantum Statistical Learning

Related Work:

The statistical query model corresponds to empirical expectation values of bounded functions with respect to a probability distribution.
Analogously, the quantum statistical query (QSQ) model corresponds to empirical expectation values of bounded observables with respect to quantum states, see
Table

1
.
It was first studied by Arunachalam, Grilo and Yuen
[
AGS21
]
and has since been studied various contexts such as quantum differential privacy
[
AK22
]
and classical verification of quantum learning
[
CHI+23
]
.
We also refer to our Note added
Section

1.4
for other more recent related work.

Positive Results:

Before applying our formalism to show QSQ lower bounds we give an incomplete collection of positive results for QSQ learning for various classes in
Section

7.1
.
This includes learning parities with noise in the quantum PAC setting as shown by
[
AGY20
]
, a reinterpretation of matrix product state tomography in the QSQ framework based on
[
CPF+11
]
and free fermion tomography via QSQ’s inspired by
[
BGZ14
,
SW19
,
GKE+18
]
, the latter, together with
[
Nie23
]
, implies a new separation between SQ and QSQ learning.

Hardness Results:

We exemplify our formalism in the quantum statistical query setting by analyzing the output states, and Born distributions, of brickwork random quantum circuits, random Clifford unitaries and Haar random unitaries.
As such, our work extends the studies by Hinsche et al.
[
HIN+22
]
and Nietner et al.
[
NIS+23
]
.
Nietner et al. investigate the average-case complexity of
\epsilon
-learning the Born distributions of random quantum circuits
P(x)=\absolutevalue{\mel{x}{U}{0^n}}
, with
U
being a random quantum circuit from statistical queries.
They focus on two parameters: The depth
d
of the circuit and the fraction
\beta
which the algorithm is required to learn.

We extend this analysis in two ways.
First, we provide the algorithm with the more powerful quantum statistical query oracle,
and second, add as additional parameter the
number of copies
of the unknown state each query can access.
In particular, we introduce the
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}
oracle

\displaystyle\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}(\rho)=\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\rho^{\otimes k})\,.

(1.20)

This additional parameter tunes the allowed correlations and entanglement between individual copies, which the algorithm can exploit.

Through
Sections

7.2
,
7.3
and
7.4
we analyze the learnability of the random unitary and Clifford circuits at different depth and for different numbers of copies
k
. We summarize our results for learning the corresponding quantum states in
Table

2
.
Our
7.12
,
7.11
and
7.16
imply a novel separation between learning stabilizer states from quantum copies
[
AG04
,
Mon17
]
and multi-copy QSQs.
While stabilizer states are known to be efficiently learnable from
O(n)
entangled, respectively
O(n^{2})
unentangled copies, our results show that no such multi-copy QSQ algorithm can exist.

Lower and upper bounds for
\epsilon
-learning the output states of
n

\mathrm{Cl}
) circuits in the

\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}

\tau=\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
. While our work only proves the
d=\omega(\log(n))
column in a worst-case sense, we prove all other lower bounds in the average-case setting for suitable
\beta=1-\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
. For the detailed dependencies on
\tau
,

and

d=O(n)

Note that the upper bound for learning the output states of Clifford circuits, i.e. stabilizer states, in the
k=\Omega(n)
-copy setting is due to
7.17
.
Every QSQ algorithm with inverse polynomial tolerance can be efficiently simulated from access to copies of the unknown state.

7.17
then regards the opposite direction.
It states that every algorithm that requires
k
-copies of an unknown quantum state can be simulated from
\log(\abs{\mc Z(\mc S)})
many queries to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}
.
As such, the lemma makes rigorous the above-mentioned interpolation between statistical learners on the one hand, and correlated and entangled learners on the other hand.

1.2.2
Parametrized Learning

Parametrized learning algorithms are defined with respect to a parametrized model.
Their aim is to find a parameter in parameter space
\vartheta\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that the model fits the data.
This fit is captured by a loss function and an optimality criterion.
The loss function encodes the notion of approximation and can be evaluated up to some precision
\tau
.
The optimality criterion defines what loss is considered sufficiently good a fit.

Prior Work:

The vast majority of practically used classical machine learning algorithms can be recast in the SQ framework.
However, the same ceases to be true for quantum machine learning algorithms and either statistical or quantum statistical queries.
Thus, to analyze the performance of QML algorithms the community borrows from the classical machine learning community another major framework:
Loss Landscape Analysis
(LLA) of an individual loss function.

In LLA one investigates the graph of an individual loss function, which is referred to as the loss landscape.
The most popular obstructions to learning due to LLA are known under the name of barren plateau (BP) and narrow gorge (NG)
[
MBS+18
,
AHC+22
]
, or cost concentration more generally
[
Nap22
]
.
Common to these obstructions is that the analysis is done with respect to a probability measure
\nu
over parameter space
\Theta
.
If a loss function admits a BP, then the gradient exponentially vanishes with overwhelming probability.
If the loss function admits a NG, then the loss is exponentially close to the mean loss with overwhelming probability, while there exists a non-trivial optimal loss which is at least inverse polynomially bounded from the mean loss.
Cost concentration more generally refers to the concentration of the loss function, its gradient, or both around some value.
Cost concentration, BP and NG thus correspond to average-case statements about properties – such as the gradient – of the loss function with respect to
\nu
.
These phenomena therefore imply hardness of learning by algorithms that rely on those properties and which explore the parameter space “according to”
\nu
.
However, these phenomena do not imply rigorous hardness results such as provided by the SQ framework.

Progress in this direction was made by Anschuetz and Kiani.
In
[
AK22a
]
they introduced the
quantum correlational
and the
quantum unitary statistical query
oracles
\mathtt{QCSQ}
and
\mathtt{QUSQ}
.
These oracles are designed to model the parameter shift routine used for evaluating the gradient in many QML models (
\mathtt{QCSQ}
) and the evaluation of the average fidelity in unitary compiling (
\mathtt{QUSQ}
).
Further, they generalized Blum et al.’s statistical dimension
[
BFJ+94
]
to their setting and apply this to show hardness for several learning problems.
The
\mathtt{QCSQ}
as well as the
\mathtt{QUSQ}
setting assumes that the unknown object has to be approximated with respect to a distribution over quantum states.
This assumption is crucial for their analysis as it maps the problem into the statistical query framework.

Our Contribution:

On a conceptual level, our approach can be seen as a combination of the SQ and the LLA philosophy.
We improve the previously mentioned approaches in the following ways.

•

Due to its generality, our framework can be applied to virtually any parametrized learning problem. As such, we can drop the central assumptions in
[
AK22a
]
including the ability to map the problem to the SQ setting and that the optimizer uses the parameter shift rule (
\mathtt{QCSQ}
) respectively the average fidelity (
\mathtt{QUSQ}
).

•

Similar to the statistical dimension by Blum et al. the dimension due to Anschuetz and Kiani is of a worst-case nature. Our formalism in contrast is of an average-case nature and as such yields insights about the generality of the derived bounds. This is particularly appealing in the analysis of practical algorithms since for any sufficiently universal underlying model worst-case hardness is to be expected.

•

Our framework can be applied both, either directly to the problem on the level of the loss function, or to the induced problem on the level of the actual access on which the optimization algorithm relies on (e.g. the parameter shift evaluation in case of
\mathtt{QCSQ}
). This can be used in order to distinguish whether hardness is due to the structure of the analyzed class, or whether hardness is due to the implementation of the optimization algorithm.

•

We can directly connect the existence of BP’s and NG’s with hardness results in our framework.
As such, our framework naturally points out the difference between inherently hard problems, and loss functions where hardness of optimization is due to a poorly chosen measure.

Set-up:

A loss function is a map
l:\Theta\to[0,1]
and for a class of loss functions
\mathcal{L}\subset[0,1]^{\Theta}
an algorithm is given access to an unknown
l\in\mathcal{L}
called the
access loss function
via the oracle
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(l)
3
3

3

For different access, such as gradient evaluations, one can simply modify the given oracle access.
.

In general, we assume that to each loss function
l
it exists an associated
operational loss function

l_{\mathrm{op}}
which, in principle, may be any function
\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
.
The aim of introducing the operational loss function is to model settings in which an algorithm optimizes one loss function in order to achieve low risk with respect to another loss function.
An example would be
\epsilon
-learning in total variation distance
l_{\mathrm{op}}(\vartheta)=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_{\vartheta},P_{\text{true}})
from maximum mean discrepancy access
l(\vartheta)=(P_{\vartheta}-P_{\text{true}})^{\dagger}\cdot K\cdot(P_{\vartheta}-P_{\text{true}})
.
The corresponding learning problem is then defined via the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{P}(\Theta)::l\mapsto\quantity{\hat\vartheta\midvert l_\mrm{op}(\hat\vartheta)\leq\mrm{opt}(\Theta,l_\mrm{op},\epsilon)}\,,

(1.21)

where, if not stated differently,
\mathrm{opt}(\Theta,l_{\mathrm{op}},\epsilon)=\min_{\vartheta}l_{\mathrm{op}}(\vartheta)+\epsilon
is the optimality criterion defining the solution set
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(l)
.

Most optimization routines used in practice are either deterministic, or only exploit a mild degree of randomness.
This is, randomness is usually restricted to a random initialization of parameters and a probabilistic perturbation to the update rule, for example a stochastic evaluation of the loss function, or its derivatives.
We exemplify in
Section

8.3.1
how random initializations can be analyzed by our deterministic bounds, which also implies the conventional interpretation of barren plateaus as in
Remark

8.2
.
Further, we assume that the parametrization and the initial parameters are not fine-tuned to the precise probabilistic update as in e.g.
[
AS20
]
.
To be precise, we assume that the stochastic update is well modelled by the imprecise evaluation as measured by
\tau
.

We now focus on the deterministic average-case bounds.
From
Section

1.1
we immediately inherit the lower bound on the query complexity of solving
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
from
q
many queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
.
For any
g:\Theta\to[0,1]
it holds

\displaystyle q\geq\frac{\beta-\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l_\mrm{op}(\vartheta)\leq\mrm{opt}(\Theta,l_\mrm{op},\epsilon)]}{\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{l(\vartheta) - g(\vartheta)}>\tau]}

or similarly

\displaystyle q\geq(\beta-\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z^\epsilon,\mu))\cdot q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)\,.

(1.22)

Finally, note that the special case
l=l_{\mathrm{op}}
and
\min_{\vartheta}l(\vartheta)=0
for all
l
is
1
-verifiable.
Thus, in this setting the above bounds hold for any probabilistic or quantum algorithm with
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z^\epsilon,\mu)
replaced by
\sup_{\vartheta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}[l(\vartheta)\leq\epsilon]
.

Connection to BP and NG:

In the following, we assume that
l_{\mathrm{op}}=l
with
\min_{\vartheta}l(\vartheta)=0
.
Introducing the dual learning problem
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
of a learning problem
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})::t\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{t}\,,

(1.23)

we can then connect BP’s and NG’s with hardness of learning as in
Equation

1.22
.

We show in
8.2
that average-case hardness in the sense of
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z^\epsilon,\mu)=1-\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
together with
\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,g,\tau)=\tau^{2}2^{-\Omega(n)}
implies a narrow gorge in an
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
fraction of instances of the dual problem
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\epsilon}
.
Here, the vanishing maximally distinguishable fraction translates to the sharp concentration of
every
dual loss function.
Similarly, the assumption on the probability of trivial learning translates to the existence of a non-trivial minimal loss for a non-negligible fraction of dual loss functions.
Using a trick from
[
AHC+22
]
, namely that, for many quantum models one can compute each gradient component from two specific queries to the loss function, we can then relate cost concentration to barren plateaus.
Our
8.1
assumes parametrized quantum models that admit an efficient parameter shift rule.
As such, we find that hardness of non-trivial learning as measured by
\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu,g,\tau)=\tau^{2}2^{-\Omega(n)}
implies the existence of a barren plateau in
every
instance of the dual problem.
Here we stress that the same argument works for any model class for which one can calculate the gradient from few specific function evaluations.
Moreover, in contrast to e.g.
[
AK22a
]
, we require the parameter shift rule only as a mathematical trick and do not rely on any particular implementation.

Based on the above observations we introduce the notion of
self-learning
in
8.4
, which leads to self-dual parametrized learning problems.
Thus, hardness of self learning in the sense above is equivalent to the existence of a NG in many loss functions, respectively a BP in every loss function if there is an efficient parameter shift rule.

Explicit Results:

We exemplify a prototypical hard problem for loss function learning in
Section

8.3.2
.
In particular, we show the hardness of learning computational basis states
\ket{z}
from the loss function
l^{z}(\ket{\psi})=1-\absolutevalue{\braket{z}{\psi}}
for both,
\ket{\psi}
being a computational basis state, corresponding to self-learning, and for general
\ket{\psi}
.
Since computational basis states can be easily learned from QSQ’s, it is apparent that the hardness of this task is due to the access given to the problem instance (c.f.
Remark

8.3
).
This underlines the care that has to be taken in the design of loss functions.

Identifying the local projector in terms of the Pauli
Z
gate as
\outerproduct{z_i}{z_i}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathds{1}+(-1)^{z_{i}}Z)
we can connect hardness of learning computational basis states with Cerezo et al.’s work on barren plateaus at any depth for global cost functions
[
CSV+21
]
.
We make this connection precise in
8.5
and
Remark

8.4
where we prove and discuss the hardness of learning global Pauli strings.
We accompany this result with a positive result in
8.6
where we give an efficient algorithm for learning
ZX
strings in the same setting.
This highlights that non-commutativity is not sufficient for the hardness of learning global Pauli strings.

We show how Cerezo et al.’s result on barren plateaus is tightly connected with self-learning parametrized quantum states.
In
Section

8.3.3
we prove hardness of the latter task for any parametrization that contains a
1
-design, which implies a barren plateau in every loss function of the problem at any depth.
As such, we see the above series of results as a generalization of
[
CSV+21
]
which offers an intuitive interpretation.

In
Section

8.3.4
we investigate self-learning of linear and data re-uploading QML models.
Those correspond to models for learning functions
f:\mathcal{X}\to[-1,1]
with respect to the square loss
l^{f}(g)=\Ex_{x}[(f(x)-g(x))^{2}]
.
As we show and explain in
8.8
and
Remark

8.5
, hardness of non-trivial self-learning of function classes is implied by concentration of
f_{\vartheta}
with respect to
\vartheta\sim\mu
.
The models considered here are parametrized by a PQC as
f(x)=\tr[M\mathcal{U}_{\vartheta,x}(\outerproduct{0}{0})]
with
\mathcal{U}_{\vartheta,x}
the unitary channel corresponding to the PQC
U(\vartheta,x)
and
M
a fixed bounded observable.
Via the concentration argument, we prove the hardness of non-trivial learning for linear and data re-uploading models in various regimes, implying the existence of a barren plateau in all instances.
However, we also find that the learning problem is average-case trivial, again due to concentration.
Let us highlight the difference between
8.9
and
8.10
.
While concentration in the first case is due to the parametrization – for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
most functions have the same value – concentration in the latter case is due to the encoding – every function has the same function value for most
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
The former resembles the hardness of self-learning parametrized quantum states while the latter resembles hardness induced by a random initialization as in
Section

8.3.1
.

While the barren plateau in QML models, parametrized quantum states and VQAs is always due to the design properties of the parametrization, our formalism makes explicit the key differences between the different settings.
The barren plateau in VQAs is first and foremost about the initialization of parameters.
In contrast, for parametrized quantum states, and similarly for a global cost function, the barren plateau requires only a 1-design, corresponds to an actual hardness lower bound due to a loss function that obfuscates any useful information and thus can not be alleviated.
The case of QML models is particularly interesting.
Here, a design-induced barren plateau states that, with respect to that measure, most functions are the same.
As such, it is a statement about the initialization.
The fact that those problems are average-case trivial may be an indication that a careful choice of parameters could lead to successful algorithms.
However, a barren plateau due to the design properties of the encoding gates immediately renders the function class impractical as all functions are identical for all but a negligible subset of the data.

We provide an exemplary comparison of our formalism to the SQ formalism introduced by Anschuetz and Kiani
[
AK22a
]
in
Section

8.3.5
.
In analogy to their Proposition 4, we analyze self-learning of the respective function class which is related to optimizing a global cost function.
Using the similarity to linear models from the preceding section we find that self-learning of the function class is average-case trivial while the complexity of non-trivial learning is exponential.
However, in their particular setting we find a much stronger result:
The learning problem they consider is hard already for any non-trivial subset of functions from the class, i.e. for any subset of size at least two.
This result is beyond what can be obtained by analyzing the statistical dimension from
[
AK22a
]
and highlights the fact that hardness in this scenario
is due to the loss function obfuscating the relevant information.

Finally, in
Section

8.3.6
we show that our framework is incapable of yielding super polynomial lower bounds on the query complexity of VQAs for local Hamiltonians.
We contrast this with the results of
[
AK22a
]
about shallow loss functions in
Section

1.3
.

1.3
Discussion and future work

This work revisits the evaluation oracle in a fresh light and provides a new framework for unifying (quantum) statistical and parametrized (quantum) algorithms.
Our framework brings new insights and results into quantum statistical learning and the generalizations thereof.
Crucially, we see the evaluation query toolbox provided as the natural continuation of the SQ toolbox and believe
that it
has the potential to be a core element
in the future analysis of
generic quantum learning algorithms.

Our proofs for the lower bounds in
Sections

7
and
8
heavily rely on concentration
bounds for ensembles of unitaries.
However, we are not aware of existing bounds similar to our multi-copy variance bounds as in
7.16
,
7.14
,
7.19
and
7.21
.
Thus, we believe that these results will be of independent interest to related fields in quantum information such as metrology, tomography and benchmarking.

The simplest way to learn stabilizers is via Bell difference sampling
[
GNW21
]
, which reduces the problem to learning affine subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}
.
Almost the same can be done from two copy access
[
Mon17
]
, with the only difference being an additional unknown shift of the affine subspace.
We have shown in
7.16
that learning stabilizers from two-copy QSQ access is hard.
We believe that this persists for larger yet constant
k
and conject that no efficient
k=O(1)

\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}
algorithm for learning stabilizer states exists.

We note that our formalism can be directly applied to the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA)
[
FGG14
]
.
As such
it is worth mentioning
recent results
[
BGM+22
]
which prove concentration of the set of QAOA loss functions for a practically motivated class of problems in precisely the sense that
\mathrm{frac}=o(1)
.
It remains open whether concentration is sufficiently pronounced to yield a rigorous hardness result or may even imply a rigorous triviality statement, the latter also crucially depending on the precise optimality criterion.

Note that the local VQE architecture studied in Corollary 2
[
AK22a
]
does not satisfy our assumption made in
Section

8.3.6
.
Hence, it is natural to ask which rigorous hardness result one obtains by applying our framework to their setting.
We believe that one can apply the techniques from their analysis to bound the expected variance in the setting from
Section

8.3.1
yielding rigorous lower bounds.
However, it is unclear whether their result, which is in expectation, sufficiently concentrates to obtain a super polynomial lower bound both in our, as well, as in their framework.

Finally, we note that our results are information-theoretic and thus yield unconditional lower bounds.
Consequently, this has the drawback of not capturing the computational complexity.
In particular, a learning problem may be computationally hard yet query efficient, as famously conjectured for LPN with respect to sample versus computational complexity.
In this regard, we point to important work by Bittel and Kliesch
[
BK21
]
, which investigates this precise question.

1.4
Note Added

During the finalization of this manuscript, we became aware of a work by Arunachalam, Havlicek and Schatzki
[
AHS23
]
, which analyzes the role of entanglement and statistics in learning.
In particular, to compare entangled and statistical measurements they, too, adapt Feldman’s
[
Fel17
]
to derive lower bounds on the single-copy quantum statistical query complexity of learning which, consequently, largely match the bounds presented here applied to single-copy QSQs.
The general scope of the two papers however is much different.

Similarly, the recently introduced frameworks for learning quantum processes by quantum statistical queries
[
Ang23
,
WD23
]
can be naturally analyzed in our formalism.
In particular, the
\mathtt{QPStat}
oracle from
[
WD23
]
corresponds to a linear evaluation oracle as in
Section

1.1.7
.
The
\mathtt{QPStat}
implements linear functions over the real vector space
\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}\otimes\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
spanned by pairs of states and observables, where the function can be written in terms of a channel
\mathcal{E}
as
f_{\mathcal{E}}(O\otimes\rho)=\left\langle\mathcal{E},O\otimes\rho\right\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}=\left\langle\left\langle\mathcal{E}\;\middle|\;O\otimes\rho\right\rangle\right\rangle=\tr[O\mathcal{E}(\rho)]
.

Part I
Formalism

2
General

subsection.2.1subsubsection.2.1.1subsection.2.2subsection.2.3subsection.2.4subsection.2.5

2.1
Set-Up

We adapt the framework of
[
FGR+17
]
to formalize general learning tasks.

Definition 2.1:
(General Learning Problem
\mathcal{Z}
)

Let
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
be sets. We refer to a map

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})

(2.1)

from the source set
\mathcal{S}
to the power set of the target set
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
as a
Learning Problem
.
For any source
s\in\mathcal{S}
we refer to
\mathcal{Z}(s)
as the solutions associated with
s
.
Moreover, for any
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
we denote by
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
the set of solution sets corresponding to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
under
\mathcal{Z}
and use the shorthand

\bigcup\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=\bigcup_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\mathcal{Z}(s)\,.

(2.2)

For any target object
t\in\mathcal{T}
we denote by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{t}=\quantity{s\in\mc S \midvert t\in\mc Z(s)}\subseteq\mathcal{S}\,,

(2.3)

the set of sources compatible with
t
, where
\mathcal{Z}_{t}=\varnothing
if
t
is no solution to any
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
By
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{T}}
we then denote the set of sets of source objects with equivalent solutions

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{T}}=\quantity{\mc Z_t \midvert t\in\mc T}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})\,,

(2.4)

and we say that the learning problem is
surjective
if it holds

\bigcup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{Z}(s)=\mathcal{T}\,.

(2.5)

For a subset
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{S}
we denote by
\mathcal{Z}|_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}
the
Restricted Problem
of
\mathcal{Z}
to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, which is defined as

\mathcal{Z}|_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})::s\mapsto\mathcal{Z}(s)\,.

(2.6)

Remark 2.1:
(On Surjectivity)

Note that the surjectivity condition in
Equation

2.5
is in general different from the requirement of surjectivity of the map
\mathcal{Z}
and regards the question, whether for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
there is an
s\in\mathcal{S}
such that
t
is contained in the solution set
\mathcal{Z}(s)
.
As such, for a surjective learning problem, every target is eventually a possible solution.
In contrast, for a non-surjective learning problem, some targets are never a valid solution for no source object
s
.
Moreover, note that any learning problem can be recast as a surjective learning problem by restricting to the relevant targets.

Let us now define the computational task of learning as defined by a general learning problem
\mathcal{Z}
.
We assume that the potential algorithm is given access to instances
s\in\mathcal{S}
by some oracle,
where we understand an oracle
\mathcal{O}
as an abstract operational model for this access.
This induces a particular oracle
\mathcal{O}(s)
to each
s\in\mathcal{S}
, which has to satisfy certain properties as defined by the model
\mathcal{O}
.

Problem 1:
(Learning
\mathcal{Z}
)

Given a general learning problem
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
as in
2.1
. The problem of
learning

\mathcal{Z}
from oracle access
\mathcal{O}
is defined as, given oracle access to
\mathcal{O}(s)
for an unknown
s\in\mathcal{S}
, to return a description of some
t\in\mathcal{Z}(s)
.

A natural example for a learning problem is Valiant’s theory of PAC learning
[
Val84
]
.

Problem 2:
(PAC Learning)

Let
n>0
,
\epsilon,\delta\in[0,1)
, let
\mathcal{H}
be a class of boolean functions
f:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
and let
\mathcal{D}
be a class of distributions over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n+1}
.
The following task is referred to as
Probably Approximately Correct
(PAC) learning of
\mathcal{H}
with respect to
\mathcal{D}
.
Given oracle access
\mathcal{O}
to an unknown
D\in\mathcal{D}
return some
h\in\mathcal{H}
, referred to as the
Hypothesis
, such that with probability at least
1-\delta
it holds

\Ex_{(x,y)\sim D}\quantity[y\neq h(x)]<\epsilon\,.

(2.7)

The original setting, as introduced by Valiant and which is referred to as the
Realizable
setting, is concerned with classes of distributions
\mathcal{D}(P,\mathcal{H})=\quantity{P^f \midvert f\in\mc H\,,\; P^f(x,y)}
, where

\displaystyle P^{f}(x,y)=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if}\;y\neq f(x)\\
P(x),&\text{if}\;y=f(x)\,.\end{cases}

(2.8)

We say a class
\mathcal{H}

can be efficiently PAC learned with respect to

\mathcal{D}
if there is an algorithm for PAC learning
\mathcal{H}
with respect to
\mathcal{D}
that runs in time
O(\mathrm{poly}(n,\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{\delta}}))
.
If
\mathcal{H}
can be PAC learned with respect to the class of all
n+1
bit distributions
\Delta(n+1)
, then we say that
\mathcal{H}

is PAC learnable
.
The case
\epsilon=0
is referred to as
exact
PAC learning
It is a simple exercise to recast PAC learning in terms of a learning problem
\mathcal{Z}
:

Example 2.1:
(PAC Learning as Learning Problem)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a class of boolean functions
f:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
and let
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\Delta(n+1)
.
PAC learning of
\mathcal{H}
with respect to
\mathcal{D}
then corresponds to the learning problem

\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{Z}(D)=\quantity{\mc Z(D)=\set{h\in\mc H\midvert\Ex_{(x,y)\sim D}\qty[y\neq h(x)]<\epsilon}\midvert D\in\mc D}\;\middle|\;D\in\mathcal{D}\,.

(2.9)

While
\epsilon
translates to the learning criterion defining the sets
\mathcal{Z}(D)
,

\delta
translates to the success probability
\alpha=1-\delta
defining the success statistics of random algorithms for learning
\mathcal{Z}
. Here, any learning algorithm in Valiant’s original model would be considered a random algorithm due to the inherent randomness of the random example oracle.

Let us also introduce the notions of dual learning problems, equivalence of learning problems and self-dual learning problems, which will turn out handy later on.

Definition 2.2:
(Duality and Equivalence)

Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a surjective learning problem.
We denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
the dual learning problem which is defined as

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})::t\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{t}\,.

(2.10)

Further, We say that two learning problems
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
and
\mathcal{Y}:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
are
Equivalent
, if there exists bijections
\Phi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
\Psi:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
such that for any
s\in\mathcal{S},s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and any
t\in\mathcal{T},t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
it holds

\displaystyle\Psi(\mathcal{Z}(s))=\mathcal{Y}(\Phi(s))

and

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathcal{Z}_{t})=\mathcal{Y}_{\Psi(t)}

(2.11)

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}(s^{\prime}))=\mathcal{Y}(\Phi^{-1}(s^{\prime}))

and

\displaystyle\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{t^{\prime}})=\mathcal{Y}_{\Psi^{-1}(t^{\prime})}\,.

(2.12)

A surjective learning problem that is equivalent to its own dual learning problem is said to be
Self-Dual
.

In this work we focus on learning problems where the elements of the source set
\mathcal{S}
admit the structure of functions and assume an oracle access that allows to query those functions up to some imprecision. This is formalized as follows.

Definition 2.3:
(Evaluation Oracle)

Let
(M,\mathrm{d})
be a metric space with metric
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
, let
\mathcal{X}
be a set and let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
. For any
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
\tau>0
we denote by
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(s)
the
evaluation oracle
of
s
with tolerance
\tau
. When queried with any
x\in\mathcal{X}
it returns some
v\in M
such that it holds
\mathrm{d}(v,s(x))\leq\tau
.
We refer to a query with respect to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(s)
as a
\tau
accurate evaluation query.

In most cases we will consider
M\subseteq\mathbb{C}
and consider the metric
\mathrm{d}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)=\absolutevalue{\cdotspace-\cdotspace}
.
However,
M
does not need to be a set of scalars and may be any set endowed with a metric.

2.1.1
Notions of Complexity

Here we briefly state the notions of complexity we work with in this work: the deterministic and random worst- and average-case complexity.

Definition 2.4:
(Worst Case Query Complexity)

The
deterministic worst-case query complexity
of the problem of learning
\mathcal{Z}
is the smallest
q
such that
there exists a deterministic algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that
solves
1
from at most
q
many queries to
\mathcal{O}(s)
.

The
random worst-case query complexity
of the problem of learning
\mathcal{Z}
with success probability
\alpha\in[0,1]
is the smallest
q
such that there
exists a random algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that solves
1

from
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}

with probability at least
\alpha
over
\mathcal{A}
’s internal randomness.

In this work we will deal with deterministic, classical probabilistic and quantum algorithms.
We refer to classical probabilistic algorithms in short by
probabilistic
algorithms.
By the term
random
algorithm we refor to both, quantum and probabilistic algorithms.

Definition 2.5:
(Average Case Query Complexities)

Let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathcal{S}
.
The
deterministic average-case query complexity
of the problem of learning
\mathcal{Z}
with success probability
\beta\in[0,1]
is the smallest
q
such that
there exists a deterministic algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that
solves
1
on at least a
\beta
fraction of instances from at most
q
many queries to
\mathcal{O}(s)
. In particular,
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}(s)}
has a deterministic output for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
, and it holds

\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\A^{\O(s)}\to\mc Z(s) \;\text{using at most $q$ queries}]\geq\beta\,.

(2.13)

The
joint average-case query complexity
of the problem of learning
\mathcal{Z}
with success probability
\beta\in[0,1]
is the smallest
q
such that there
exists a random algorithm
\mathcal{A}
such that it holds

\Pr_{s\sim\mu,\mathcal{A}}\quantity[\A^{\O(s)}\to\mc Z(s) \;\text{using at most $q$ queries}]\geq\beta\,,

(2.14)

where
\Pr_{\mathcal{A}}
denotes the probability with respect to the internal randomness of
\mathcal{A}
(and the oracle).

The
random average-case query complexity
of the problem of learning
\mathcal{Z}
with success probabilities
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
\beta\in[0,1]
is the smallest
q
such that there
exists a random algorithm
\mathcal{A}
such that it holds

\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\Pr_\A\qty[\A^{\O(s)}\to\mc Z(s) \;\text{using at most $q$ queries}]\geq\alpha]_{\mathcal{A}}\quantity[\A^{\O(s)}\to\mc Z(s) \;\text{using at most $q$ queries}]\geq\alpha\geq\beta\,.

(2.15)

In particular, there is a set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
with
\mu(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\geq\beta
such that, with probability at least
\alpha
over
\mathcal{A}
’s (and the oracle’s) internal randomness,
\mathcal{A}
solves
\mathcal{Z}
restricted to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
from
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}(s)
.

2.2
Deterministic Average-Case Lower Bound

Throughout this and the following
Sections

2.2
,
2.3
,
2.4
and
4
we assume
\tau>0
and
\mathcal{S},M,\mathrm{d}
and
\mathcal{X}
as in
2.3
.

Theorem 2.1:
(Deterministic Average Case Lower Bound. Adapted from Lemma C.2 in
[
Fel17
]
)

Suppose there is a deterministic algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
with probability
\beta
over the instance
s\sim\mu
from
q
many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries. Then, for any
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
it holds

\displaystyle q\geq\frac{\beta-\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_t]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(2.16)

Proof:
.

Denote by
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
a set of size
\mu(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=\beta
on which
\mathcal{A}
successfully learns
\mathcal{Z}
from
q
many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries.
We run
\mathcal{A}
and simulate every query response to
x\in\mathcal{X}
by
f(x)
. By assumption the algorithm makes
q
queries
x_{1},\dots,x_{q}
and, without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathcal{A}
returns some
t\in\mathcal{T}
.
Let
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{t}
.
Since
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
the algorithm will be correct when given access to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(s^{\prime})
. Because
s^{\prime}\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}
,
on the other hand,
t
is not a valid solution to
s^{\prime}
.
Hence, either at least one query must distinguish
s^{\prime}
from
f
in the sense that for some
i\in[q]
it holds

\mathrm{d}\quantity(s'(x_i), f(x_i))>\tau
, or we arrive at a contradiction, because all responses
f(x_{i})
are valid responses for
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(s^{\prime})
.
Hence, it holds

\begin{split}\beta-\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_t]&\leq\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\exists i : \met\qty(s(x_i), f(x_i))>\tau]\\
&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{q}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x_i), f(x_i))>\tau]\\
&\leq q\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[ \met\qty(s(x), f(x))>\tau]\,.\end{split}

(2.17)

Now assume that, after interacting with
f
the algorithm did not return a
t\in\mathcal{T}
. then again by
\mathcal{A}
’s determinism, for
any

s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
there must exist a distinguishing query
x_{i}
that distinguishes
s^{\prime}
from
f
. The claim then follows by taking the supremum over
t\in\mathcal{T}
to bound the mass of the unknown
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
.
∎

2.3
Joint Average-Case Lower Bound

Theorem 2.2:
(Joint Average Case Lower Bound for Probabilistic Algorithms. Adapted from Lemma C.2 in
[
Fel17
]
)

Suppose there is a classical probabilistic algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
with probability
\beta
over the joint measure over the instance
s\sim\mu
and
\mathcal{A}
’s internal randomnes, from
q
many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries. Then, for any
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
it holds

\displaystyle q\geq\frac{\beta-\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_t]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(2.18)

Proof:
.

We interpret any probabilistic algorithm
\mathcal{A}
as an ensemble
\quantity{\A_r}
of deterministic algorithms depending on the internal randomnes (as made explicit by
r\in R
for a suitable set
R
).
Now suppose a probabilistic algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that succeed with success rate at least
\beta
over the joint measure of its internal randomnes, abusing notation
r\sim\mathcal{A}
, and the instance
s\sim\mu
.
Further denote by
P^{+}\subseteq R\times\mathcal{S}
the subset such that for any
(r,s)\in P^{+}
the algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{r}
succeeds when given access to
s
, denote by
\mathcal{S}_{r}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
the set on which
\mathcal{A}_{r}
is succesfull and denote by
\mathcal{A}\times\mu
the joint measure over
R\times\mathcal{S}
.
Then,
\mathcal{A}\times\mu(P^{+})\geq\beta
implies that there exists some
r\in R
such that
\mu(\mathcal{S}_{r})\geq\beta
.

To arrive at the claim as in
Equation

2.18
recall that by

2.1
the average-case problem with success probability
\beta
over the instance requires
at least
q
many queries in case of deterministic algorithms.
∎

The general statement for random algorithms now follows from the following simple observation.

Observation 2.1:

Any quantum algorithm with evaluation query access translates to a probabilistic algorithm with evaluation query access, at the price of an at most exponential overhead in computational time and hence random resources.
The statement in
2.2
on the other hand bounds the number of queries any probabilistic algorithm must make, independent of its computational resources.
Thus, as the just mentioned translation does not increase the number of queries made the probabilistic bound also holds for quantum algorithms yielding the following corollary.

Corollary 2.3:
(Joint Average Case Lower Bound.)

Suppose there is a random algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
with probability
\beta
over the joint measure over the instance
s\sim\mu
and
\mathcal{A}
’s internal randomnes, from
q
many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries. Then, for any
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
it holds

\displaystyle q\geq\frac{\beta-\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_t]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(2.19)

2.4
Random Average-Case Lower Bound

In order to derive a general bound the random average-case complexity we continue to follow Feldmans work
[
Fel17
]
.
Later we introduce simplified bounds in
Section

4.2
for verifyable learning problems.
To begin with, a crucial notion is that of the probability of guessing the correct solution.
To this end introduce the following definition.

Definition 2.6:

Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a learning problem. For any distribution
\vartheta
over
\mathcal{T}
and
\gamma>0
denote by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\vartheta}(\gamma)=\quantity{s\in\mc S\midvert\vartheta\qty(\mc Z(s))\geq\gamma}

(2.20)

the set of sources whos solutions are at least
\gamma
probable under
\vartheta
.

Theorem 2.4:
(Random Average-Case Lower Bound)

Suppose there is a random algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
with probability
\beta
with respect to
s\sim\mu
and with probability
\alpha
with respect to
\mathcal{A}
’s internal randomness, from
q
many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries. Then, for any
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
and any
\gamma\in(0,\alpha)
it holds

q>\frac{\quantity(\alpha-\gamma)\cdot\beta-\sup_{\vartheta}\quantity(\beta- \sup_\vartheta\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[s\in\mc Z_{\vartheta}(\gamma)])_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_{\vartheta}(\gamma)]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[ \met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}\,,

(2.21)

where the supremum is over all measures
\vartheta
over
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof:
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a random algorithm that successfuly learns
\mathcal{Z}
with probability
\beta
with respect to
\mu
and probability
\alpha
with respect to its own internal randomnes.
Similar as in the proof of
2.1
we run
\mathcal{A}
and simulate every query
x\in\mathcal{X}
by
f(x)
. Denote by
x_{1},\dots,x_{q}
the queries made, which are now random variables with respect to
\mathcal{A}
’s randomnes.
Without loss of generality assume that after interaction
\mathcal{A}
returns some
t\in\mathcal{T}
, which is now a random variable and we denote by
\vartheta(f)
the corresponding distribution induced by
f
and
\mathcal{A}
over
\mathcal{T}
.
Denote by
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
the set on which
\mathcal{A}
is correct with probability at least
\alpha
over its internal randomnes.
For any
s\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
denote by

p(s)=\Pr_{\mathcal{A}}\quantity[\exists i\in[q] : \met\qty(s(x_i), f(x_i))>\tau]

(2.22)

the probability that one of the queries made by
\mathcal{A}
distinguishes between
s
and
f
.

Now, let
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\vartheta(f)}(\gamma)
.
By the correctness of the algorithm, it has to output some
t\in\mathcal{Z}(s^{\prime})
with probability at least
(1-p(s^{\prime}))-(1-\alpha)
,
where the first term corresponds to the probability that all query responses given are valid for
s^{\prime}

and the second term corresponds to the failure probability.
In contrast, the probability of
\mathcal{A}
returning a
t\in\mathcal{Z}(s^{\prime})
after interacting with
f
is bounded by
\vartheta(f)(\mathcal{Z}(s^{\prime}))<\gamma
due to
2.6
.
Thus,
p(s^{\prime})>\alpha-\gamma
.

This implies

\begin{split}\alpha-\gamma&<\Pr_{s\sim\mu,\mathcal{A}}\quantity[ \exists i\in[q] : \met\qty(s(x_i),f(x_i))>\tau\midvert s\in\mc S'\setminus\mc Z_{\vartheta(f)}(\gamma) ]\\[8.0pt]
&\leq\frac{\Pr_{s\sim\mu,\mathcal{A}}\quantity[\exists i\in[q] : \met\qty(s(x_i),f(x_i))>\tau]}{\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc S'\setminus\mc Z_{\vartheta(f)}(\gamma)]}\leq\frac{\Pr_{s\sim\mu,\mathcal{A}}\quantity[\exists i\in[q] : \met\qty(s(x_i),f(x_i))>\tau]}{\beta-\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_{\vartheta(f)}(\gamma)]}\\[8.0pt]
&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{\Pr_{s\sim\mu,\mathcal{A}}\quantity[ \met\qty(s(x_i),f(x_i))>\tau]}{\beta-\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_{\vartheta(f)}(\gamma)]}\leq q\cdot\frac{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[ \met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}{\beta-\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_{\vartheta(f)}(\gamma)]}\,.\end{split}

(2.23)

Reordering yields

q>\frac{\quantity(\alpha-\gamma)\cdot\beta-\quantity(\beta- \Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[s\in\mc Z_{\vartheta(f)}(\gamma)])_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_{\vartheta(f)}(\gamma)]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[ \met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(2.24)

The claim then follows from taking the supremum in order to bound the contribution by
\vartheta(f)
.
∎

2.5
Summary

The structure of
Equation

2.16
is typical for the bounds presented in this work, and it is easy to see, that the bound is tight in a particular sense.
Let us restate
Equation

2.16

\displaystyle q\geq\frac{\beta-\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_t]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}\,,

and start with investigating the numerator.
To begin with, the numerator is bounded by
\leq 1
and can therefore only weaken the bound on
q
.
Interestingly, the numerator is related to what we call the probability of trivial learning.
Let
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z,\mu)=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_t]
and denote by
t^{*}=\argmax_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_t]
.
Further, define
\mathcal{A}^{*}
to be the trivial algorithm which, when run makes not even a single query and always outputs
t^{*}
.
Then,
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z,\mu)
is the probability of success of
\mathcal{A}^{*}
with respect to the instances drawn according to
s\sim\mu
.
Consequently,
q=0
must hold for every
\beta\leq\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z,\mu)
, which implies that
Equation

2.16
is tight with respect to
\beta
.
We refer to
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z,\mu)
as the
Probability of Trivial Learning
.

The probability
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z,\mu)
and thus the numerator depend on
\mathcal{T}
.
The denominator in contrast is independent of
\mathcal{T}
and only depends on
\mathcal{S}
(and thus
\mathcal{X}
) and
\mu
.
This implies that the denominator is identical for every learning problem over
\mathcal{S}
.
With the previous paragraph in mind, we find that the denominator of
Equation

2.16
asymptotically lower bounds
every
sufficiently non-trivial learning problem over
\mathcal{S}
.
We thus call the denominator the
Query Complexity of Non-Trivial Learning
.
Let us collect this in the following definition.

Definition 2.7:
(Trivial and Non-Trivial Learning)

Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a learning problem over
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
and let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathcal{S}
. We then make the following definitions.

•

(Non-)Trivial Learning:
We denote by

\displaystyle\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z,\mu)=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\;\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z_t]

(2.25)

the
Probability of Trivial Learning

\mathcal{Z}
with respect to
\mu
.

•

Complexity of Learning:
For any
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
we denote by

\displaystyle\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu, f, \tau)=\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met(s(x),f(x))>\tau]\,,

(2.26)

the
Maximally Distinguishable Fraction
from
f
with respect to
\mu
.
The
Query Complexity of Non-Trivial Learning
with respect to
\mu
and tolerance
\tau
is then defined as

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu, \tau)=\max_{f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}}\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu, f, \tau)^{-1}\,.

(2.27)

Intuitively, we say that a learning problem is non-trivial with respect to a measure
\mu
over
\mathcal{S}
if for every trivial algorithm
\mathcal{A}
there is a realistic chance to observe an
s\sim\mu
which cannot be solved by
\mathcal{A}
.
This is formalized as follows.

Definition 2.8:
(Non-Trivial Learnign Problems)

With respect to a scaling parameter
n\in\mathbb{N}
we say that a family of learning problems
\{\mathcal{Z}_{n}\}
is
Non-Trivial
with respect to
\mu
if it holds
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z_n,\mu)=1-\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
as
n\to\infty
.

In many cases, one can directly estimate the concentration that defines
\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu, f, \tau)
.
