However, sometimes it is more practical to apply either Markov’s or Chebyshev’s inequality.
For the sake of completeness, we state the corresponding bounds as follows.

Corollary 2.5:
(Variance and Average Discrimination Bound for
q_{\mathtt{nt}}
)

The complexity of non-trivial learning is bounded from below by the
Average Discrimination
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu, f, \tau)\leq\tau^{-1}\cdot\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Ex_{s\sim\mu}[\mathrm{d}(s(x),f(x))]\,.

(2.28)

Moreover, if the variance is well-defined, then it holds

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu, \tau)\geq\tau^{2}\cdot\Var_{s\sim\mu}[s(x)]^{-1}\,.

(2.29)

Proof:
.

The first statement is an immediate consequence of Markov’s inequality. The second follows from Chebyshev’s inequality.
∎

While the average discrimination will in general be well-defined, the variance may be not defined in some metric spaces. We refer to

Appendix

C

for the more general definition of the metric variance covering those cases.

Remark 2.2:

We summarize this section in the following equation bounding the average-case query complexity for deterministic algorithms

\displaystyle q\geq(\beta-\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z, \mu))\cdot q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)\geq(\beta-\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z, \mu))\cdot\tau^{2}\cdot\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Var_{s\sim\mu}[s(x)]^{-1}\,,

(2.30)

with
\beta
the success probability over
\mu
.

3
Overview of Learning Problems

subsection.3.1subsection.3.2subsection.3.3

This section contains definitions and examples for particular types of learning problems, which we will analyze in more detail in this work.

3.1
Decision Problems

The following many-to-one decision problem is the paradigmatic learning problem to gain intuition about the query complexity of learning problems.

Example 3.1:
(Decission Problem
\mathtt{Dec}
)

Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
be a set,
s^{*}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. We define the
Decision Problem
of distinguishing between
\mathcal{S}
and
s^{*}
as

\text{$\mathtt{Dec}$}(\mathcal{S},s^{*}):\mathcal{S}\cup\quantity{s^*}\to\quantity{\set{\mc S}, \set{\set{s^*}}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\quantity{\mc S, \set{s^*}})::s\mapsto\begin{cases}\quantity{\mc S}&,\quad s\in\mathcal{S}\\
\quantity{\set{s^*}}&,\quad s\notin\mathcal{S}\,.\end{cases}

(3.1)

In words, given access to some unknown
s\in\mathcal{S}\cup\quantity{s^*}
, the task is to decide whether
s\in\mathcal{S}
or
s\not\in\mathcal{S}
.
If
s^{*}\in\mathcal{S}
we say the decision problem is
trivial
and else we say it is
non-trivial
.

Typical instances of
\mathtt{Dec}
would be for example uniformity testing.

Similarly to the many-to-one decision problem just introduced is the many-to-many decision problem which, abusing notation, will also be denoted by
\mathtt{Dec}
.

Example 3.2:
(Many-To-Many Decision Problem)

Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
be a set and for
i=0,1
let
\mathcal{S}_{i}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
And assume that
\mathcal{S}_{0}\cap\mathcal{S}_{1}=\varnothing
.
Define the
Many-To-Many Decision Problem

\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S}_{0},\mathcal{S}_{1})
as

\displaystyle\text{$\mathtt{Dec}$}(\mathcal{S}_{0},\mathcal{S}_{1}):\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{S}_{1}\to\quantity{\set{\mc S_0}, \set{\mc S_1}}\subset\mathcal{P}(\quantity{\mc S_0, \mc S_1})::s\mapsto\begin{cases}\quantity{\mc S_0}\,,&s\in\mathcal{S}_{0}\,,\\
\quantity{\mc S_1}\,,&s\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\,.\end{cases}

(3.2)

3.2
Verifiable Learning Problems

Verifiable learning problems are, in short, problems, for which there exists an efficient test to verify a solution.
This is formalized as follows.

Definition 3.1:
(Verifyable Learning Problem)

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathcal{S}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}

and let

f\in\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
.
We refer to a learning problem

\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})

as
deterministic

q
-
verifiable
with respect to
f
from oracle access
\mathcal{O}
, if there exists a family of deterministic
algorithms
\quantity{\A_t\midvert t\in\mc T}
, such that, for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
, the algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{t}
can solve
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{Z}_{t},f)
from
q
many queries to
\mathcal{O}
.

Similarly, we refer to the learning problem as
random

q
-
verifiable
with respect to
f
from oracle access
\mathcal{O}
with success probability
\alpha
, if there exists a family of random algorithms
\quantity{\A_t}
, such that for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
the algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{t}
solves
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{Z}_{t},f)
with success probability at least
\alpha
from at most
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}
.

Note, that this notion of verification is different from the notion of verification as introduced by Goldwasser et al.
[
GRS+21
]
.

3.1
is motivated by the necessities
for the reduction in
Section

4.2
.
However, note, that problems that Goldwasser et al. refer to as trivially verifiable will in general translate to
p
-verifiable problems with respect to any
f
.
According to their notion, a trivially verifiable problem comes along with a simple objective that assures that the actual source object
s
the verifier has access to is indeed contained in
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
.
For example, PAC learning in the realizable setting is trivially verifiable, since every concept
g
satisfying
\Ex_{x}[f(x)\neq g(x)]<\epsilon
is a valid solution to
f
.
Hence, a correlational query with
g
to
\mathtt{CSQ}_{\tau}[f]
will suffice to estimate whether
g\in\mathcal{Z}_{g}
.
In particular, realizable PAC learning is
1
-verifiable from correlational statistical queries if
\tau\leq\epsilon
against any
f
and for any concept class.

3.3
Linear Learning Problems

Intuitively, a linear learning problem is a learning problem over a linear function class, where a linear function class is a class of linear functions over a linear space
\mathcal{V}
.
In particular, a linear function class is a subset of the dual space of the linear space
\mathcal{V}
.
Here, we focus on linear spaces over
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 3.2:
(Linear Function Class and Linear Learning Problems)

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a linear space over
\mathbb{K}
with
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
equipped with the absolute value as metric, let
\norm{\cdotspace}
be a norm on
\mathcal{V}
and denote by
\overline{B}_{r}
the closed ball with radius
r>0
around zero in
\mathcal{V}
with respect to
\norm{\cdotspace}
.
Denote by
\mathcal{V}^{\star}
the dual space of
\mathcal{V}

and by
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}
the dual norm

\norm{f}_{\star}=\sup\quantity{\abs{f(x)}\midvert\norm{x}\leq 1}=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{V}}\frac{\absolutevalue{f(x)}}{\norm{x}}\,.

(3.3)

We identify a subset
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\star}
with the set

\mathcal{S}=\quantity{f\vert_{\overline{B}_1}\midvert f\in\mc S'}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}\,,

(3.4)

and refer to
\mathcal{S}
as a
Linear Function Class
.
We refer to a learning problem
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
over a linear function class
\mathcal{S}
as a
Linear Learning Problem
.

The equivalence of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}
follows immediately by linearity.
The restriction of functions in
\mathcal{S}
to the unit ball has a simple reason.
The evaluation oracle for a function comes with a tolerance parameter
\tau
.
Without some restriction of this kind one could easily boost the oracles’ accuracy by scaling the function argument and exploiting the linearity.

We now list some linear function classes together with the respective evaluation oracles.
We first consider the standard SQ oracle in terms of linear functions in
3.3
before doing the same for the QSQ oracle in
3.4
and for functionals and the CSQ oracle in
3.5
.
Then, we will consider the less known quantum correlational and quantum unitary statistical oracles in
3.6
and
3.7
before introducing a generalization of the CSQ oracle based on the Gelfand–Naimark–Segal inner product in
3.8
.

Example 3.3:
(Probability Distributions and Statistical Queries)

Let
\Delta(N)\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
be the
N
-dimensional simplex, or likewise the set of
N
-dimensional probability distributions.
A subset
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\Delta(N)
is referred to as a
distribution class
.
A distribution class
\mathcal{D}
is identified with a linear function class as follows:
Let
\mathcal{V}
be
\mathbb{R}^{N}
equipped with the infinity norm
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\ell_{\infty}}
.
The unit ball
\overline{B}_{1}
then is the set of vectors with entries bounded by
1
, or equivalently, the set of functions
\phi:[N]\to[-1,1]
.
Naturally
\mathcal{V}^{\star}=\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{N}
, the dual norm is the
\ell_{1}
-norm
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}=\norm{\cdotspace}_{1}

and

\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{V}^{\star}
can be identified with the linear function class
\mathcal{D}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
.
In particular, for
P,Q\in\mathcal{D}
it holds

\displaystyle\norm{P-Q}_{\star}=\norm{P-Q}_{1}=2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(P,Q)\,.

(3.5)

The evaluation oracle
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
with respect to
\mathcal{D}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
is identical to the
statistical query
(SQ) oracle
[
Kea93
,
FPV18
]

\mathtt{Stat}_{\tau}
for
\mathcal{D}
which, for every
P\in\mathcal{D}
when queried with some function
\overline{B}_{1}\ni\phi:[N]\to[-1,1]
returns a value
v\in\Ex_{i\sim P}[\phi(i)]+[-\tau,\tau]
.

Example 3.4:
(Quantum States and Quantum Statistical Queries)

Let
\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}\subset\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
be the real vector space of
N\times N
dimensional Hermitian matrices and denote by
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\tr}
the trace norm on
\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
defined as

\norm{M}_{\tr}=\tr\quantity[\sqrt{M^\dagger\cdot M}]\,.

(3.6)

Denote by

\mathcal{S}(N)=\quantity{\rho\midvert\rho\in\mb H^{N\times N}\;\text{and}\; \norm{\rho}_{\tr}=1}

(3.7)

the set of
N
-dimensional quantum states.
We refer to a set
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(N)
as a
class of quantum states
.
Similar to probability distributions we can associate
\mathcal{Q}
to a linear function class as follows:
Let
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
be equipped with the Schatten
\infty
-norm, or equivalently the
\ell_{2}
-induced operator norm
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\mathrm{op}}
.
The closed unit ball
\overline{B}_{1}\subset\mathcal{V}
corresponds to the set of Hermitian
N\times N
-dimensional operators with spectrum bounded by
1
.
It holds
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}^{\star}=\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
,
the dual norm is the trace norm
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}=\norm{\cdotspace}_{\tr}

and
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{V}^{\star}
can be identified with the linear function class

\mathcal{Q}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
.

The evaluation oracle
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
with respect to
\mathcal{D}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
is identical to the
quantum statistical query
(QSQ) oracle
[
AGY20
]

\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}
for
\mathcal{Q}
which, for every
\rho\in\mathcal{Q}
returns a value

v\in\tr\quantity[\rho O]+[-\tau,\tau]\,,

(3.8)

when queried with an operator
O\in\overline{B}_{1}
.

Example 3.5:
(Functionals and Correlational Statistical Queries)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda
be a probability measure on
[N]
, let
\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle_{\lambda}
be the
L^{2}(\lambda)
inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
given by

\displaystyle\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\lambda}=\Ex_{i\sim\lambda}[f(i)g(i)]\,,

and let

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}=\sqrt{\left\langle f,f\right\rangle_{\lambda}}=\sqrt{\Ex_{i\sim\lambda}\quantity[\abs{f(i)}^2]}

(3.9)

be the induced norm on
L^{2}(\lambda)
.
Then, let
\overline{B}_{r}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
be the closed ball with radius
r>0
with respect to the
L^{2}(\lambda)
-norm.
Since the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\lambda)
is self-dual it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}=L^{2}(\lambda)=\mathcal{V}^{\star}

and

\displaystyle\norm{\cdotspace}=\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}=\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}\,.

(3.10)

We refer to a set
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
as a
functional class

and identify
\mathcal{F}
with the linear function class
\mathcal{F}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
.
The evaluation oracle
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
with respect to
\mathcal{F}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
is identical to the
correlational statistical query
(CSQ) oracle
[
BFJ+94
,
BF01
]

\mathtt{CSQ}_{\tau}
with respect to
\mathcal{F}
and
\lambda
which, for every
f\in\mathcal{F}
returns a value

v\in\Ex_{i\sim\lambda}\quantity[g(i)f(i)]+[-\tau,\tau]\,,

(3.11)

when queried with a function
\overline{B}_{1}\ni g:[N]\to[-1,1]
.

Example 3.6:
(Quantum Correlational Statistical Queries)

A special case of
3.5
is due to
[
AK22a
]
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(N)
be a class of quantum states,
\mathcal{V}
be the real linear space of functions consisting of elements

\displaystyle f_{M}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbb{R}::\rho\mapsto f_{M}(\rho)=\tr\quantity[\rho M]

for

\displaystyle M\in\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}\,.

(3.12)

This is well-defined by the linearity of the trace and since the Hermitian operators are a real linear space.
In particular,
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
are isomorphic.
For a distribution
\lambda
over
\mathcal{Q}
we define the
L^{2}(\lambda)
inner product on
\mathcal{V}
as

\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\lambda}=\Ex_{\rho\sim\lambda}\quantity[f(\rho)g(\rho)]

(3.13)

and let
\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}
be the corresponding norm.
Again,
L^{2}(\lambda)
is a Hilbert space, is self-dual
and for any
M\in\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
it holds

\norm{\tr\qty[\cdotspace M]}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}\leq\norm{M}_{\infty}\,.

(3.14)

We identify any subset
\mathcal{F}\subset L^{2}(\lambda)
with the linear function class
\mathcal{F}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
.

The evaluation oracle is then by
3.5
equivalent to the correlational statistical query oracle which,
in the special case of this example, is equivalent to the
quantum correlational statistical query
(QCSQ) oracle
\mathtt{QCSQ}_{\tau}
as defined in
[
AK22a
]
.
The QCSQ oracle for a function
f_{M}
defined by the
Hermitian matrix
M
denoted by
\mathtt{QCSQ}_{\tau}(M)
returns a value

v\in\Ex_{\rho\sim\lambda}\quantity[\tr[\rho\cdot O]\tr[\rho\cdot M]]+[-\tau,\tau]\,,

(3.15)

when queried with another Hermitian matrix
O
with
\norm{O}_{\infty}\leq 1
.

Example 3.7:
(Quantum Unitary Statistical Query)

Another special case of CSQ’s due to
[
AK22a
]
is the following.
For a class of quantum states
\mathcal{Q}
and
\lambda
a measure over
\mathcal{Q}
we can define an inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
by

\left\langle M,K\right\rangle_{\lambda}=\Ex_{\rho\sim\lambda}\quantity[\tr[M^\dagger\cdot K \cdot\rho]]\,,

(3.16)

and denote by
\norm{\cdotspace}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}
the induced norm.
Again,
L^{2}(\lambda)
is a Hilbert space, is self-dual and it holds
\norm{M}_{L^{2}(\lambda)}\leq\norm{M}_{\mathrm{op}}
.
We can identify any subset
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
with the corresponding linear function class
\mathcal{F}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
containing elements

\displaystyle f_{M}:\overline{B}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}::O\mapsto f_{M}(O)=\Ex_{\rho\sim\lambda}\quantity[\tr[M^\dagger\cdot O\cdot\rho]]\,.

(3.17)

The corresponding evaluation oracle for
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathrm{U}(N)
when restricted to unitary queries
Q\in\mathrm{U}(N)\subset\overline{B}_{1}
corresponds to the
quantum unitary statistical query
oracle
\mathtt{QUSQ}_{\tau}(U)[Q]
from
[
AK22a
]
which returns a value

v\in\Ex_{\rho\sim\lambda}\quantity[\tr[U^\dagger\cdot Q\cdot\rho]]+\overline{B}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\tau}(0)\,,

(3.18)

when queried with unitary
Q

and
with
\overline{B}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\tau}(0)
is the complex closed
\tau
ball around 0.

Example 3.8:
(Matrix Functionals and Matrix Correlational Statistical Queries)

This example gives a generalization of
3.5
to the quantum setting.
We refer to matrix functionals and matrix correlational statistical queries in order to avoid confusion with
3.6
.
Let
N>0
,
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(N)
a quantum state and define the GNS-inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
with respect to
\rho
as

\displaystyle\left\langle A,B\right\rangle_{\rho}=\tr[A^{\dagger}\cdot\rho\cdot B]

with induced norm

\displaystyle\norm{A}_{L^{2}(\rho)}=\sqrt{\tr[A^{\dagger}\cdot\rho\cdot A]}\,.

(3.19)

Again,
L^{2}(\rho)
is a Hilbert space, self-dual, and it holds
\norm{M}_{L^{2}(\rho)}\leq\norm{M}_{\mathrm{op}}
.

We refer to a set
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
as a
matrix functional class
and identify it with the linear function class
\mathcal{F}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
containing the elements

\displaystyle f_{A}:\overline{B}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}::B\mapsto f_{A}(B)=\left\langle A,B\right\rangle_{\rho}\,.

(3.20)

We then denote evaluation oracle
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
with respect to
\mathcal{F}|_{\overline{B}_{1}}
as the
Matrix Correlational Statistical Query
(MCSQ) oracle
\mathtt{MCSQ}_{\tau}
for
\mathcal{F}
and
\rho
.
For every matrix
A\in\mathcal{F}
the oracle
\mathtt{MCSQ}_{\tau}(M)
returns a value
v
which is promised to be in the interval

v\in\left\langle M,O\right\rangle_{\rho}+[-\tau,\tau]\,,

(3.21)

when queried with a matrix
O\in\overline{B}_{1}
.

4
Simplified Lower Bounds

subsection.4.1subsection.4.2

4.1
Decision Problems

In this section we give worst-case bounds for the particular learning problem
\mathtt{Dec}
as in
3.1
.
Throughout this section we assume
\tau>0
and
\mathcal{S},M,\mathrm{d},\mathcal{X}
as in
2.3
.

Theorem 4.1:
(Deterministic Query Complexity of
\mathtt{Dec}
)

Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
and
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}\setminus\mathcal{S}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic algorithm that solves
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},f)
from
q
many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries.
Then for any measure
\mu
over
\mathcal{S}
it holds

q\geq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\quantity(\max_{x\in\mc X} \Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[\met\qty(s(x), f(x))>\tau] )_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x), f(x))>\tau]^{-1}\,.

(4.1)

Proof:
.

We run
\mathcal{A}
and answer every query
x
by
f(x)
. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{q}
be the queries made by
\mathcal{A}
.
For the sake of a contradiction assume the existence of some
s\in\mathcal{S}
such that there is no distinguishing query.
In particular, assume that for all
i\in[q]
it holds
\mathrm{d}\quantity(s(x_i),f(x_i)\leq\tau)
.
Thus, all responses
f(x_{i})
made would correspond to valid evaluation queries with tolerance
\tau
to
s
, so
\mathcal{A}
can not distinguish
s
from
f
contradicting the correctness of
\mathcal{A}
.
Thus, for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
there must exist at least one distinguishing query.
In particular, it must hold

\displaystyle 1=\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\exists i\in[q]:\; \met\qty(s(x_i),f(x(i)))>\tau]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{q}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[ \met\qty(s(x_i),f(x_i))>\tau]

(4.2)

\displaystyle\leq q\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[ \met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]\,,

(4.3)

which completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 4.2:
(Random Query Complexity of
\mathtt{Dec}
)

Let
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
and
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}\setminus\mathcal{S}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a random algorithm that solves
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},f)
from
q
many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries with success probability
\alpha
over
\mathcal{A}
’s internal randomness.
Then for any measure
\mu
over
\mathcal{S}
it holds

q\geq\frac{2\cdot\quantity(\alpha- \sfrac 12)}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x), f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(4.4)

Proof:
.

We run
\mathcal{A}
and answer any query
x
by
f(x)
.
Denote by
x_{1},\dots,x_{q}
the queries made, which are now random variables with respect to
\mathcal{A}
’s randomness.
Assume the output is
X\in\quantity{\mc S,\set{f}}
.
Let
s\in\mathcal{S}
and denote by

p(s)=\Pr_{\mathcal{A}}\quantity[\exists i\in[q]:\; \met\qty(s(x_i),f(x_i))>\tau]

(4.5)

the probability of the existence of a distinguishing query between
s
and
f
.
We can lower bound
p(s)
following
[
Fel17
]
.
By the correctness of
\mathcal{A}
the output
X
will be
\mathcal{S}
with probability at most
1-\alpha
.
Now assume
p(s)<2(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})
.
Thus, for some valid responses of
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(s)
the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
will output
\quantity{f}
with probability at least
1-p(s)-(1-\alpha)>1-\alpha
,
contradicting the correctness assumption on
\mathcal{A}
.
Hence,
p(s)\geq 2(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})
.

The remainder of the proof follows as in the proof of
4.1
with the left-hand side of
Equation

4.2
replaced by
2(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})\leq p(s)

\displaystyle 2\cdot\quantity(\alpha-\sfrac 12)=\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim\mu\\
\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\quantity[\exists i\in[q]:\; \met\qty(s(x_i),f(x(i)))>\tau]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{q}\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\sim\mu\\
\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\quantity[ \met\qty(s(x_i),f(x_i))>\tau]

(4.6)

\displaystyle\leq q\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[ \met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]\,.

(4.7)

∎

It is worth mentioning that, similar to the bounds in
[
Fel17
]
, the previous bound is tight in the following sense.

Theorem 4.3:
(Random Query Complexity Upper Bound)

Let
\alpha=1-\delta\in(0,1)
,
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
compact sets and let
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}\setminus\mathcal{S}
.
Then, there exists a random algorithm that decides
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},f)
from
q
many queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
and with success probability
\alpha
over its internal randomness, with

\displaystyle q\leq q_{\max}\coloneq\frac{\ln\quantity(\frac{1}{\delta})}{\min_{\mu}\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met(s(x), f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(4.8)

Proof:
.

Since
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{S}
are compact, the space of probability distributions
\nu
over
\mathcal{X}
, respectively
\mu
over
\mathcal{S}
is convex and compact with respect to the weak-* topology
[
Wal18
, Propositions 7.2.3 and 7.2.4]
.
Thus, for any decision problem
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},f)
we find

\displaystyle\min_{\mu}\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(f(x), s(x))>\tau]=\min_{\mu}\max_{\nu}\Pr_{s\sim\mu,x\sim\nu}\quantity[\met\qty(f(x), s(x))>\tau]

(4.9)

\displaystyle=\max_{\nu}\min_{\mu}\Pr_{s\sim\mu,x\sim\nu}\quantity[\met\qty(f(x), s(x))>\tau]

\displaystyle\,,

(4.10)

where in the third equality we have used von Neumann’s minimax theorem.

Let
\nu
be the measure over
\mathcal{X}
that maximizes
Equation

4.10
.
The probability that none of
q_{\max}
samples
x_{1},\dots,x_{q_{\max}}\sim\nu
solves the decision problem can then be bounded by
Equation

4.10
and the definition of
q_{\max}
as

\displaystyle\Pr_{x_{1},\dots,x_{q_{\max}}\sim\nu}\quantity[\abs{s(x_i)-f(x_i)}\leq\tau\midvert\forall i\in[q_{\max}]]\leq\quantity(1 - \frac{\ln(\sfrac{1}{\delta})}{q_{\max}})^{q_{\max}}\leq\delta\,,

(4.11)

for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
Thus,
q_{\max}
non-adaptive queries selected according to
\nu
suffice to solve
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},f)
, which completes the proof.
∎

4.2
Verifiable Learning Problems

The previous section yields lower bounds on the query complexity of the decision problem
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
from
Section

3.1
.
While those bounds are important in themselves, they also deal as a prominent tool for obtaining simpler lower bounds for more general learning problems,
namely verifiable learning problems as introduced in
Section

3.2
.
This goes via the well known learning-to-deciding reduction which is formalized as follows.

Lemma 4.4:
(Learning is as Hard as Deciding)

Let
p_{v}\geq 0
,
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
and let
f\in\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
. Further, let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})

be a deterministic
p_{v}
-verifiable learning problem with respect to
f
from oracle access
\mathcal{O}
and denote
by
p_{d}\geq 0
the deterministic query complexity of
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},f)
with respect to
\mathcal{O}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic algorithm for learning
\mathcal{Z}
from oracle access
\mathcal{O}
using
q
many queries. Then it must hold

q\geq p_{d}-p_{v}\,.

(4.12)

Similarly, let
\alpha_{v},\alpha_{l}\in(0,1]
and define
\alpha_{d}=\alpha_{l}\cdot\alpha_{v}
. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be random
p_{v}
-verifiable with respect to
f
from oracle access
\mathcal{O}
with success probability
\alpha_{v}
and let
p_{d}\geq 0
be the random query complexity of
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},f)
with success probability
\alpha_{d}
and with respect to
\mathcal{O}
.
Then again, for any random algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for learning
\mathcal{Z}
from oracle access
\mathcal{O}
with probability at least
\alpha_{l}
using
q
many queries it must hold
Equation

4.12
.

Proof:
.

We begin with the deterministic case.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deterministic algorithm for learning
\mathcal{Z}
and, for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
, let
\mathcal{B}_{t}
be the of deterministic algorithm that decides
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{Z}_{t},f)
from at most
p_{v}
queries to
\mathcal{O}
.
Assume we are given oracle access
\mathcal{O}(s)
to an unknown
s\in\mathcal{S}\cup\quantity{f}
.
We run
\mathcal{A}
on
\mathcal{O}(s)
and without loss of generality obtain a solution
t\in\mathcal{T}
(otherwise, by the correctnes of
\mathcal{A}
we already know
s=f
).
Finally, we run
\mathcal{B}_{t}
on
s
and receive either “
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
” or “
\quantity{f}
”. In the latter case return “
\quantity{f}
”, in the former return “
\mathcal{S}
”.
By the correctness of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}_{t}
we have thus constructed an algorithm for solving
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},f)
that makes at most
q+p_{v}
queries.
Thus, by assumption,
q+p_{v}\geq p_{d}
which implies the claim.

The proof of the random case uses the identical construction.
The only difference is in the analysis.
The constructed algorithm for deciding between
\mathcal{S}
and
f
is certainly correct when both,
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}_{t}
are correct.
Thus, we find it is correct with probability at least
\alpha_{d}\geq\alpha_{l}\cdot\alpha_{v}
.
∎

To connect to the discussion after
3.1
, we note that it is unclear whether a claim similar to
4.4
can be made for verifiable problems in the sense of Goldwasser et al.
[
GRS+21
]
, too.
In particular, the notion of verifiable learning by Goldwasser et al. leaves the case open that both, the verifier and the prover access the reference object
f
which is not contained in the class to be learned, the verifier in their setting always is given access to some
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

We now combine
4.1
and
4.4
to obtain the following bound.

Theorem 4.5:
(Deterministic Average-Case Lower Bound for Verifyable Learning Problems)

Let
p_{v}>0,\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
and let
f:\mathcal{X}\to M
with
f\not\in\mathcal{S}
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be random
p_{v}
-verifiable with respect to
f
from
\tau
accurate evaluation queries.
Further, let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathcal{S}
.
Then, any algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}
with probability at least
\beta
over
s\sim\mu
requires at least
q
-many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries, with

q+p_{v}\geq\frac{\beta}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x), f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(4.13)

Proof:
.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{+}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
be a subset on which
\mathcal{A}
is successful and for which it holds
\mu(\mathcal{S}^{+})=\beta
.
Let
\mu_{+}
be the measure
\mu
conditioned on
\mathcal{S}^{+}
, such that for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
it holds
\mu_{+}(s)=\mu(s\mid s\in\mathcal{S}^{+})
.
Then, due to the definition of conditional probability we find, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}

\Pr_{s\sim\mu_{+}}\quantity[\met(s(x),f(x))>\tau]=\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met(s(x),f(x))>\tau\midvert s\in\mc S^+]\leq\beta^{-1}\cdot\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met(s(x),f(x))>\tau]\,.

(4.14)

Next we observe that a
\beta
-average-case learner
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{Z}
implies a worst-case learner for
\mathcal{Z}^{+}=\mathcal{Z}|_{\mathcal{S}^{+}}
.
The complexity of learning
\mathcal{Z}^{+}
in turn can be bounded by
4.4
. The bound as in
Equation

4.13
then follows from applying
4.1
to
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S}^{+},f)
with respect to the measure
\mu_{+}
.
∎

Similarly we obtain a corresponding bound for the random average-case query complexity as follows.

Theorem 4.6:
(Random Average-Case Lower Bound for Verifyable Learning Problems)

Let
p_{v}>0,\alpha_{v}\in(0,1],\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
and let
f:\mathcal{X}\to M
with
f\not\in\mathcal{S}
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
, with probability
\alpha_{v}
, be random
p_{v}
-verifiable with respect to
f
from
\tau
accurate evaluation queries.
Further, let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathcal{S}
.
Then, any algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}
with probability at least
\beta
over
s\sim\mu
and success probability
\alpha
over the internal randomness of
\mathcal{A}
requires at least
q
-many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries, with

q+p_{v}\geq\frac{2\cdot\quantity(\alpha\cdot\alpha_v-\sfrac 12)\cdot\beta}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x), f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(4.15)

Proof:
.

The proof is identical to that of
4.5
applying the second instead of the first part of
4.4
and invoking
4.2
instead of
4.1
.
∎

3.1
and
4.5
and
4.6

place some constraints on the choice of
f
in order to find non-trivial lower bounds.
On the one hand,
s\sim\mu
must concentrate around
f
.
On the other hand for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
there must exist an algorithm to distinguish
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
from
f
with few queries.

Thus, the given bounds can not be directly used if
s\sim\mu
concentrates within
\mathcal{S}
, or when
f
, around which
s\sim\mu
concentrates, can not be distinguished query efficiently from a subset
\mathcal{S}^{f}\subset\mathcal{S}
.
In these cases it is practical to apply
4.5
and
4.6

to a restricted problem
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}|_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}
.
Lower bounds on learning
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
then imply the corresponding lower bounds on learning
\mathcal{Z}
.
Here,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{S}
is any suitable subset that ensures verifiability.
In particular:

•

For any
t\in\bigcup_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\mathcal{Z}(s)
it must hold that
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{Z}_{t},f)
can be solved using at most
p_{v}
queries.

We write
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{f}
with
\mathcal{S}^{f}
the subset of sources, which cannot be distinguished from
f
using
p_{v}
queries.
Replacing the set
\mathcal{S}^{+}
in the proofs of
4.5
and
4.6

by
\mathcal{S}^{+}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{f}
and letting
\mu_{+}
in the proof denote the measure
\mu
restricted on the corresponding subset then yields the following corollary.

Corollary 4.7:

Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a learning problem such that
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}|_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}
for some
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{S}
is, with probability
\alpha_{v}
random
p_{v}
-verifiable with respect to
f
and
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
.
Let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathcal{S}
.
Then, for any algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}
with probability at least
\beta
over
s\sim\mu
and
\alpha
over
\mathcal{A}
’s internal randomness requires at least
q
many queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
, with

\displaystyle q+p_{v}\geq 2\cdot\quantity(\alpha-\sfrac 12)\cdot\frac{\beta-\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc S^f]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met(s(x), f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(4.16)

The deterministic bound corresponds to setting
\alpha_{v}=\alpha=1
.

5
Special Case: Linear Function Classes

subsection.5.1subsection.5.2subsubsection.5.2.1subsubsection.5.2.2

5.1
Characterization of Linear Learning

In this section we will characterize the query complexity of linear learning problems in the evaluation-oracle model.
In contrast to our original definition, we will identify linear function classes with sets in
\mathcal{V}
rather than the dual space.
Our bounds will require that both
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{V}
and the closed dual unit ball
\overline{B}_{1}^{\star}(0)
are compact.
Since this can be simplified to requiring
\mathcal{V}
to be reflexive with respect to
\norm{\cdotspace}
, we are free to swap the roles of
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\star}
as long as we are consistent.

Before state upper bounds on the query complexity for learning linear function families, let us first introduce a corresponding dimension for learning with evaluation queries.
We will then prove, in analogy to Feldman’s work in
[
Fel17
]
that this dimension characterizes the query complexity of linear learning problems.

Definition 5.1:
(Random Dimension)

Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
and
r\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
.
We define the
Random Dimension
of a decision problem
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},r)
with tolerance
\tau
as

\mathtt{rd}(\mathcal{S},r,\tau)=\sup_{\mu}\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\quantity(\max_{x\in\mc X} \Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[\met(r(x),s(x))>\tau])_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met(r(x),s(x))>\tau]^{-1}\,,

(5.1)

where the
\sup_{\mu}
is over all probability measures over
\mathcal{S}
.

For a learning problem
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
with
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
we define the
Dimension of Learning
and the
Random Dimension of Learning
respectively as follows

\displaystyle\mathtt{dl}(\mathcal{Z},\tau)

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}}\inf_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\mathtt{rd}\quantity(\mc S\setminus\mc Z_t, f, \tau)

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathtt{rdl}(\mathcal{Z},\tau,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}}\inf_{\vartheta}\mathtt{rd}\quantity(\mc S\setminus\mc Z_\vartheta(\gamma), f, \tau)\,,

(5.3)

for any
\gamma>0
, where
\mathcal{Z}_{\vartheta}(\gamma)
is as defined in
2.6
and where the infimum is over all probability measures
\vartheta
over
\mathcal{S}
.

As an immediate consequence we can adapt

2.1
and
2.4

to obtain worst-case lower bound for learning problems in terms of the (random) dimension of learning.

Corollary 5.1:
(Deterministic Worst Case Lower Bound of Learning)

Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a learning problem with
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
and let
\alpha,\tau>0
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be any deterministic algorithm that learns
\mathcal{Z}
from
q
-many
\tau
-accurate evaluation queries.
Then it must hold

q\geq\mathtt{dl}(\mathcal{Z},\tau)\,.

(5.4)

Proof:
.

The proof is identical to that of
2.1
with
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}
and where the
left-hand side of
Equation

2.17
is replaced by
\Pr_{s\sim\mu}[s\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}]
.
∎

Corollary 5.2:
(Random Worst Case Lower Bound of Learning)

Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a learning problem with
\mathcal{S}\subseteq M^{\mathcal{X}}
and let
\alpha,\tau>0
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be any random algorithm that learns
\mathcal{Z}
with success probability at least
\alpha
over its internal randomness from
q
-many
\tau
-accurate evaluation queries.
Then, for any
\alpha>\gamma>0
it must hold

q\geq(\alpha-\gamma)\cdot\mathtt{rdl}(\mathcal{Z},\tau,\gamma)\,.

(5.5)

Proof:
.

The proof is identical to that of
2.4
with
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}
and where the conditional probability in
Equation

2.23
is kept throughout.
∎

The above lower bounds hold for any learning problem in the evaluation-oracle model.
Proofs for upper bounds, however, require additional structure.
To this end, in this work and regarding upper bounds we focus on linear learning problems.

Interestingly, one can use the tools from convex optimization, namely the mirror descent algorithm,
in order to prove query complexity upper bounds for linear learning problems.
Throughout this section we denote by
\mathcal{V}
a linear space over
\mathbb{R}

and denote by
\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle
the inner product in
\mathcal{V}
with the convention of anti-linearity in the first argument.
Let
\norm{\cdotspace}
be a norm on
\mathcal{V}
,
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}
its dual norm and
let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be some open convex set.
We refer to
\mathcal{W}
as the primal space.
We refer to
Appendix

B
for the technical definitions that we skip for now.

Definition 5.2:
(Mirror Descent)

The
Mirror Descent
(MD) algorithm with
\zeta
-regular mirror map
R
over primal space
\mathcal{W}
, constraint set
\mathcal{K}
, step size
\eta>0
, initial guess
f_{1}\in\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{W}
and linear advice is defined as follows.
In each update step
t
, the algorithm receives a linear function
\left\langle g_{t},\,\cdot\,\right\rangle
and updates according to

f_{t+1}=\Pi^{R}_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{W}}\quantity((\nabla R)^{-1}(\nabla R(f_t)-\eta g_t))\,.

(5.6)

Theorem 5.3:
(Analysis of Online Mirror Descent)

The mirror descent algorithm from
5.2
, when given access to a list of linear functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{T}
, will produce a list of vectors
f_{1},\dots,f_{T}
such that for any
f\in\mathcal{K}
it holds

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\quantity(\lrangle{g_t,f_t}-\lrangle{g_t,f})\leq\frac{D_{R}(f,f_{1})}{\eta}+\frac{\eta}{2\zeta}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\norm{g_t}_{\star}\,.

(5.7)

In particular, assuming
\norm{g_t}_{\star}=1
for all
t
, the average regret is upper bounded by

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\quantity(\lrangle{g_t,f_t}-\lrangle{g_t,f})\leq\frac{\eta}{\zeta}\,,

(5.8)

for all
T\geq\frac{2\zeta D_{R}(f,f_{1})}{\eta^{2}}
.

The proof can be found in e.g.
[
Har18
]
.
We are now able to prove the upper bounds.

Theorem 5.4:
(Deterministic Upper Bound for Linear Learning)

Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a linear learning problem with
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.
Let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be a primal space,
\mathcal{K}
a constraint set such that
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
and let
R
be an
\zeta
-regularizer on
\mathcal{W}
with Bregman divergence
D_{R}
.
Define
r=\min_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}D_{R}(s,v)
.
For every
\tau>0
there exists a deterministic algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}
from
q
-many
\nicefrac{{\tau}}{{3}}
-accurate evaluation queries, where

q\leq O\quantity(\frac{r\cdot\dl(\mc Z,\tau)}{\zeta\cdot\tau^2}\cdot\ln(\abs{\mc S}))\,.

(5.9)

Proof:
.

Assume we run the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
with access to some unknown
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{S}
.
Let
f_{1}\in\mathcal{S}
be a function that minimizes
r
.
At step
i
denote by
f_{i}
the current hypothesis of the algorithm.
Denote by
d=\mathtt{rd}(\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{t},f_{i},\tau)
.
By
5.1
there exists a
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that
d\leq\mathtt{dl}(\mathcal{Z},\tau)
.
Similar as in Lemma C.1 from
[
Fel17
]
for some

p\leq d\ln(\abs{\mc S\setminus\mc Z_t})
there exist functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{p}
with
\norm{g_j}_{\star}\leq 1
such that for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
there exists a
j\in[p]
with
\absolutevalue{\tilde f(g_j)- f_i(g_j)}>\tau
.
In particular, if no such
j
exists, then
f_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}
must hold.

The algorithm now proceeds as follows. At each step
i
let
t\in\mathcal{T}
be the target maximizing
\mathtt{rd}(\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{t},f_{i},\tau)
, let
g_{1},\dots,g_{p}
be a cover as above
and denote by
v_{1},\dots,v_{p}
the responses of
\mathtt{Eval}_{\frac{\tau}{3}}(\tilde{f})
to the queries
g_{j}
.
If there exists a
j
such that
\absolutevalue{\tilde f(g_j)- f_i(g_j)}>\frac{2\tau}{3}
define
h_{i}
as
h_{i}=g_{j}
if
f_{i}(g_{j})>v_{j}
and
h_{i}=-g_{j}
if
f_{i}(g_{j})\leq v_{j}
.
Then, make a mirror descent update as in
5.2
with the linear function
h_{i}
and step size
\eta=\frac{\tau\zeta}{3}
.
In contrast, if no such
j
exists, return
t
.

To establish the correctness of
\mathcal{A}
note that the algorithm returns some
t\in\mathcal{T}
only when it cannot find a distinguishing query
g_{j}
at a given step.
However, by the discussion in the first paragraph,
this only happens if
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}
, which proves the correctness.

Let us now analyze the complexity of
\mathcal{A}
.
In each step the algorithm makes
p\leq\mathtt{dl}(\mathcal{Z},\tau)\ln(\abs{\mc S})
queries.
In case of an update we know that
\absolutevalue{\tilde{f}(g_j)-f_i(g_j)}>\frac{2\tau}{3}-\frac{\tau}{3}=\frac{\tau}{3}
.
Thus, by the definition of
h_{i}
it holds
\tilde{f}(h_{i})-f_{i}(h_{i})=\left\langle h_{i},\tilde{f}\right\rangle-\left\langle h_{i},f_{i}\right\rangle>\frac{\tau}{3}
.
Finally, by
5.3
we know that there can be at most

T=\frac{18D_{R}(\tilde{f},f_{1})}{\zeta\tau^{2}}\leq\frac{18r}{\zeta\tau^{2}}
such steps completing the proof.
∎

In analogy, we can prove the same statement for random algorithms.

Theorem 5.5:
(Random Upper Bound for Linear Learning)

Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a linear learning problem with
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
compact and compact closed unit ball
\overline{B}_{1}^{\star}(0)\subset\mathcal{V}^{\star}
.
Let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be a primal space,
\mathcal{K}
a constraint set such that
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
and let
R
be an
\zeta
-regularizer on
\mathcal{W}
with Bregman divergence
D_{R}
.
Define
r=\min_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}D_{R}(s,v)
.
For every
\tau>0
and
0<\delta<\alpha\leq 1
there exists a random algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that, with probability
\alpha-\delta
learns
\mathcal{Z}
from
q
-many
\frac{\tau}{3}
-accurate evaluation queries, where

q=O\quantity(\frac{r\cdot\rdl(\mc Z,\tau,\alpha)}{\zeta\cdot\tau^2}\cdot\log(\frac{r}{\tau\cdot\delta}))\,.

(5.10)

Proof:
.

Set
T=\frac{18r}{\zeta\tau^{2}}
,
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{\delta}{T}
and
p=d\ln\quantity(\frac{1}{\delta'})
.

The requirements
\mathcal{S}
and
\overline{B}_{1}^{\star}(0)
compact ensure that the spaces of probability distributions
\mu
over
\mathcal{S}
and
\nu
over
\overline{B}_{1}(0)^{\star}
are convex and compact with respect to the weak-* topology
[
Wal18
, Propositions 7.2.3 and 7.2.4]
.
Thus, for any decision problem
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},r)
we find for the random dimension

\begin{split}\mathtt{rd}(\mathcal{S},r,\tau)&=\min_{\mu}\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\quantity(\min_\mu\max_{x\in\mc X} \Pr_{s\sim\mu}\qty[\met\qty(r(x), s(x))>\tau])_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(r(x), s(x))>\tau]^{-1}\\
&=\min_{\mu}\max_{\nu}\quantity(\min_\mu\max_{\nu} \Pr_{s\sim\mu, x\sim\nu}\qty[\met\qty(r(x), s(x))>\tau])_{s\sim\mu,x\sim\nu}\quantity[\met\qty(r(x), s(x))>\tau]^{-1}\\
&=\max_{\nu}\min_{\mu}\quantity( \max_{\nu} \min_\mu\Pr_{s\sim\mu, x\sim\nu}\qty[\met\qty(r(x), s(x))>\tau])_{s\sim\mu,x\sim\nu}\quantity[\met\qty(r(x), s(x))>\tau]^{-1}\\
&=\min_{\nu}\min_{\mu}\quantity( \min_\mu\Pr_{s\sim\mu, x\sim\nu}\qty[\met\qty(r(x), s(x))>\tau])_{s\sim\mu,x\sim\nu}\quantity[\met\qty(r(x), s(x))>\tau]^{-1}\,,\end{split}

(5.11)

where in the third equality we have used von Neumann’s Minimax theorem.
Now, set
d=\mathtt{rdl}(\mathcal{Z})
.

The algorithm is now almost exactly as that from the proof of
5.4
.
The adaptations are as follows.
Recall, that at any given step
i
the algorithm has a current hypothesis
f_{i}
.
At each step, instead of working with a deterministic target
t
that maximizes
\mathtt{rd}(\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{t},f_{i},\tau)
we work with a “random target”, i.e. any measure
\vartheta
over
\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathtt{rd}(\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\vartheta}(\alpha),f_{i},\tau)\leq d
.
Instead of the deterministic cover we also use a “random cover”, this is, any measure
\nu
over
\overline{B}_{1}^{\star}(0)
that satisfies, for all
f\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\vartheta}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\Pr_{g\sim\nu}\quantity[\abs{f(g)-f_i(g)}\geq\tau]\geq\frac{1}{d}\,.

(5.12)

The existence of which is guaranteed by
Equation

5.11
.

The remainder is now as in the proof of
5.4
.
Sample
p
many functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{p}
and obtain the values
v_{j}=\mathtt{Eval}_{\frac{\tau}{3}}[g_{j}]
. Then, find a distinguishing query
g_{j}
and depending on the sign of
f_{i}(g(j))-v_{j}
define
h_{i}
and apply a mirror descent update with
h_{i}
and step size
\frac{\tau\zeta}{3}
.
Else, if no distinguishing query exists return a random target
t\sim\vartheta
.

We start by bounding the complexity of the algorithm.
Again, by
5.3
we find that at most
T=\frac{18r}{\zeta\tau^{2}}
updates will be performed.
Thus, the query complexity is obtained from the query complexity of each individual round times
T
giving rise to
Equation

5.10
.

Let us now analyze the correctness of the algorithm.
During the last round of the algorithm, either
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\vartheta}(\alpha)
, or the random functions
g_{j}\sim\nu
did not admit a cover, such that the algorithm wrongly did not update
f_{i}
.
In the former case the algorithm will sample some valid solution
\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{f})\ni t\sim\vartheta
with probability at least
\alpha
.
Let us now bound the probability of the latter case.
In each round
i
the algorithm will not find a distinguishing query with probability

\displaystyle\Pr_{(g_{1},\dots,g_{p})\sim\nu}\quantity[\forall j\in[p]\,:\;\abs{\tilde f(g_j)-f_i(g_j)}<\tau]\leq\quantity(1-\frac 1d)^{-p}\leq e^{-\frac{p}{d}}=\delta^{\prime}\,.

(5.13)

Thus, the probability that the algorithm does not find a distinguishing query in one of the at most
T
consecutive rounds is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\exists i\in[T]:\; \forall j\in[p]:\; \abs{\tilde f(g_j) - f_i(g_j)}<\tau]\leq T\delta^{\prime}=\delta\,,

(5.14)

where we have used the union bound.
Thus, the probability of success is at least
\alpha-\delta
proving the claim.
∎

5.2
Further Implications

This section contains further results about linear learning problems.
Since linear learning problems can be seen as the prototype for many learning problems, we believe they may be of independent interest.

In contrast to the previous section, we return to our original identification of linear function classes with sets in the dual space.
Throughout, let
\mathcal{V}
be a vector space over the field
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
with norm
\norm{\cdotspace}
, dual norm
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}
and denote by
\mathcal{V}^{\star}
the dual space.
By
\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\norm{x-y}_{\star}
for any
x,y\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
we denote the distance induced by the dual norm.
As in
3.2
we identify any
x\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
with a linear function

\displaystyle x:\overline{B}_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{K}::v\mapsto x(v)\,,

(5.15)

where
\overline{B}_{1}(0)
denotes the closed ball around
0\in\mathcal{V}
with radius
1
with respect to
\norm{\cdotspace}
.
Similarly, we denote by
B_{\epsilon}^{\star}(x)
the open
\epsilon
-ball around
x\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
with respect to
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}
.
Moreover, we denote by
B_{\epsilon,\mathrm{d}}^{\star}(x)
the open
\epsilon
-ball around
x
with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{\star}
.
Thus,
B_{\epsilon}^{\star}(x)=B_{2\epsilon,\mathrm{d}}^{\star}(x)
.
For the sake of ease we assume
\mathcal{V}
(and thus
\mathcal{V}^{\star}
) to be finite dimensional.
The reason for this assumption is that it implies that
\overline{B}_{1}(0)
is compact and hence we can replace any
\sup_{w\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)}
by a
\max_{w\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)}
and thus there exists a (non-unique) maximizer
v\in\argmax_{w\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)}
.
We note that our results carry over to the infinite dimensional case at the price of a more technical presentation.

5.2.1
Learning Linear Functions as Verifiable Problem

A prototypical learning problem that often arises is the following.

Definition 5.3:
(
\epsilon
-Learning)

Let
0<\tau\leq\epsilon
and
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\star}
.
We denote by

\displaystyle\mathtt{Learn}\quantity(\mc S, \epsilon):\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{V}^{\star})::x\mapsto B_{\epsilon,\mathrm{d}}^{\star}(x)=\quantity{y\in\mc V^\star\midvert\met_\star(x,y)<\epsilon}

(5.16)

the problem of
\epsilon
-Learning
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{\star}
.

In analogy to Lemma 28
[
NIS+23
]
we now modify
\mathtt{Learn}(\mathcal{S},\epsilon)
to a deterministically 1-verifiable learning problem.

Lemma 5.6:

Let
0<\tau\leq\epsilon
and
z\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
.
Define
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}\setminus B_{2\epsilon+\tau,\mathrm{d}}^{\star}(z)
.
Then,
\mathtt{Learn}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\epsilon)
is deterministically
1
-verifiable with respect to
z
from
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
.

Proof:
.

By
3.1
we need to construct a family of algorithms
\quantity{\mc A_y\midvert y\in\mc V^\star}
such that for every
y
, algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{y}
solves

\displaystyle\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{Z}_{y},z)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{y}=\mathtt{Learn}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\epsilon)_{y}\,,

(5.17)

from a single query to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(\tilde{x})
to the unknown
\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{Z}_{y}\cup\quantity{z}
.

Without loss of generality, let
y
be such that
\mathcal{Z}_{y}\neq\varnothing
.
By definition, it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{y}=B_{\epsilon,\mathrm{d}}^{\star}(y)\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\,.

(5.18)

Now, let
x\in\mathcal{Z}_{y}
and denote by
v\in\mathcal{V}
a vector that maximizes the dual distance

\displaystyle v\in\argmax_{w\in B_{1}(0)}\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{x(w)-z(w)}\,.

(5.19)

In particular,
\absolutevalue{x(v)-z(v)}=2\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,z)\geq 4\epsilon+2\tau
.

Then, we define
\mathcal{A}_{y}
as follows: Query
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(\tilde{z})
with
v
and receive a value
r
.
If
\absolutevalue{r-z(v)}>\tau
return
\quantity{\mc Z_y}
.
Else, if
\absolutevalue{r-z(v)}<\tau
return
\quantity{\set{z}}
.

If
\tilde{x}=z
the algorithm will be correct by definition.
Now assume
\tilde{x}=x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{y}
.
Then, since
\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,x^{\prime})\leq 2\epsilon
we find

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{x'(v)-z(v)}\geq\absolutevalue{\smash{\underbrace{\abs{z(v)-x(v)}}_{=2\met_\star(x,z)}} - \vphantom{\abs{x}}\smash{\underbrace{\abs{x(v)-x'(v)}}_{\leq 2\met_\star(x,x')}}}\geq 2\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,z)-2\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,x^{\prime})>4\epsilon+2\tau-4\epsilon=2\tau\,,\vphantom{\absolutevalue{\underbrace{\abs{x}}_{=X}}}

(5.20)

where we have used
\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,z)\geq 2\epsilon+\tau>2\epsilon>\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,x^{\prime})
.
Thus,
\absolutevalue{r-z(v)}>\tau
,
which completes the proof.
∎

Thus, we obtain the corollary.

Corollary 5.7:
(Complexity of
\epsilon
-Learning)

Let
0<\tau\leq\epsilon
,
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\star}
and
\mu
some measure over
\mathcal{S}
.
Assume there exists an algorithm that learns
\mathtt{Learn}(\mathcal{S},\epsilon)
with probability
\beta
over
x\sim\mu
and probability
\alpha
over its internal randomness from
q
queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
.
Then, for every
z\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
, it holds

\displaystyle q+1\geq 2\cdot(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})\cdot\frac{\beta-\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(x,z)<2\epsilon+\tau]}{\max_{v\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)}\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{x(v)-z(v)}>\tau]}\,.

(5.21)

Similarly, for any closed subset
\mathcal{M}\subset\overline{B}_{1}(0)
one can define the pseudo distance on
\mathcal{V}^{\star}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{V}^{\star}\times\mathcal{V}^{\star}\to\mathbb{R}::(x,z)\mapsto\max_{v\in\mathcal{M}}\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{x(v)-z(v)}\,.

(5.22)

Note that
\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{M}}
is symmetric and satisfies the triangle inequality.
However, depending on
\mathcal{M}
it is not point separating.
We then define.

Definition 5.4:

Let
0<\tau\leq\epsilon
,
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\overline{B}_{1}(0)
and
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{V}^{\star}
.
Then, we denote by

\displaystyle\mathtt{Learn}\quantity(\mc S, \epsilon, \mc M):\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{V}^{\star})::x\mapsto B_{\epsilon,\mathcal{M}}^{\star}(x)\coloneq\quantity{y\in\mc V^\star\midvert\met_\mc M(x,y)<\epsilon}

(5.23)

the problem of
\mathcal{M}
-restricted
\epsilon
-Learning
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
\mathcal{M}
.

Redoing the arguments in
5.6
with
\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{M}}
instead of
\mathrm{d}_{\star}
then yields.

Corollary 5.8:
(Complexity of
\mathcal{M}
-Restrivted
\epsilon
-Learning)

Let
0<\tau\leq\epsilon
,
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\star}
,
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\overline{B}_{1}(0)
and
\mu
some measure over
\mathcal{S}
.
Assume there exists an algorithm that learns
\mathtt{Learn}(\mathcal{S},\epsilon,\mathcal{M})
with probability
\beta
over
x\sim\mu
and probability
\alpha
over its internal randomness from
q
queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
.
Then, for every
z\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
, it holds

\displaystyle q+1\geq 2\cdot(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})\cdot\frac{\beta-\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\mc M(x,z)<2\epsilon+\tau]}{\max_{v\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)}\Pr_{x\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{x(v)-z(v)}>\tau]}\,.

(5.24)

5.2.2
Far From Any Fixed Function

A simple yet important observation from
[
NIS+23
]
is their “far from any fixed distribution” observation.

Lemma 5.9:
(Lemma 39 from
[
NIS+23
]
)

Let
\epsilon,\tau>0
and let
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\Delta(\mathcal{X})
be a class of distributions over domain
\mathcal{X}
.
Let
\mu
be a probability measure over
\mathcal{D}
and let
Q\in\mathcal{D}
be an arbitrary distribution.
Then, for any
D\in\mathcal{D}
it holds

\displaystyle\Pr_{P\sim\mu}\quantity[\tv(P,D)<\epsilon]\leq\max\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu, Q, \tau),\quantity{\kfrac\qty(\mu, Q, \tau), \Pr_{P\sim\mu}\qty[\tv(P,Q)\leq 2\epsilon+\tfrac\tau 2]}_{P\sim\mu}\quantity[\tv(P,Q)\leq 2\epsilon+\tfrac\tau 2]\,.

(5.25)

Where we slightly adapted the
\tau
dependence.

This lemma is interesting for two reasons.
First, it characterizes the maximal weight of any
\epsilon
-ball in terms of the maximally distinguishable fraction against
Q
and the weight of the
2\epsilon+\tau
-ball around
Q
.

Secondly, we note that the Lemma can be seen as a connection between the general deterministic bound for
\epsilon
-learning linear function classes as by
2.1
and the lower bound via verifiable learning as in
5.7
.
In particular, while the former translates to a bound in terms of the largest
\epsilon
-ball, the latter works in terms of just a single
2\epsilon+\tau
-ball.
In the following we show that this is true in general for learning linear function classes.

Lemma 5.10:

Let
\epsilon,\tau>0
and let
z,x\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
be such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,z)\geq\epsilon+\frac{\tau}{2}\,.

(5.26)

Then, for any measure
\mu
over
\mathcal{V}^{\star}
it holds

\displaystyle\Pr_{y\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(x,y)<\epsilon]\leq\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu, z, \tau)=\sup_{v\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)}\Pr_{y\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{y(v)-z(v)}>\tau]\,.

(5.27)

Proof:
.

Recall that by the definition of the dual norm it holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,y)=\sup_{v\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)}\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{x(v)-y(v)}\,.

(5.28)

Thus, for every
y\in B_{\epsilon}^{\star}(x)
and any
v\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)
we find

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{y(v) - z(v)}\geq\absolutevalue{ \abs{y(v) - x(v)} - \abs{x(v)-z(v)} }\geq\absolutevalue{2\met_\star(x,y) - 2\met_\star(x,z)}>\absolutevalue{2\epsilon+\tau- 2\epsilon}=2\tau\,,

(5.29)

where we used
\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,z)\geq\epsilon+\frac{\tau}{2}>\epsilon>\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,y)
.
Thus, for any
v\in\overline{B}_{1}(0)
it holds

\displaystyle\Pr_{y\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(x,y)<\epsilon]\leq\Pr_{y\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{y(v)-z(v)}>\tau]\leq\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu, z, \tau)\,.

(5.30)

∎

Similar to
[
NIS+23
, Lemma 39]
we conclude with.

Corollary 5.11:

Let
\epsilon,\tau>0
and let
z\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
and let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathcal{V}^{\star}
.
Then for every
x\in\mathcal{V}^{\star}
it holds

\displaystyle\Pr_{y\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(x,y)<\epsilon]\leq\max\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu, z, \tau),\quantity{\kfrac\qty(\mu, z, \tau), \Pr_{y\sim\mu}\qty[\met_\star(y,z)<2\epsilon+\tfrac{\tau}{2}]}_{y\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(y,z)<2\epsilon+\tfrac{\tau}{2}]\,.

(5.31)

Proof:
.

If
\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,z)<\epsilon+\frac{\tau}{2}
, then
\Pr_{y\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(x,y)<\epsilon]\leq\Pr_{y\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(y,z)<2\epsilon+\frac{\tau}{2}]
.
Otherwise, if
\mathrm{d}_{\star}(x,z)\geq\epsilon+\frac{\tau}{2}
it holds by
5.10
that
\Pr_{y\sim\mu}\quantity[\met_\star(x,y)<\epsilon]\leq\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mu, z, \tau)
.
∎

Part II
Applications

6
Preliminaries

subsection.6.1subsection.6.2subsection.6.3

We denote by
\Delta(N)\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
the set of
N
-dimensional probability distributions and similarly by
\Delta(\mathcal{X})
the set of probability distributions over the set
\mathcal{X}
4
4

4

Throughout this work we assume to work over “nice” subsets of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, such that without loss of generality, we assume that the event space, or
\sigma
-algebra, corresponds to the induced Lebesgue measurable sets. Similarly, by
X\subseteq\mathcal{X}
we implicitly assume that
X
is also contained in the respective
\sigma
-algebra.
.

For two probability distributions
P,Q\in\Delta(\mathcal{X})
we write
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(P,Q)=\max_{X\subseteq\mathcal{X}}\absolutevalue{P(X)-Q(X)}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\absolutevalue{P(x)-Q(x)}
, where abusing notation we write
P(x)=P(\{x\})
in the general case and
P(X)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{X}}P_{i}
for discrete probability distributions.
We denote by
\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}\subset\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
the set of
N
-dimensional Hermitian operators and by
\mathcal{S}(N)\subset\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
the set of
N
-dimensional quantum states
0\leq\rho=\rho^{\dagger}
,
\tr[\rho]=1
.
Further, for any
M\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
we denote by

\displaystyle\norm{M}_{\tr}=\tr[\sqrt{M^{\dagger}M}]

the trace norm and by

\displaystyle\norm{M}_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{x}\frac{\norm{Mx}_{2}}{\norm{x}_{2}}

(6.1)

the operator norm,
with
\norm{x}_{2}
the
\ell_{2}
norm of the vector
x\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
We use the standard braket notation for pure state vectors
\rho=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
with
\ket{\psi}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
\bra{\psi}
its dual.
It holds
\sqrt{\innerproduct{\psi}{\psi}}=\norm{\ket\psi}_{2}
as and denote by
\innerproduct{\phi}{\psi}
the inner product between two state vectors.
If not mentioned differently, we assume
\ket{\psi}
to be normalized
\norm{\ket\psi}_{2}=1
.

As already introduced before, we denote by
\Delta(N)\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
the simplex of
N
dimensional probability distributions.
By
\norm{x}_{1}=\sum_{i}\absolutevalue{x_i}
we denote the
1
-norm and define the total variation distance between two probability distributions
P,Q\in\Delta(N)
via
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}\norm{P-Q}_{1}
.
Similarly, we define the trace distance between quantum states by
\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(\rho,\sigma)=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\rho-\sigma}_{\tr}
.
The Fidelity between quantum states is defined as

\displaystyle F(\rho,\sigma)=\quantity(\tr[\sqrt{\sqrt{\sigma}\rho\sqrt{\sigma}}])^{2}\,.

(6.2)

By the Fuchs-van de Graaf inequality it holds

\displaystyle 1-\sqrt{F(\rho,\sigma)}\leq\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(\rho,\sigma)\leq\sqrt{1-F(\rho,\sigma)}\,.

(6.3)

In particular, for
\rho=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
pure it holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\norm{\rho-\sigma}_{\tr}=\sqrt{1-F(\rho,\sigma)}=\sqrt{1-\!\matrixelement{\psi}{\sigma}{\psi}}\,.

(6.4)

We denote by
\mathds{1}_{N}
the
N
dimensional identity matrix and, if
N
is clear from context simply write
N
. Similarly, it will be made clear if the index has a different meaning.

6.1
Random Quantum Circuits

Most of our applications will consider quantum circuits and ensembles of random quantum circuits. Those are defined as follows.
Important objects we are interested in then are the output states of quantum circuits and the corresponding Born distributions.

Definition 6.1:
(Brickwork Quantum Circuits)

For a gate set
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathrm{U}(4)
and
n,d\in\mathbb{N}
denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\mathrm{U}\quantity(2^n)
the set of
Brickwork Quantum Circuits
on
n
qubits of depth
d
.

\mathcal{C}_{\,\mathrm{bw}}
consists of all unitaries that can be decomposed as

U=U^{(d)}\cdots U^{(2)}\cdot U^{(1)}=\quantity(\mds 1_2\otimes U_{23}^{(d)}\otimes U_{45}^{(d)}\otimes\cdots)\cdots\quantity(\mds 1_2\otimes U_{23}^{(2)}\otimes U_{45}^{(2)}\otimes\cdots)\cdot\quantity(U_{12}^{(1)}\otimes U_{34}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots)\,,

(6.5)

where each
U_{ij}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{G}
acts on qubits
i
and
j
only and where for simplicity we have assumed
d
even.

Similarly, we denote by
\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}(\mathcal{G})
the class of states

\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}=\quantity{\rho= \rho(U)\midvert\rho(U) = U\ketbra{0} U^\dagger\,,\; U\in\Cbrqc[,n,d](\mc G)}\,,

(6.6)

which we call
Brickwork Quantum States
.

For any quantum state
\rho
we define its
Born Distribution
as the probability distribution
P_{\rho}(x)=\!\matrixelement{x}{\rho}{x}
. Denote by

\mathcal{D}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}=\quantity{P_\rho\in\Delta\qty(2^n)\midvert\rho\in\Qbrqc}\,,

(6.7)

the set of
Brickwork Quantum Circuit Born Distributions
.
Similarly, for a unitary (quantum circuit)
U
we refer to
P_{U}\coloneq P_{\rho(U)}
the
Born Distribution
or
Output Distribution
of the unitary (quantum circuit)
U
.

Random quantum circuits are then defined via the following process.

Definition 6.2:
(Brickwork Random Quantum Circuits)

Let
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}
be the probability distribution over
\mathrm{U}\quantity(2^n)
that corresponds to drawing a unitary
U\in\mathcal{C}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}
by drawing each
U_{ij}^{(l)}
i.i.d. from the Haar measure over
\mathrm{U}(4)
.
We refer to the distribution
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}
as
Brickwork Random Quantum Circuits
on
n
qubits of depth
d
.

6.2
Unitary Designs and the Haar Measure

One of the key features behind that makes random quantum circuits interesting is that they approximate the Haar measure on the unitary group
\mathrm{U}(N)
in a specific sense.

Definition 6.3:
(Unitary
t
-Designs)

For any measure
\mu
over
\mathrm{U}(N)
we define the
t
’th Moment Operator
as

\displaystyle\Phi^{(t)}_{\mu}:\mathbb{C}^{N^{t}\times N^{t}}\to\mathbb{C}^{N^{t}\times N^{t}}::A\mapsto\Ex_{U\sim\mu}\quantity[U^{\otimes t}\cdot A\cdot(U^\dagger)^{\otimes t}]\,.

(6.8)

We denote by
U\sim\mathrm{U}(N)
the
Haar measure
(or similarly, the uniform measure) over
\mathrm{U}(N)
.

We say that a measure
\mu
over
\mathrm{U}(N)
forms a
Unitary
t
-Design
if it holds

\displaystyle\Phi^{(t)}_{\mathrm{U}(N)}=\Phi^{(t)}_{\mu}\,.

(6.9)

By
U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t}}{{\sim}}\mathrm{U}(N)
we denote the process of sampling from a unitary
t
design.

Note that any
t
-design forms a
t-1
-design since

\displaystyle\Ex_{U\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr_{t}\qty[U^{\otimes t}(A\otimes\tfrac{1}{N} \id_N)(U^\dagger)^{\otimes t}]]=\Ex_{U\sim\mu}\quantity[U^{\otimes t-1}A(U^\dagger)^{\otimes t-1}]\,,

(6.10)

where
\tr_{t}[\dots]
denotes the partial trace on the
t
’th copy and
N^{-1}\mathds{1}_{N}
denotes the maximally mixed state in
N
dimensions.

Lemma 6.1:
(Second Moments
[
KR21
]
)

Let
A\in\mathbb{C}^{N^{2}\times N^{2}}
be an operator with
N\geq 2
, then

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathrm{U}(N)}^{(2)}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\tr[AP_{\vee}]}{N(N+1)}P_{\vee}+\frac{2\tr[AP_{\wedge}]}{N(N-1)}P_{\wedge}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(N-1)(N+1)}\quantity(\tr[A]\id+ \tr[A\mb F]\mb F)+\frac{2}{(N-1)N(N+1)}\quantity(\tr[A]\mb F + \tr[A\mb F]\id)\,,

where
P_{\vee}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathds{1}+\mathbb{F})
and
P_{\wedge}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathds{1}-\mathbb{F})
.

The typical examples for designs are the Pauli group, which forms a
1
-design, and the Clifford group.

Lemma 6.2:
(The Clifford Group Forms a
3
-Design
[
Zhu17
,
ZKG+16
]
)

The set
\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)
of
n
-qubit Clifford unitaries forms a unitary
3
-design
\Phi_{\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}^{(3)}=\Phi_{\mathrm{U}(2^{n})}^{(3)}
. However, The Clifford group fails ”gracefully” to be a unitary
4
-design
\Phi_{\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}^{(4)}\neq\Phi_{\mathrm{U}(2^{n})}^{(4)}
.

Approximations of
t
-designs exist for various norms.
The most common definition is with respect to the diamond norm
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\diamond}

[
Haf22
]
.
For a measure
\mu
over
\mathrm{U}(N)
it requires

\displaystyle\norm{\Phi^{(t)}_{\U(N)} - \Phi^{(t)}_{\mu}}_{\diamond}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{N^{t}}\,,

(6.11)

to hold for
\mu
to form an
\epsilon
-approximate unitary
t
-design.
The dimensional factor is connected to the definition of a
relative

\epsilon
-approximate unitary
t
-design, which requires
[
BHH16
]

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\quantity(\Phi_{\U(N)}^{(t)}\otimes\id^{\otimes t})\quantity(\Omega_N^{\otimes t})\leq\quantity(\Phi_{\mu}^{(t)}\otimes\id^{\otimes t})\quantity(\Omega_N^{\otimes t})\leq(1+\epsilon)\quantity(\Phi_{\U(N)}^{(t)}\otimes\id^{\otimes t})\quantity(\Omega_N^{\otimes t})

(6.12)

to hold, with
\ket{\Omega_N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ket{ii}
the maximally entangled state.
In particular, while
Equation

6.11
refers to an additive error between the maps
\Phi_{\mathrm{U}(N)}^{(t)}
and
\Phi_{\mu}^{(t)}
,
Equation

6.12
enforces a small a relative error.

Another definition is that of an
\epsilon
-approximate
Tensor Product Expander
(TPE).
This is,
\mu
forms an
\epsilon
-approximate TPE if it holds
[
BHH16
]

\displaystyle g(\mu,t)\coloneq\norm{\Phi^{(t)}_{\U(N)} - \Phi^{(t)}_{\mu}}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\epsilon\,.

(6.13)

Since the operator norm and the diamond norm are only related by a dimensional factor
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\diamond}\leq N\norm{\cdotspace}_{\mathrm{op}}
(in dimension
N
) the TPE condition is much weaker.
Yet, the TPE condition can be easily boosted as
g(\mu^{*r},t)\leq g(\mu,t)^{r}
.
Here
\mu^{*r}
denotes the measure that samples
U_{1},\dots,U_{r}\sim\mu
identically and independently and then returns
U=U_{r}\cdots U_{2}\cdot U_{1}
.
Thus, most techniques for proving approximate unitary designs go via first proving a statement on
g(\mu,t)
and then boosting to the required accuracy.

In this work we are mainly interested in approximate designs with a small additive error.
We thus define.

Definition 6.4:
(Additive
\epsilon
-Approximate Unitary
t
-Design)

A measure
\mu
over
\mathrm{U}(N)
is said to form an
Additive
\epsilon
-Approximate Unitary
t
-Design
if it holds

\displaystyle\norm{\Phi^{(t)}_{\U(N)} - \Phi^{(t)}_{\mu}}_{\diamond}\leq\epsilon\,,

(6.14)

We write
U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon,t}}{{\sim}}\mathrm{U}(N)
if we sample from an additive
\epsilon
-approximate unitary
t
-design.

Importantly, as shown by Brandão, Harrow and Horodecki and improved upon by Haferkamp, brickwork random quantum circuits form approximate designs
[
BHH16
,
Haf22
]
.

Lemma 6.3:
(Random Quantum Circuits Form a
t
-Design
[
Haf22
]
)

Let
n\geq\left\lceil 2\log(4t)+1.5\sqrt{\log(4t)}\right\rceil
, then
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}
forms an additive
\epsilon
-approximate unitary
t
-design if

\displaystyle d\geq C\ln(t)^{5}t^{4+3\frac{1}{\log(t)}}\quantity(nt+\log\qty(\epsilon^{-1}))\,,

(6.15)

where
C
is a constant that can be taken to be
C=2^{44}
.

Moreover, for small
t
those bounds were improved by Haferkamp and Hunter-Jones.

Lemma 6.4:
(RQC Design Depth for Small
t

[
HH21
]
)

Let
t\in\quantity{2,4}
and
n
as before.
Then
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}
forms an additive
\epsilon
approximate
t
-design if

\displaystyle d\geq 6.4\quantity(n+\log(n)+\log\qty(\epsilon^{-1}))

for

\displaystyle t=2

(6.16)

and

\displaystyle d\geq 38\quantity(4n+\log\qty(\epsilon^{-1}))

for

\displaystyle t=4\,.

(6.17)

Note that we adapted the prefactor of the
O(n)
term by
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
compared to the values in
[
HH21
, Table I]
.
This is because we state the values for additive approximate designs, whereas the numbers in their Table I are for approximate designs.

6.3
Spherical and Projective Designs

The main use case of unitary designs in this work is to define complex spherical and complex projective designs.
Those correspond to measures over the complex unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{N-1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
We identify the set of
N
dimensional pure state vectors with the complex unit sphere in
N
-dimensions
\ket{\psi}\in\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
and use the standard braket notation for dual vectors
\bra{\psi}
.
We call a finite subset
T\subset\mathbb{N}^{2}
a
Lower Set
if for any
(k,l)\in T
it holds that every
(m,n)\in T
with
m\leq k
,
m\leq l
.

Definition 6.5:
(Complex Spherical
T
-Designs
[
RS11
]
)

For any
(s,t)\in\mathbb{N}
and any measure
\mu
over
\mathbb{S}^{N-1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{N}
denote by

\displaystyle K_{\mu}^{(s,t)}=\Ex_{\psi\sim\mu}\quantity[\ket\psi^{\otimes s}\bra\psi^{\otimes t}]\in\mathbb{C}^{N^{s}\times N^{t}}\,,

(6.18)

the
(s,t)
’th moment operator.
We use the shorthand
K_{\mu}^{(t)}=K_{\mu}^{(t,t)}
.
We denote by
\psi\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
the
Haar Measure
(or similarly the uniform measure) over
\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
.
For a lower set
T\subset\mathbb{N}^{2}
we say that
\mu
forms a
Complex Spherical
T
-Design
if for all
(s,t)\in T
it holds

\displaystyle K_{\mu}^{(s,t)}=K_{\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}^{(s,t)}\,.

(6.19)

Similarly, we say that
\mu
forms a
Complex Projective
t
-Design
if for every
s\leq t
it holds

\displaystyle K_{\mu}^{(s)}=K_{\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}^{(s)}\,.

(6.20)

By
\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(t,t)}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
(
\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
) we denote the process of sampling from a complex spherical
(t,t)
-design (projective
t
-design).
We use the shorthand
(s,t)
-design for a
T=\quantity{(k,l)\midvert k\leq s, l\leq l}\subset\mathbb{N}^{2}
-design.

The definition of projective and spherical designs is very similar.
The practical difference, why in some cases it is handy to require the stronger notion of a spherical
t
-design, is that in principle nothing is known about the “off-diagonal” moments of a projective design.
To see this first note that for a
T
-design it holds

\displaystyle K_{\mu}^{(k,l)}=0

for all

\displaystyle(k,l)\in T

with

\displaystyle k\neq l\,,

(6.21)

see for example Lemma 3.3 in
[
RS11
]
.
Now, as in
[
RS11
, Example 3.5]
,
let
L
be the standard basis in
\mathbb{C}^{N}
.

L
forms a projective
1
-design, but

K_{L}^{(0,1)}=\Ex_{\psi\sim L}[\ket{\psi}]=\frac{1}{N}(1,\dots,1)\neq 0
.

Explicit formula for projective designs can be found in e.g.
[
KR21
]
.

Lemma 6.5:
(Projective Moment Operators: Lemma 34 from
[
KR21
]
)

It holds

\displaystyle K_{\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}^{(t)}=\binom{N+t-1}{t}^{-1}P_{\vee^{t}}\,,

(6.22)

with
P_{\vee^{t}}
the projector on the symmetric subspace with respect to the permutation group
S_{t}
on the
t
copies of Hilbert space.
In particular, for
t=1,2
this implies

\displaystyle K_{\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}^{(1)}=\frac{\mathds{1}}{N}

and

\displaystyle K_{\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}^{(2)}=\frac{\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}+\mathbb{F}}{N(N+1)}

(6.23)

with
\mathbb{F}
the operator that flips the two copies of Hilbert space.

Similar to the unitary case we now define approximate designs.
In going from channels to states, the diamond norm naturally translates to the trace norm.

Definition 6.6:
(Additive
\epsilon
-Approximate Spherical
T
-Design)

Let
T\subset\mathbb{N}^{2}
be a lower set. A measure
\mu
over
\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
forms an
Additive
\epsilon
-Approximate Complex Spherical
T
-Design
if for all
(s,t)\in T
it holds

\displaystyle\norm{K_{\mb S^{N-1}}^{(s,t)}-K_{\mu}^{(s,t)}}_{\tr}\leq\epsilon\,.

(6.24)

Again, we use the shorthand
(t,t)
-design as in
6.5
and define
Additive
\epsilon
-Approximate Complex Projective
t
-Designs
accordingly.
We write
\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon,(t,t)}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
(
\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon,t}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
) if we sample from an additive
\epsilon
-approximate complex spherical
(t,t)
-design (projective
t
-design).

The main reason, why we are interested in additive approximate designs is the following lemma.

Lemma 6.6:

Let
\mu,\nu
be measures over
\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
and let
O\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\norm{K_{\nu}^{(t)}-K_{\mu}^{(t)}}_{\tr}\leq\epsilon\,,

for
t=1,2
implies

\displaystyle\Var_{\psi\sim\mu}\quantity[\mel{\psi}{O}{\psi}]\leq\Var_{\psi\sim\nu}\quantity[\mel{\psi}{O}{\psi}]+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}\,.

(6.25)

Proof:
.

The claim follows from direct calculation

\displaystyle\Var_{\psi\sim\mu}\quantity[\mel{\psi}{O}{\psi}]=\Ex_{\psi\sim\mu}\quantity[(O\otimes O)K_\mu^{(2)}]-\Ex_{\psi\sim\mu}\quantity[OK_\mu^{(1)}]^{2}\leq\quantity(\Ex_{\psi\sim\nu}\qty[(O\otimes O)K_\nu^{(2)}]+\epsilon)_{\psi\sim\nu}\quantity[(O\otimes O)K_\nu^{(2)}]+\epsilon-\quantity(\Ex_{\psi\sim\nu}\qty[OK_\nu^{(1)}] - \epsilon)_{\psi\sim\nu}\quantity[OK_\nu^{(1)}]-\epsilon^{2}

(6.26)

\displaystyle=\Var_{\psi\sim\nu}\quantity[\mel{\psi}{O}{\psi}]+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}-2\epsilon\Ex_{\psi\sim\nu}\quantity[OK_\nu^{(1)}]\leq\Var_{\psi\sim\nu}\quantity[\mel{\psi}{O}{\psi}]+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}\,.

(6.27)

∎

A direct consequence of
6.3
is the following corollary.

Corollary 6.7:
(Random Quantum Circuits Form a Projective
t
-Design)

Let
n\geq\left\lceil 2\log(4t)+1.5\sqrt{\log(4t)}\right\rceil
,
N=2^{n}
and denote by
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}^{\star}
the measure on
\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
induced by
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}
which corresponds to first sampling
U\sim\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}
and then outputting
U\ket{0}
.
Then, for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
it holds that
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}^{\star}
forms an additive
\epsilon
-approximate complex projective
t
-design if

\displaystyle d\geq C\ln(t)^{5}t^{4+3\frac{1}{\log(t)}}\quantity(nt + \log\qty(\epsilon^{-1}))\,.

(6.28)

Moreover,
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}^{\star}
forms an additive
\epsilon
-approximate
t
-design if

\displaystyle d\geq 6.4\quantity(n+\log(n)+\log\qty(\epsilon^{-1}))

for

\displaystyle t=2

(6.29)

and

\displaystyle d\geq 38\quantity(4n+\log\qty(\epsilon^{-1}))

for

\displaystyle t=4\,.

(6.30)

7
Quantum Statistical Query Learning

subsection.7.1subsubsection.7.1.1subsubsection.7.1.2subsubsection.7.1.3subsubsection.7.1.4subsection.7.2subsubsection.7.2.1subsubsection.7.2.2subsection.7.3subsubsection.7.3.1subsubsection.7.3.2subsection.7.4subsubsection.7.4.1subsubsection.7.4.2

The quantum statistical query model has been studied in a number of works
[
AGY20
,
AK22
,
CHI+23
]
.
Recall the definition of the quantum statistical query oracle as introduced in
3.4
.

Definition 7.1:
(Quantum Statistical Query Oracle
\mathtt{QStat}
)

Let
N>0
,
\tau>0
and let
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(N)\subset\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
with
0<\rho<\mathds{1}
be an
N
dimensional quantum state.
We denote by
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\rho)
the
Quantum Statistical Query
(QSQ) oracle of
\rho
with tolerance
\tau
.
When queried with an observable
O\in\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
with
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
it returns a value
v
, which is promised to lie in the interval

v\in\quantity[\tr[\rho\cdot O]-\tau, \tr[\rho\cdot O]+\tau]\,.

(7.1)

As mentioned previously, the
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}
oracle implements the
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
oracle with respect to the absolute value
\mathrm{d}(v,w)=\absolutevalue{v-w}
in the following way:
We identify a quantum states
\rho
with the corresponding functions over bounded operators
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
, which maps
O\mapsto\tr[\rho O]
.
Thus, the
\mathtt{QStat}
query complexity of non-trivial learning for any problem
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
over a set of quantum states
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{S}(N)
and measure
\mu
over
\mathcal{Q}
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu, \tau)\geq\tau^{2}\cdot\min_{O}\Var_{\rho\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[\rho O]]^{-1}\,.

(7.2)

7.1
Positive Results

We begin by collecting a few positive results about QSQ based learning algorithms.

7.1.1
State Testing

It is easy to see that for any pure state
\rho=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
one can test from a single query to
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}
whether an unknown pure state
\sigma
equals
\rho
or is at least
\epsilon
far, for
\tau<\epsilon^{2}
.
To this end, denote by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{pure}}(N)=\quantity{\rho\in\mc S(N)\midvert\tr[\rho^2]=1}
the set of
N
dimensional pure states.

Theorem 7.1:
(
\epsilon
-Testing Pure States)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<\epsilon\leq 1
and
0\leq\tau<\nicefrac{{\epsilon^{2}}}{{2}}
,
\rho=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\in\mathcal{S}(N)
and denote by
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{pure}}(N)\setminus B_{\epsilon}(\rho)
, where
B_{\epsilon}
is the open
\epsilon
-ball with respect to the trace distance. Then
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{Q},\rho)
can be solved from a single query to
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}
.

Proof:
.

Let
\sigma=\outerproduct{\varphi}{\varphi}\in\mathcal{Q}\cup\quantity{\rho}
. Then, querying
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\sigma)
with
\rho
returns some
v\in\absolutevalue{\braket{\varphi}{\psi}}^{2}+[-\tau,\tau]
.
For the trace distance of pure states it holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(\rho,\sigma)=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\rho-\sigma}_{\tr}=\sqrt{1-\absolutevalue{\braket{\varphi}{\psi}}^{2}}\,.

(7.3)

Thus, by assumption
\absolutevalue{\braket{\varphi}{\psi}}^{2}
is either
1
or at most
1-\epsilon^{2}
.
Therefore, for any
\tau<\nicefrac{{\epsilon^{2}}}{{2}}
the response
v
can distinguish between
\absolutevalue{\braket{\varphi}{\psi}}^{2}=1
and
\absolutevalue{\braket{\varphi}{\psi}}^{2}\leq 1-\epsilon^{2}
.
∎

7.1.2
PAC Learning

Let us now consider PAC learning
as in
2

in terms of the QSQ oracle.

Definition 7.2:
(Quantum PAC and QSQ)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
f:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
be a boolean function and let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
.
We refer to the
n+1
-qubit state
\rho_{f}=\outerproduct{\psi_f}{\psi_f}
with

\ket{\psi_f}=\sum_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\sqrt{\mathcal{D}(x)}\ket{x, f(x)}

(7.4)

as the
Quantum Probably Approximately Correct
(qPAC) state with respect to
f
and
\mathcal{D}
.
A
qPAC Oracle
then is any oracle that provides copies of the qPAC state
\rho_{f}
.
Similarly, the
Noisy qPAC Oracle
with noise rate
\eta\in[0,1]
is defined similarly with respect to
\rho_{f}^{\eta}=\outerproduct{\psi_{f,b}}{\psi_{f,b}}
, where

\displaystyle\rho_{f}^{\eta}=\Ex_{b}\quantity[\ketbra{\psi_{f,b}}]

with

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{f,b}}=\sum_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\sqrt{\mathcal{D}(x)}\ket{x, f(x)\oplus b_x}\,,

(7.5)

with
b
a length
2^{n}
bit string where each
b_{i}
is sampled i.i.d. according to a biased coin flip
b_{i}\sim\quantity(1-\eta,\;\eta)
5
5

5

Each
2^{n}
-bit string
b
encodes a specific noise pattern. The state
\rho_{f}^{\eta}
then is the mixture over all noise configurations.

.
For a class
\mathcal{C}
of boolean functions denote by
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
the set of qPAC states and by
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\eta}
the set of noisy qPAC states.
The oracle that provides copies of
\rho_{f}
is referred to as qPAC oracle and similarly in case of noisy qPAC.
We say we are given QSQ access to the (noisy) qPAC oracle if we are given access to a QSQ oracle
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\rho_{f})
(respectively
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\rho_{f}^{\eta})
).

The statistical query model was introduced by Kearns
[
Kea93
]
in order to model learning algorithms in the presence of noise.
Thus, one of the core contributions was a result showing that SQ based PAC learners imply PAC learners, which are robust to random classification noise.
In
[
AGY20
]
the corresponding result is shown for QSQ based learners.

Theorem 7.2:
(QSQ, PAC and noisy PAC, Theorem 3.4 from
[
AGY20
]
)

Let
\tau,\delta\in(0,1)
and let
\eta<\max\quantity{\sfrac 12, 2\tau^2}
. Further, let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of boolean functions and
suppose there exists an algorithm for
\epsilon
-learning
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
from
q
many queries to
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}
.
Then it holds:

1.

There exists a quantum algorithm for
(\epsilon,\delta)
-PAC learning
\mathcal{C}
from
O(\tau^{-2}q\log(\sfrac{q}{\delta}))
many copies of the qPAC state.

2.

There exists a quantum algorithm for
(\epsilon,\delta)
-PAC learning
\mathcal{C}
from
O((\tau-\sqrt{\eta})^{-2}q\log(\sfrac{q}{\delta}))
many copies of the noisy qPAC state with noise rate
\eta
.

Moreover, Arunachalam, Grilo and Yuen have shown in
[
AGY20
]
that, in contrast to learning from statistical queries, parities can be learned from quantum statistical queries.

Theorem 7.3:
(Learning Parities, Lemma 4.2 in
[
AGY20
]
)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathcal{C}
be the class of parity functions

\mathcal{C}=\quantity{\chi_s:\mb F_2^n\to\mb F_2\doublecolon x\mapsto x\cdot s\midvert s\in\mb F_2^n}\,.

(7.6)

Then for any
\tau<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
the class
\mathcal{C}
can be learned exactly from
O(n)
QSQ queries to the qPAC oracle with respect to
\mathcal{D}
the uniform distribution.

Similarly, in
[
AGY20
]
it is shown that the class of
k
-juntas can be
\epsilon
-learned efficiently from
O(n+2^{O(k)})
