many quantum statistical queries of tolerance
\tau=O(\epsilon 2^{-\nicefrac{{k}}{{2}}})
and that
the class of
\mathrm{poly}(n)
sized DNF’s can be learned from
O(\mathrm{poly}(n))
many
\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}}
-accurate quantum statistical queries (Lemmas 4.3 and 4.5).

Combining
7.2
and
7.3
yields then the following corollary.

Corollary 7.4:
(Solving LPN From QSQ’s
[
AGY20
]
)

Let
\tau>0
. The learning parities with noise problem can be solved efficiently from
\tau
-accurate QSQ access to the noisy qPAC state for any noise rate
\eta<\max\quantity{1/2, 2\tau^2}
.

7.1.3
Matrix Product States

Matrix product states are a prominent class of states.

Definition 7.3:

Let
n,D\in\mathbb{N}
. An
n
-qubit pure state
\rho=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
is referred to as
Matrix Product State
(MPS) with
Bond Dimension

D
if it can be decomposed as

\ket{\psi}=\ket{\mrm{MPS}(A_1,\dots,A_n)}=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}A_{1}^{(x_{1})}\cdot A_{2}^{(x_{2})}\cdots A_{n-1}^{(x_{n-1})}\cdot A_{n}^{(x_{n})}\ket{x}\,,

(7.7)

where the
A_{l}^{(i)}
are complex
D_{l,1}\times D_{l,2}
matrices with
D_{1,1}=D_{n,2}=1
and all other
D_{l,1}=D_{l,2}=D
.

It is easy to see, that the MPS tomography schemes as introduced in
[
CPF+11
]
can in fact be implemented by quantum statistical queries.
Thus, it holds.

Theorem 7.5:
(Learning MPS)

Let
n,D\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathcal{Q}
be the set of matrix product states on
n
-qubits of bond dimension
D
endowed with the trace distance.
Then,
\mathcal{Q}
can be efficiently
\epsilon
-learned from
O(nD^{2})
many
O(\nicefrac{{\epsilon}}{{D^{2}}})
-accurate quantum statistical queries.

Proof:
.

Note that the sequential scheme of unitary transformations in
[
CPF+11
]
only requires the
\epsilon
-approximate knowledge of
\kappa
-local reduced density matrices of unitary transformations of
\rho
,
with
\kappa=\lceil\log(D)\rceil+1
.
The claim then follows from the fact that any
\kappa
-qubit state can be measured to accuracy
\epsilon
from
2^{2\kappa}
many
(\epsilon 2^{-2\kappa})
-accurate statistical queries.
∎

7.1.4
Free Fermions and Free Bosons

Another example of quantum systems that can be learned in the quantum statistical query framework are free fermions
[
Bra04
,
GKE+18
,
SW19
,
AG23
,
Nie23
]
.
In short, due to Wick’s theorem this class of states can be learned efficiently from knowledge about all
O(n^{2})
many two-point functions, which can be evaluated from quantum statistical queries.

Definition 7.4:
(Fermionic Gaussian Operators and Pure States)

For
l\in\mathbb{N}
denote for
i\in[l]
by
f_{i}^{\dagger}
and
f_{i}
the fermionic creation and annihilation operators which satisfy the anti-commutation relations

\displaystyle\quantity{f_i,f_j^\dagger}=\delta_{ij}\,,

and

\displaystyle\quantity{f_i,f_j}=\quantity{f_i^\dagger,f_j^\dagger}=0\,.

(7.8)

We denote by
n_{i}=f^{\dagger}_{i}f_{i}
the number operator of the
i
’th mode and by
n=\sum_{i}n_{i}
the total number operator.
The self adjoint Majorana operators are then defined via

\displaystyle m_{2i-1}=f_{i}+f_{i}^{\dagger}

and

\displaystyle m_{2i}=-i(f_{i}-f_{i}^{\dagger})\,,

and they satisfy

\displaystyle\quantity{m_i,m_j}=2\delta{ij}\,.

(7.9)

A Hermitian operator
H\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
with
N=2^{l}
is called Gaussian (or free) if there exists a matrix
\mathbf{A}=-\mathbf{A}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{2l\times 2l}
such that it holds

\displaystyle H=\frac{i}{4}\mathbf{m}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{m}\,,

with

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{2l})\,.

(7.10)

Similarly, we call a unitary
U\in\mathrm{U}(N)
Gaussian if it can be written in terms of a Gaussian Hamiltonian as
U(t)=e^{-itH}
.
A pure Gaussian state
\rho=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
can then be defined by the state vector

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}=U\ket{\mbf{n}}\,,

with

\displaystyle\ket{\mbf{n}}=\ket{(\mbf{n}_1,\dots,\mbf{n}_{n})}=(f^{\dagger}_{n})^{\mathbf{n}_{n}}\cdots(f^{\dagger}_{1})^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\ket{0^n}\,,

(7.11)

where
U
is a Gaussian unitary and
n_{i}\ket{\mbf{n}}=\mathbf{n}_{i}\ket{\mbf{n}}
.

Then we find.

Theorem 7.6:
(Learning Pure Gaussian States)

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
N=2^{l}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and denote by
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{S}(N)
the class of pure Gaussian states.
Then, for every
\tau<\frac{\epsilon}{2l^{2}}
, the class
\mathcal{G}
can be learned to accuracy
\epsilon
in fidelity from
l(l-1)
many queries to
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}
.

We introduce the covariance matrix before proving
7.6
and restate a basic lemma about Gaussian unitaries.

Definition 7.5:
(Covariance Matrix)

Let
\rho
be an arbitrary fermionic state on
l
modes and let
m_{i}
denote the Majorana operators.
The covariance matrix (CM) of
\rho
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(\rho)_{ij}=\frac{i}{2}\tr[\rho\quantity[m_i,m_j]]\,.

(7.12)

It holds that
\mathbf{M}(\rho)
is real and anti-symmetric.

Lemma 7.7:
(From
[
GKE+18
]
)

Let
U(t)
be a Gaussian unitary generated by
H
which is defined by
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{2l\times 2l}
and let
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{2l})
.
Then it holds

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}(t)=\quantity(U^\dagger(t)m_1 U(t),\dots,U^\dagger(t)m_{2l}U(t))=\mathbf{Q}(t)\cdot\mathbf{m}\,,

with

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(t)=e^{t\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{SO}(2l)\,.

(7.13)

Note, that
7.7
implies that a pure Gaussian state
U\ket{\mbf{n}}
in the basis, which diagonalizes
U
, has a block diagonal CM

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda_{i}\\
-\lambda_{i}&0\end{pmatrix}\eqcolon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}B(\lambda_{i})

(7.14)

with
\lambda_{i}=\pm 1
for all
i
.
To see this we note
\nicefrac{{i}}{{2}}\tr[U\outerproduct{\mbf{n}}{\mbf{n}}U^{\dagger}[m_{i},m_{j}]]=\nicefrac{{i}}{{2}}\tr[\outerproduct{\mbf{n}}{\mbf{n}}[U^{\dagger}m_{i}U,U^{\dagger}m_{j}U]]
.

Proof of

7.6
:

We follow the standard approach of learning the CM which is what is also performed in fermionic quantum optics, see for example
[
GKE+18
]
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{G}
be the unknown state and let
\mathbf{M}
be the corresponding CM and let
\Gamma_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}[m_{i},m_{j}]
.
Querying
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\rho)
with
i\Gamma_{ij}
for
0\leq j<i\leq l
we obtain values
v_{ij}
and thus an estimate
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}=-\mathbf{M}^{\prime T}
of
\mathbf{M}
with

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{ab}=\begin{cases}v_{ij}\,,&j<i\,,\\
v_{ji}\,,&i<j\,.\end{cases}

(7.15)

Note that the
\tau
-guarantee translates to a bound in
\ell_{\infty}
norm which in turn implies an
\ell_{2}
-norm bound
\norm{A}_{2}\leq k\norm{A}_{\infty}
for
A
a
k\times k
dimensional matrix.

Since
\rho
is a pure Gaussian state,
\mathbf{M}
has eigenvalues in
\pm 1
as argued after
7.7
.
Therefore, we modify
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
as follows.
Let
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
be the matrix that block diagonalizes
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}=\mathbf{Q}^{\prime T}\cdot\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
with
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{i}B(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})
.
Since
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
has dimension
2l
and is
\tau
-close in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm to
\mathbf{M}
it follows that
each
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
is at least
2l\tau
-close to
\pm 1
.
Thus, setting
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}=\mathbf{Q}^{\prime T}\widehat{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
with
\widehat{\mathbf{D}}
being
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
with the
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
set to
\pm 1
results in a valid pure Gaussian state CM.

We now use Equation (8) from Gluza et al.
[
GKE+18
]
to estimate the fidelity between our estimate and the true state

\displaystyle F\quantity(\rho,\widehat{\rho})\geq 1-\frac{1}{4}\tr[\quantity(\mbf{M}-\widehat{\mbf{M}})^{T}\cdot\mathbf{M}]\geq 1-\frac{1}{4}\norm{\mbf{M}-\widehat{\mbf{M}}}_{2}\norm{\mbf{M}}_{2}\geq 1-\frac{2l}{4}\norm{\mbf{M}-\widehat{\mbf{M}}}_{2}\,,

(7.16)

in terms of the
\ell_{2}
-norm (which for matrices is the Frobenius norm) between the true and the observed CM.

By construction,

\displaystyle\norm{\mbf{M}-\widehat{\mbf{M}}}_{2}\leq\norm{\mbf{M}'-\widehat{\mbf{M}}}_{2}+\norm{\mbf{M}-\mbf{M}'}_{2}^{\prime}\leq 2l\tau+2l\tau=4l\tau\,.

(7.17)

This implies
F(\rho,\widehat{\rho})\geq 1-2l^{2}\tau
or equivalently
F(\rho,\widehat{\rho})\geq 1-\epsilon
for
\epsilon>2l^{2}\tau
, which proves the claim.
∎

7.2
Single Copy QSQ’s

As a consequence of our formalism, we will now show the extension of the results from
[
NIS+23
]
from the statistical query to the quantum statistical query setting.
In particular, we derive bounds on learning brickwork random quantum circuit Born distributions in
Section

7.2.1
.
Then, we give the corresponding results for Clifford unitaries in
Section

7.2.2
.

7.2.1
Random Quantum Circuits: Part I

To begin, recall the task of
\epsilon
-learning of distributions as analyzed in
[
NIS+23
]
which is an instance of
5.3
.

Definition 7.6:
(
\epsilon
-Leraning of Distributions)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon>0
,
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\Delta(N)
be a class of distributions and denote by
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}
the total variation distance.
We define
\epsilon
-
Learning
of distributions with respect to the total variation distance as the learning problem

\mathtt{Learn}(\mathcal{D},\epsilon):\mathcal{D}\to\quantity{B_\epsilon(D)\subset\Delta(N)\midvert D\in\mc D}::D\mapsto B_{\epsilon}(D)\,,

(7.18)

with
B_{\epsilon}(D)
the open
\epsilon
-ball with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}
in
\Delta(N)
around
D
.

In words, the task of
\epsilon
-learning of a class of distributions
\mathcal{D}
with respect to the total variation distance is, when given access to an unknown
P\in\mathcal{D}
, to learn the description of a distribution
Q
that is
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(P,Q)<\epsilon
close.
Recall the definition of the statistical query oracle, which
maps a class of distributions
\mathcal{D}
to a class of linear functions as in
3.3
.

Definition 7.7:
(Statistical Query Oracle
\mathtt{Stat}
)

Let
N>0
,
\tau>0
and let
P\in\Delta(N)
be an
N
-dimensional probability distribution.
We denote by
\mathtt{Stat}_{\tau}(P)
the
Statistical Query
(SQ) oracle of
P
with tolerance
\tau
.
When queried with a function
\phi:[N]\to[-1,1]
it returns a value
v
, which is promised to lie in the interval

\displaystyle v\in\quantity[P[\phi]-\tau, P[\phi]+\tau]\,,

where

\displaystyle P[\phi]=\Ex_{i\sim P}\quantity[\phi(i)]\,.

(7.19)

As shown in
[
NIS+23
]
and similarly, by
5.7
, the query complexity of
\epsilon
-learning as by
7.6
from
\mathtt{Stat}_{\tau}
is lower bounded by

q\geq\frac{\beta-\Pr_{P\sim\mu}\quantity[\tv(P,Q)\leq 2\epsilon+\tau]}{\max_{\phi}\Pr_{P\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{P[\phi]-Q[\phi]}>\tau]}\,.

(7.20)

Applying this bound to the class of brickwork quantum circuit born distributions with respect to the measure of brickwork random quantum circuits they then show the following.

Theorem 7.8:
(Excerpt from Main Result of
[
NIS+23
]
)

Let
n,d\in\mathbb{N},\epsilon,\beta\in(0,1)
and
\tau>0
and let
\mathcal{A}
be an algorithm that
\epsilon
-learns
\mathcal{C}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}
from
q
many queries to
\mathtt{Stat}_{\tau}(P_{U})
with probability at least
\beta
with respect to
U\sim\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}
.
Then:

•

Linear Depth:

For
d\geq cn
and
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{300}-\tau
it holds

q+1\geq\quantity(\beta- 3200\cdot 2^{-n})\cdot 2^{n-2}\tau^{2}\,,

(7.21)

where
c
is a constant that can be taken to be
c=1.2\cdot 10^{20}
.

•

Infinite Depth:

As
d\to\infty
and for
\epsilon\leq e^{-1}-2^{-\frac{n}{2}-1}-\tau
setting
\xi=e^{-1}-2^{-\frac{n}{2}-1}-\tau
it holds

q+1\geq\frac{\beta-2\exp\quantity(-\frac{2^{n+2} \xi^2}{9 \pi^3})}{2\exp\quantity(-\frac{2^n \tau^2}{9\pi^3})}\,.

(7.22)

We generalize this to the QSQ setting as follows.

Theorem 7.9:
(Hardness of Learning RQC’s from
\mathtt{QStat}
)

Let
n,d\in\mathbb{N},\epsilon,\beta\in(0,1]
and
0<\tau\leq\epsilon
and let
\mathcal{A}
be an algorithm that,
when given access to
\rho\in\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}
learns the corresponding Born distribution
P_{\rho}\in\mathcal{D}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}
to precision
\epsilon
in total variation distance from
q
many queries to
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\rho(U))

with probability at least
\beta
over
U\sim\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}
.
Then,

•

Linear Depth:

For
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{300}-\tau
and
d\geq cn
with
c=2^{67}

it holds

q+1\geq\quantity(\beta- 3200\cdot 2^{-n})\cdot\tau^{2}(2^{n}+1)\,.

(7.23)

•

Infinite Depth:

As
d\to\infty
and for
\epsilon\leq e^{-1}-2^{-\frac{n}{2}-1}-\tau
setting
\xi=e^{-1}-2^{-\frac{n}{2}-1}-\tau
it holds

q+1\geq\frac{\beta-2\exp\quantity(-\frac{2^{n+2} \xi^2}{9 \pi^3})}{2\exp\quantity(-\frac{2^n \tau^2}{9\pi^3})}\,.

(7.24)

The proof follows from the following technical ingredient.

Lemma 7.10:
(Non-Trivial Learning of Approximate
2
-Designs)

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\tau\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon>0
,
let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathrm{U}(N)
that forms an additive
\epsilon
-approximate
2
-design.
Then, for every
O\in\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
with
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
it holds

\displaystyle\Var_{U\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[O\rho(U)]]\leq\frac{1}{N+1}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}

(7.25)

In particular, for the query complexity of non-trivial learning
N
-dimensional quantum states it holds

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu, \tau)\geq\begin{cases}\tau^{2}(N+1)\cdot\quantity(1-o(1))&,\quad\epsilon=o(N^{-1})\,,\\[8.0pt]
\tau^{2}\epsilon^{-1}\cdot\quantity(1-o(1))&,\quad\epsilon=\omega(N^{-1})\,,\\[8.0pt]
\tau^{2}\tfrac{N+1}{1+c}\cdot\quantity(1+\tfrac{\epsilon}{N+1})^{-1}&,\quad\epsilon=\tfrac{1}{c}N^{-1}\,.\end{cases}

(7.26)

Proof:
.

We start by bounding
the Haar variance.
By
6.5
it holds

\displaystyle\Ex_{U\sim\mathrm{U}(N)}\quantity[\tr[O\rho(U)]]=\tr[O\smash{\Ex_{U\sim\mathrm{U}(N)}\quantity[\rho(U)]}]=\frac{\tr[O]}{N}\,,

(7.27)

and

\displaystyle\tr[\quantity(O\otimes O)\cdot\Ex_{U\sim\mu}\quantity[\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U)]]=\frac{\tr[(O\otimes O)\cdot(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}+\mathbb{F})]}{N(N+1)}=\frac{\tr[O^{2}]+\tr[O]^{2}}{N(N+1)}\,,

(7.28)

where we have written the projector onto the symmetric subspace as
P_{\vee}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}+\mathbb{F})
with
\mathbb{F}
the flip operator between the two copies of the Hilbert space.
Thus,

\displaystyle\Var_{U\sim\mathrm{U}(N)}=\frac{\tr[O^{2}]N-\tr[O]^{2}}{N^{2}(N+1)}\leq\frac{1}{N+1}\,,

(7.29)

where the inequality uses
\tr[O^{2}]\leq N
and
-\tr[O]^{2}\leq 0
.

Combining this with
6.6
then yields

\displaystyle\Var_{U\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[U\rho(U)]]\leq\frac{1}{N+1}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}\,.

(7.30)

The claim on
q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)
follows from
(1+x)^{-1}\geq 1-x
for
x\in[0,1]
.
∎

We are now set to
prove
7.9
.

Proof of

7.9
:

Let
N=2^{n}
.
The stated problem can be cast as a learning problem

\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw}}\to\quantity{B_\epsilon(P_\rho)\midvert\rho\in\Qbrqc}::\rho\mapsto B_{\epsilon}(P_{\rho})\,,

(7.31)

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw}}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}(N))::\rho\mapsto\quantity{\sigma\in\mc S(N)\midvert\met_\mrm{diag}(\rho,\sigma)<\epsilon}\,,

(7.32)

where we have introduced the pseudo-distance

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{diag}}(\rho,\sigma)=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1\\
O\text{ diagonal}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{\tr[\rho O] - \tr[\sigma O]}\,,

(7.33)

with
O\in\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
.

By
5.8
, fixing the reference state
\sigma=N^{-1}\mathds{1}
, we lower bound the query complexity of learning
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
from
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}
by

\displaystyle q+1\geq\frac{\beta-\Pr_{U\sim\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc}}}\quantity[\met_\mrm{diag}(\rho(U), \sigma)<2\epsilon+\tau]}{\max_{\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1}\Pr_{U\sim\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc}}}\quantity[\abs{\tr[\rho(U) O]-\tr[\sigma O]}>\tau]}

(7.34)

\displaystyle=\beta-\quantity(\beta-\Pr_{U\sim\mubrqc}\qty[\tv(P_U, \mc U)<2\epsilon+\tau])_{U\sim\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc}}}\quantity[\tv(P_U, \mc U)<2\epsilon+\tau]\cdot\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mubrqc,\sigma,\tau)^{-1}\,.

(7.35)

Since
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{diag}}(\rho,\sigma)=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_{\rho},P_{\sigma})
we find that the numerator is equal to the corresponding numerator from the proof of
7.8
, which is bounded by a far from uniform bound.
In the linear depth regime the far from uniform bound is proven via the
\delta
-approximate 8-design property with
\delta=O(N^{-3})
, implying that
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc}}
forms a
\delta
-apprioximate 2-design.
Thus, noting that
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc}}
is an exact 1-design, the claim follows by bounding the denominator by
7.10
.

At infinite depth, it suffices to note that the proof in
[
NIS+23
]
, which goes via Lemma 22 and Lemma 23 together with Levy’s Lemma, is performed for general bounded operators and not just diagonal operators.
Hence, we can directly carry over the corresponding bounds.
∎

Remark 7.1:

As used in the previous proof, statistical query problems over output distributions of quantum states with respect to the measure induced by a measure over quantum states on the one hand, and quantum statistical query problems over quantum states with respect to this measure over quantum states and the same identification of solutions on the other hand, can be related.
In particular, the triviality as measured by
\mathtt{triv}
of both problems is equivalent.
More generally, since
q_{\mathtt{nt}}
lower bounds the query complexity for all problems over the same source set for which
\mathtt{triv}
is sufficiently bounded away from one, any bound on
q_{\mathtt{nt}}
translates from one non-trivial problem to any other non-trivial problem over the same source set.
Therefore, in the following, we restrict to learning quantum states, which is more natural in the QSQ setting, without mentioning the respective implications on learning the output distributions.

7.2.2
Clifford Unitaries: Part I

In the same spirit, we can show hardness of learning stabilizer states.
Moreover, those results imply hardness of learning the output distribution as defined in
Equation

7.31
via
[
NIS+23
, Lemma 27]
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)
denote the
n
-qubit Clifford group,
denote by
\mu_{\,\mathrm{Cl},n}
the uniform measure over
\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)

and denote by
\mathrm{Stab}(n)=\quantity{\rho=\ketbra{\psi}\midvert\ket\psi=C\ket 0\,,\; C\in\Cl(2,n)}\subset\mathcal{S}(2^{n})

the set of
n
-qubit stabilizer states.
Then, we find.

Theorem 7.11:
(Hardness of Learning Stabilizer States from
\mathtt{QStat}
)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta\in(0,1]
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algorithm that learns
\mathrm{Stab}(n)
from
q
queries to
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}
with probability at least
\beta
over
U\sim\mu_{\,\mathrm{Cl},n}
.
Then, it holds

q+1\geq\beta\tau^{2}2^{n-2}\,.

(7.36)

Proof:
.

We write the learning problem as

\mathcal{Z}:\mathrm{Stab}(n)\to\quantity{\set{\rho}\midvert\rho\in\mrm{Stab}(n)}\,.

(7.37)

By definition, it holds
\mathcal{Z}_{\rho}=\quantity{\rho}=\mathcal{Z}(\rho)
.
Thus, since for every pure state
\rho
one can distinguish
\sigma=2^{-n}\mathds{1}
from
\rho
by simply querying
\rho
,
\tr[\sigma\rho]=2^{-n}\neq 1=\tr[\rho^{2}]
,
the problem as in
Equation

7.37
is deterministically
1
-verifyable with respect to
\sigma
.

We apply
4.6
with respect to
\sigma
and obtain

q+1\geq\beta\cdot\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mucl,\sigma,\tau)^{-1}=\beta\cdot\mathrm{frac}\quantity(\mb S^{N-1},\sigma,\tau)^{-1}\,,

(7.38)

where the second step is due to
\mu_{\,\mathrm{Cl}}
being an exact
2
-design.

7.10
then yields the claim
6
6

6

Alternatively one can reduce the expression to the diagonal setting.
The Clifford group forms a unitary
2
-design and hence, the moment operators absorb the diagonalizing unitary for
O
.
In particular, we can reduce the expression to that with
O
being restricted to diagonal operators.
The claim then follows from observing that this reduces the denominator to that of e.g. Eq. (22) in
[
NIS+23
, Lemma 7]
with
\delta=0
.
There, the corresponding concentration for this expression in the diagonal setting is proven via second moments and assuming a unitary
2
-design.
.

∎

Remark 7.2:

The query complexity of the problem as in
Equation

7.37
would remain unchanged when introducing an approximation parameter
\epsilon<\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{2}}}
in trace distance.
To see this, note that any pair of stabilizer states
\rho,\sigma
have overlap
\absolutevalue{\braket{\psi}{\phi}}=2^{-\frac{k}{2}}
with integer
k
.
This implies by Fuchs-van de Graaf for pure stabilizer states
\rho\neq\sigma
(see
Equation

6.4
) that
\norm{\rho-\sigma}_{\tr}\geq\sqrt{2}
.

As a consequence of
7.11
we find the following corollary.

Corollary 7.12:
(Hardness of learning
\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw}}(\mathrm{Cl}(2,2))
from
\mathtt{QStat}
)

Let
n,d\in\mathbb{N}
. Learning
n
-qubit depth
d
stabilizer states
\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}(\mathrm{Cl}(2,2))
requires at least
q
queries to
\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}
, with

q+1\geq 2^{\nicefrac{{d}}{{10}}}\tau^{2}\,.

(7.39)

Proof:
.

Note that the statement is of a worst-case nature.
In particular, it suffices to prove the query complexity for any measure over
\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}
setting
\beta=1
.

Any
n
-qubit Clifford unitary can be implemented by a depth
9n
brickwork Clifford circuit
[
BM21
]
.
The claim thus follows from
7.11
using the measure induced by
\mu_{\,\mathrm{Cl},\left\lfloor\frac{d}{9}\right\rfloor,d}\otimes\mu_{\mathds{1}}
, where
\mu_{\mathds{1}}
is the point measure on the identity on the remaining
n-\left\lfloor\frac{d}{9}\right\rfloor
qubits.
∎

7.3
Two-Copy Quantum Statistical Queries

In the previous section we have seen that the results from
[
NIS+23
]
do generalize from the setting of statistical query access to
P_{\rho}
to the setting of quantum statistical query access to
\rho
.
In this section we will make a further extension to what we call the two-copy QSQ oracle.

Definition 7.8:
(Two Copy Quantum Statistical Queries
\mathtt{2QStat}
)

Let
N>0
,
\tau>0
and let
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(N)\subset\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
with
0<\rho<\mathds{1}
be an
N
dimensional quantum state.
We denote by
\mathtt{2QStat}_{\tau}(\rho)
the
Two-Copy Quantum Statistical Query
(2QSQ) oracle of
\rho
with tolerance
\tau
.
When queried with an observable
O\in\mathbb{H}^{N^{2}\times N^{2}}
with
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
it returns a value
v
, which is promised to lie in the interval

v\in\quantity[\tr[\rho\otimes\rho\cdot O]-\tau, \tr[\rho\otimes\rho\cdot O]+\tau]\,.

(7.40)

The two-copy QSQ oracle is thus nothing but a QSQ oracle to two copies of the state

\mathtt{2QStat}_{\tau}(\rho)=\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}(\rho\otimes\rho)\,.

(7.41)

The transition from
\mathtt{Stat}(P_{\rho})
to
\mathtt{QStat}(\rho)
corresponds to the enhanced power of measuring expectation values in any basis versus only in the computational basis.
Likewise, the transition from
\mathtt{QStat}(\rho)
to
\mathtt{2QStat}(\rho)
corresponds to the enhanced power of measuring expectation values in (simple) entangled bases, such as the Bell basis, versus non-entangled bases.

We now show how our formalism extends to the two-copy setting.
Let
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{S}(N)
be a set of quantum states and let
\mathcal{Z}_{1}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a learning problem over a set of
N
dimensional quantum states.
Naturally
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
is equivalent to the learning problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{2}:\mathcal{Q}^{\odot 2}\coloneq\quantity{\rho\otimes\rho\midvert\rho\in\mc Q}\subset\mathcal{S}(N^{2})\to\mathcal{Z}_{1}(\mathcal{Q})\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})\,,

(7.42)

where we have introduced the operation
\odot

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\odot k}=\quantity{\smash{\underbrace{\rho\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho}_{\times k}}\midvert\rho\in\mc Q}\,.

(7.43)

The
\mathtt{QStat}
oracle maps learning problems over quantum states to linear learning problems.
The same can be said about the
\mathtt{2QStat}
oracle: it maps quantum learning problems to linear learning problems.
The linear functions corresponding to the
\mathtt{QStat}
oracle are functions of the type

\displaystyle f_{\rho}:\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}\to\mathbb{R}::O\mapsto\tr[\rho O]\,.

(7.45)

Similarly, the linear functions corresponding to
\mathtt{2QStat}
are functions of the type

\displaystyle f_{\rho}^{\odot 2}=f_{\rho\otimes\rho}:\mathbb{H}^{N^{2}\times N^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}::O\mapsto\tr[(\rho\otimes\rho)O]\,.

(7.46)

It is worth mentioning that problems remain in general non-trivial under
\odot
.
This is due to the tensor product structure.
In particular,

\displaystyle\rho\otimes\rho-\norm{\rho\otimes\rho- \Ex_\sigma[\sigma\otimes\sigma]}_{\sigma}[\sigma\otimes\sigma]_{\tr}\geq\tr_{2}[\rho\otimes\rho-\norm{\tr_2[\rho\otimes\rho- \Ex_\sigma[\sigma\otimes\sigma]]}_{\sigma}[\sigma\otimes\sigma]]_{\tr}=\rho-\norm{\rho- \Ex_\sigma[\sigma]}_{\sigma}[\sigma]_{\tr}\,,

or similarly, for any
O\in\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}

\displaystyle\tr[O\otimes\mathds{1}(\rho\otimes\rho-\Ex_{\sigma}[\sigma\otimes\sigma])]=\tr[O(\rho-\Ex_{\sigma}[\sigma])]\,.

Before we generalize the hardness results from the previous section let us introduce a simple positive result for learning from
\mathtt{2QStat}
.

Theorem 7.13:
(Purity Testing)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta>0
, let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{pure}}\subset\mathcal{S}(N)
be the set of pure states
and denote by
\mathcal{S}_{\delta}(N)=\quantity{\rho\in\mc S(N)\midvert\tr[\rho^2]\leq 1-\delta}
the set of states that are at least
\delta
mixed.
Let
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{pure}}(N),\mathcal{S}_{\delta}(N))
denote the problem of deciding whether an unknown
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{pure}}(N)\cup\mathcal{S}_{\delta}(N)
is pure, or at least
\delta
mixed.

If
\tau<\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}
, then
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{pure}}(N),\mathcal{S}_{\delta}(N))
can be solved from a single query to
\mathtt{2QStat}_{\tau}
.

Proof:
.

Recall that for
\mathbb{F}
the flip operator between two copies of Hilbert space it holds

\tr[(\rho\otimes\rho)\mathbb{F}]=\tr[\rho^{2}]\,.

(7.47)

Thus, measuring the expectation of
\mathbb{F}
to accuracy
\tau<\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}
suffices to decide between pure and at least
\delta
mixed.
∎

7.3.1
Random Quantum Circuits: Part II

We start by lifting
7.10
to the 2QSQ setting by means of requiring
4
-designs instead of
2
-designs, which then will imply the corresponding statements on
\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw}}
and
\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc}}
from
6.1
and
6.2
.

Theorem 7.14:
(Hardness of Learning Unitary
4
-Designs from
\mathtt{2QStat}
)

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tau,\epsilon\in(0,1]
.
Let

\mu
be any measure over
\mathcal{S}(N)
that forms an additive
\epsilon
-approximate projective
4
-design.
Then it holds

\displaystyle\Var_{\rho\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[(\rho\otimes\rho)O]]=O\quantity(N^{-1})+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}\,.

(7.48)

In particular, for the query complexity of non-trivial learning from
\mathtt{2QStat}
it holds

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu^{\odot 2},\tau)\geq\begin{cases}\Omega\quantity(\tau^2 N)&,\quad\epsilon=O(N^{-1})\\[8.0pt]
\Omega\quantity(\tau^2\epsilon^{-1})&,\quad\epsilon=\omega(N^{-1})\,.\end{cases}

(7.49)

Proof:
.

We first show the claim in case
\epsilon=0
corresponding to a projective
4
-design.
Then, for any
O\in\mathbb{H}^{N^{2}\times N^{2}}
it holds

\displaystyle\Var_{\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\tr[(\rho\otimes\rho) O]]=\Var_{\rho\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\tr[(\rho\otimes\rho) O]]=\quantity(M_4(O\otimes O)-M_2(O)^2)\,,

(7.50)

where we have introduced the moments

\displaystyle M_{r}(A)=\Ex_{\rho\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\tr[\rho^{\otimes r}A]]=\tr[\smash{\underbrace{\Ex_{\rho}\quantity[\rho^{\otimes r}]}_{K^{(r)}_{\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}}}A]\,,

where

\displaystyle A\in\mathbb{C}^{N^{r}\times N^{r}}\,.

(7.51)

By
6.5
it holds

\displaystyle M_{2}(O)=\binom{N+1}{2}^{-1}\tr[P_{\vee}O]=\binom{N+1}{2}^{-1}\frac{1}{2}\tr[\quantity(\id\otimes\id+ \mb F)O]\,,

(7.53)

with
P_{\vee}
the projector on the symmetric subspace and
\mathbb{F}
the flip operator between the two copies of the Hilbert space.
Similarly,

\displaystyle M_{4}(O\otimes O)=\binom{N+3}{4}^{-1}\tr[P_{\vee^{4}}O\otimes O]=\binom{N+3}{4}^{-1}\frac{1}{4!}\sum_{\varsigma\in S_{4}}\tr[\Pi(\varsigma)O\otimes O]\,,

(7.54)

with
P_{\vee^{4}}
the symmetric projector on the four copies of the Hilbert space and
\Pi(\varsigma)
the unitary representation on the copies of the Hilbert space corresponding to the permutation
\varsigma
.

Now define
(n)_{k}=\frac{n!}{(n-k)!}
and note that

\binom{X}{r}r!=(X)_{r}
.
We bound

\displaystyle\binom{N+3}{4}^{-1}\frac{1}{4!}-\quantity(\binom{N+1}{2}^{-1}\frac{1}{2!})^{-1}\frac{1}{2!}^{2}=(N+3)_{4}^{-1}-(N+1)_{2}^{-2}

(7.55)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{4}}\quantity(
\qty(\prod_{j=1}^4 \qty(1+2\frac{k}{N}-\frac{jk}{kN}))^{-1}
-
\qty(\prod_{j=1}^2 \qty(1+2\frac{k}{N}-\frac{jk}{kN}))^{-2}
)

(7.56)

\displaystyle=-4N^{-5}+O\quantity(N^{-6})\,.

(7.57)

Defining
M_{4}^{\otimes}
and
M_{4}^{\times}
via

\displaystyle M_{4}(O\otimes O)=\underbrace{\binom{N+3}{4}^{-1}\frac{1}{4!}\sum_{\varsigma_{1},\varsigma_{2}\in S_{2}}\tr[\Pi(\varsigma_{1})\otimes\Pi(\varsigma_{2})O\otimes O]}_{\eqcolon M_{4}^{\otimes}(O\otimes O)}\,+\,M_{4}^{\times}(O\otimes O)\,,

(7.58)

from
Equation

7.57
then follows

\absolutevalue{M_4^\otimes(O\otimes O)-M_2(O)^2}\leq 4N^{-1}+O(N^{-2})\,.

(7.59)

In order to estimate
M_{4}^{\times}(O\otimes O)
note that for any
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1

it holds

\absolutevalue{M_4^\times(O\otimes O)}\leq M_{4}^{\times}(\mathds{1}^{\otimes 4})\,.

(7.60)

Since
\Var_{\rho}[\tr[(\rho\otimes\rho)(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1})]]=0
it must hold

\absolutevalue{M_4^\times(O\otimes O)}\leq M_{2}(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1})^{2}-M_{4}(\mathds{1}^{\otimes 4})\leq 4N^{-1}+O(N^{-2})\,.

(7.61)

Thus, we conclude that

\Var_{\rho\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\tr[(\rho\otimes\rho)O]]\leq 8N^{-1}+O(N^{-2})=O(N^{-1})\,,

(7.62)

proving the claim for
\epsilon=0
.

The case
\epsilon>0
then follows
by
6.6
, since

\displaystyle\Var_{\rho\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[(\rho\otimes\rho)O]]\leq\Var_{\rho\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\tr[(\rho\otimes\rho)O]]+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}\,.

(7.63)

The claim on
q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu^{\odot 2}, \tau)
then follows from expanding
(O(N^{-1}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}))^{-1}
where the cases differentiate which of the contributions due to
\epsilon
and
N^{-1}
is the dominating one.
∎

This immediately implies the extension of the results from
Section

7.2.1
to the 2QSQ-setting.

Corollary 7.15:

Let
n,d\in\mathbb{N},\epsilon,\in(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},1],\beta\in(0,1]
and
0<\tau\leq\epsilon
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algorithm that,
when given access to
\rho(U)\in\mathcal{Q}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}
learns the corresponding Born distribution
P_{U}\in\mathcal{D}_{\,\mathrm{bw},n,d}
to precision
\epsilon
in total variation distance from
q
many queries to
\mathtt{2QStat}_{\tau}[\rho]

with probability at least
\beta
over
U\sim\mu_{\,\mathrm{brqc},n,d}
.
Then, for
d\geq cn
and
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{300}-\tau
it holds

q+1\geq\Omega\quantity((\beta-b_{c,n})\tau^2 2^n)\,,

(7.64)

with
c=2^{67}
as in
7.9
.

Proof:
.

The claim follows immediately from combining the far from uniform bound as used in the proof of
7.9
via
[
NIS+23
, Corollary 11]
with
7.14
.
∎

7.3.2
Clifford Unitaries: Part II

The proofs for random unitary and random Clifford circuits in
Section

7.2
were almost identical.
This is due to the fact that both classes form a unitary
2
-design.
As we have seen in the previous section
Section

7.3.1
, however, we were using the
4
-design property to show hardness of learning random quantum circuits from
\mathtt{2QStat}
.
Although the Clifford group “gracefully fails to be a
4
-design”
[
ZKG+16
]
we are still able to extend those results to Clifford circuits.

Theorem 7.16:
(Two-Copy Hardness of Learning Stabilizer States)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta\in(0,1]
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algorithm that learns any
\rho(U)\in\mathrm{Stab}(n)
from
q
queries to
\mathtt{2QStat}_{\tau}
with probability at least
\beta
over
U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)
.
Then, it holds

q+1\geq\Omega\quantity(\beta\tau^2 2^n)\,.

(7.65)

Note that
7.16
straight forwardly lifts
7.12
to the 2QSQ setting with
2^{\nicefrac{{d}}{{10}}}
replaced by
\Omega(2^{\nicefrac{{d}}{{10}}})
.

Proof:
.

Let
N=2^{n}
.
We start by recalling Theorem 3 from
[
KZG16
]
, which states in Equation (32)

\displaystyle K_{\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}^{(4)}(\outerproduct{z}{z})=\Ex_{U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}\quantity[\rho(U)^{\otimes 4}]=N\binom{N+2}{3}^{-1}\quantity((\alpha(z)-\beta(z))P_1 + \beta(z)P_{\vee^4})\,,

(7.66)

with
z=\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
the input state,
-2(N(N+1))^{-1}\leq\alpha(z)\leq N^{-1}
,
\alpha(\ket{0}^{\otimes n})=N^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\beta(z)=\frac{4(1-\alpha(z))}{(N+4)(N-1)}\,,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\alpha(z)-\beta(z)}=O(N^{-1})\,,

(7.67)

and
P_{1}=P_{\vee^{4}}Q
with
Q
a rank
N^{2}
projector.
Rewriting this yields

\displaystyle K_{\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}^{(4)}\quantity(\ketbra{0}^{\otimes n})=\Theta\quantity(N^{-3})P_{1}+N\binom{N+2}{3}^{-1}\frac{4(1-N^{-1})}{(N+4)(N-1)}P_{\vee^{4}}\,.

(7.68)

For any bounded
O\in\mathbb{H}^{N^{2}\times N^{2}}
,
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
we now bound the variance

\displaystyle\Var_{U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}\quantity[\tr[O(\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U))]]=\Ex_{U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}\quantity[\tr[O(\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U))]^2]-\Ex_{U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}\quantity[\tr[O(\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U))]]^{2}\,.

(7.69)

By the
2
-design property, the last term coincides with the corresponding expectation value over the full unitary group
M_{2}(O)^{2}
as in
Equation

7.53
.
Focussing on the first term then yields

\displaystyle\Ex_{U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}\quantity[\tr[O(\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U))]^2]=\tr[(O\otimes O)\Ex_{U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}\quantity[\rho(U)^{\otimes 4}]]

(7.70)

\displaystyle=O(N^{-1})+\underbrace{N\binom{N+2}{3}^{-1}\frac{4(1-N^{-1})}{(N+4)(N-1)}}_{\eqcolon A(N)}\tr[(O\otimes O)P_{\vee^{4}}]\,,

(7.71)

where we have used that
Q
and hence
P_{1}=P_{\vee^{4}}Q
is a rank
N^{2}
projector, which implies
\Theta(N^{-3})\mathrm{tr}[(O\otimes O)P_{1}]=O(N^{-1})
.
In order to compare
Equations

7.70
and
7.54
we rewrite
A(N)
as

\displaystyle N\binom{N+2}{3}^{-1}\frac{4(1-N^{-1})}{(N+4)(N-1)}=\binom{N+3}{4}^{-1}\cdot\frac{1+3N^{-1}}{1+4N^{-1}}\,.

(7.72)

We find

\displaystyle M_{4}(O\otimes O)-\absolutevalue{M_4(O\otimes O) - \Ex_{\Cl(2,n)}[\tr[O(\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U))]^2]}_{\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}[\tr[O(\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U))]^{2}]\leq O(N^{-1})+\absolutevalue{M_4(O\otimes O) - A(N)\tr[(O\otimes O)P_{\vee^4}]}

(7.73)

\displaystyle=O(N^{-1})+\absolutevalue{1-\frac{1+3N^{-1}}{1+4N^{-1}}}M_{4}(O\otimes O)=O(N^{-1})\quantity(1 + M_4(O\otimes O))=O(N^{-1})\,,

(7.74)

where we have used that
\absolutevalue{M_4(O\otimes O)}\leq\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\leq 1
.
Thus, from
Equations

7.69
and
7.14
we find

\displaystyle\Var_{U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}\quantity[\tr[O(\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U))]]=M_{4}(O\otimes O)-M_{2}(O)^{2}+O(N^{-1})=O(N^{-1})\,.

(7.75)

The hardness of learning stabilizer states from
\mathtt{2QStat}_{\tau}
access is then bounded similar to

Equation

7.38
by
q+1\geq\beta q_{\mathtt{nt}}(\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)^{\odot 2},\tau)
with

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\Cl(2,n)^{\odot 2},\tau)^{-1}\leq\max_{O}\Pr_{U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}\quantity[\abs{\tr[O(\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U))] - \tr[O\Ex_{U\sim\Cl(2,n)}[\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U)]]}>\tau]>\tau

(7.76)

\displaystyle\leq\tau^{-2}\Var_{U\sim\mathrm{Cl}(2,n)}\quantity[\tr[O(\rho(U)\otimes\rho(U))]]=\tau^{-2}O(N^{-1})\,,

(7.77)

with
O\in\mathbb{H}^{N^{2}\times N^{2}}
and
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
, which proves the claim.
∎

7.4
Multi-Copy Quantum Statistical Queries

Let us now extend the idea from the previous
Section

7.3
.
Similar to the two-copy QSQ oracle
\mathtt{2QStat}
, which allows for entangling always two copies of the state, we define the multi-copy QSQ oracle as allowing for entangling multiple copies of the state per query.
Thus, the multi-copy QSQ oracle will, depending on the allowed amount of entangled copies, naturally interpolate between the statistical setting of QSQ’s and the more general quantum learning, tomography or metrology settings.

Definition 7.9:
(Multi Copy QSQ’s)

Let
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
K=N^{k}
,
\tau>0
and let
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(N)\subset\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
with
0<\rho<\mathds{1}
be an
N
dimensional quantum state.
We denote by
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}(\rho)
the
k
-Copy Quantum Statistical Query
(QSQ) oracle of
\rho
with tolerance
\tau
.
When queried with an observable
O\in\mathbb{H}^{K\times K}
with
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
it returns a value
v
, which is promised to lie in the interval

v\in\quantity[\tr[\rho^{\otimes k} \cdot O]-\tau, \tr[\rho^{\otimes k} \cdot O]+\tau]\,.

(7.78)

As in case of the
\mathtt{2QStat}
oracle, the
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}
oracle can be rewritten in terms of the
\mathtt{QStat}
oracle as

\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}(\rho)=\mathtt{QStat}_{\tau}\quantity(\rho^{\otimes k})\,.

(7.79)

Similarly, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
we can identify a learning problem over quantum states
\mathcal{Z}_{1}:\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(N)\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
with a corresponding learning problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{k}:\mathcal{Q}^{\odot k}\subset\mathcal{S}\quantity(K)\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})\,,

(7.80)

with
K=N^{k}
.
The
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}
oracle then maps quantum learning problems to linear learning problems and the linear functions corresponding to the
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}
oracle are of the type

\displaystyle f_{\rho}:\mathbb{H}^{K\times K}\to\mathbb{R}::O\mapsto\tr[\rho^{\otimes k}O]\,.

(7.81)

It is easy to see from Höffding bounds that any problem, which can be solved from
q
queries to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}(\rho)
can, with probability
1-\delta
be solved from
O(\nicefrac{{qk}}{{\tau^{2}}}\log(\sfrac{q}{\delta}))
copies of the unknown state
\rho
(this is similar to
7.2
).
The number of copies in fact can be further reduced by the tools for shadow tomography to

\displaystyle\widetilde{O}\quantity(\frac{k\log(\sfrac{1}{\delta})\cdot\log(q)^4\cdot\log(N)}{\tau^4})\,,

where
\widetilde{O}
hides polynomial factors in
\log\log(q),\log(k\log(N)),\log(\sfrac 1\tau)
. See for example
[
Aar18
]
and Equation (B9) in
[
JGM+23
]
to map the observables to two output POVM’s.

In reverse, the multi-copy QSQ framework also allows for implications about the sample complexity from entangled measurements by means of multi-copy QSQ results.
The following lemma makes the previously mentioned notion of “interpolation” between statistical and entangled measurements rigorous.

Lemma 7.17:
(Entangled Measurements to Multi-Copy QSQ reduction)

Let
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in[0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})
and denote by
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(N)
a set of
N
dimensional quantum states.
Let
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
be a learning problem over
\mathcal{Q}
with
\mathcal{T}
the set of solutions.
Assume that for all
\mathcal{T}_{i}\neq\mathcal{T}_{j}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
it holds
\mathcal{T}_{i}\cap\mathcal{T}_{j}=\varnothing
.
If there is a quantum algorithm that, with probability
1-\delta
learns
\mathcal{Z}
from
k
copies,
then, for every
\tau<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-\delta
there exists a statistical algorithm that learns
\mathcal{Z}
from
q
queries to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}
with

\displaystyle q=\left\lceil\log(\abs{\mc Z(\mc S)})\right\rceil\,.

(7.82)

In particular, setting
\tau=\nicefrac{{1}}{{10}}
, if learning
\mathcal{Z}
requires at least
q>\left\lceil\log(\abs{\mc Z(\mc S)})\right\rceil
queries to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}
to be solved, then
\mathcal{Z}
cannot be solved with probability
1-\delta\geq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
given
k
copies from entangled measurements.

We note that the disjointedness assumption
\mathcal{T}_{i}\cap\mathcal{T}_{j}=\varnothing
can in general be enforced without changing the sample complexity of the quantum algorithm, for example by means of an
\epsilon
-net on
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof of

7.17
:

Set
K=N^{k}
and
L=\left\lceil\log(\abs{\mc Z(\mc S)})\right\rceil
.
Let
\quantity{\Pi_t\in\mb H^{K\times K}: t\in\mc T}
denote the POVM corresponding to the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}
.
Let
\mathtt{tree}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S}))
be a tree decomposition of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
, that is

\displaystyle\mathtt{tree}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S}))=\quantity{\mc P_{l,b}\mc T\subseteq\mc Z(\mc S)\midvert 0\leq l\leq L\,, \;b\in\mb F_2^{l}}\,,\quad\text{with}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{P}_{0,0}\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})\,,&&\mathcal{P}_{l,b0}\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{P}_{l,b1}\mathcal{T}=\varnothing\,,&&&\mathcal{P}_{l,b0}\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{P}_{l,b1}\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{P}_{(l-1),b}\mathcal{T}\,,&&&&\text{for all $l\in[L]$ and $b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{l-1}$}\,,\end{aligned}

where we identify
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{0}=\quantity{0}
and for
b=(b_{1},\dots,b_{l-1})
denoted by
b0=(b_{1},\dots,b_{l-1},0)
and similarly for
b1
.
Note that each leaf
\mathcal{P}_{L,b}
corresponds to a set in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{S})
.

For any pair
(l,bi)
with
b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{l-1}
and
i=0,1
define the POVM elements

\displaystyle\Pi_{l-1,b}=\Pi_{l,b0}+\Pi_{l,b1}\,,

and

\displaystyle\Pi_{L,b(s)}=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{Z}(s)}\Pi_{t}\,,

(7.83)

where the bit string
b(s)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{L}
denotes the path in
\mathtt{tree}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S}))
that singles out the subset
\mathcal{Z}(s)\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
.
The statistical learning algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{stat}}
with access to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}
then proceeds as follows.

Given access to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}(\rho)
for some unknown
\rho\in\mathcal{Q}
, in the first step
l=1
the algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{stat}}
queries one of
\Pi_{1,0}
and
\Pi_{1,1}
and receives a value
v_{1,i}
for the corresponding
i
.
At any given step
l\leq L
, the algorithm has a value
v_{(l-1),bi}
with
b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{l-2}
and
i=0
or
1
.
Let
v_{(l-1),bi}
be greater than
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{stat}}
queries in the
l
’th step one of the observables
\Pi_{l,bi0}
and
\Pi_{l,bi1}
. Else, if
v_{(l-1),bi}
is smaller
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
it queries in the
l
’th step one of the observables
\Pi_{l,b\neg i0}
and
\Pi_{l,b\neg i1}
.
In the
L+1
’st step the algorithm has obtained one value
v_{L,bi}
.
Assume without loss of generality that
v_{L,bi}
is greater
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
(else
v_{L,b\neg i}>\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
will hold) and denote by
\mathcal{Z}(s)
the corresponding solution set. Then
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{stat}}
returns any
t\in\mathcal{Z}(s)
.

Let us now analyze the algorithm.
By construction,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{stat}}
makes at most
L
queries. Thus, it remains to prove the correctness.
Since for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
it holds
0\leq\Pi_{t}\leq\mathds{1}
and
\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pi_{t}=\mathds{1}
, each query made is a valid query.
Moreover, at each step

\displaystyle v_{l,b}\geq\tr[\Pi_{l,b}\rho^{\otimes k}]-\tau=\quantity(\sum_{t\in\mc Z(s)\in\mc P_{l,b}\mc T} \tr[\Pi_t \rho^{\otimes k}])-\tau\,.

(7.84)

Now, assume that in the
l
’th step, the correct solution set
\mathcal{Z}(s)\in\mathcal{P}_{l,bi}\mathcal{T}
.
Then, by assumption and since all
\tr[\Pi_{t}\rho^{\otimes k}]\geq 0
, we find
v_{l,bi}\geq\sum_{t\in\mathcal{Z}(s)}\tr[\Pi_{t}\rho]-\tau=1-\delta-\tau>/sfrac12
and similarly
v_{l,b\neg i}\leq\delta+\tau<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
,
which completes the proof.
∎

An example application of
7.17
is
7.13
.
In the same spirit, we make the following statement.

Corollary 7.18:
(Stabilizer Testing)

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{pure}}(2^{n})
be the set of pure
n
-qubit states and let
\mathrm{Stab}(n)\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{pure}}(2^{n})
be the set of
n
-qubit stabilizer states.
Further, let
\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{Stab}}(2^{n})\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{pure}}(2^{n})
be the set of pure states that are at least
\epsilon
-far from any stabilizer state in the sense that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\braket{S}{\psi}}^{2}\leq 1-\epsilon^{2}

for all

\displaystyle\outerproduct{S}{S}\in\mathrm{Stab}(n)\,,\;\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\in\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{Stab}}(2^{n})\,.

(7.85)

Then, for
\tau<\nicefrac{{\epsilon^{2}}}{{8}}
the problem
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathrm{Stab}(n),\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{Stab}}(2^{n}))
of deciding whether an unknown pure state is a stabilizer state, or at least
\epsilon
-far from all stabilizer states, can be solved from a single query to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{6}_{\tau}
.

Proof:
.

By Theorem 3.3 from
[
GNW21
]
their Algorithm 1 (see also their Figure 2 for the circuit description) performs a measurement on
6
copies of the unknown state
\rho
and, if
\rho\in\mathrm{Stab}(n)
returns
1
.
However, if
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{Stab}}(2^{n})
then the algorithm returns
1
with probability at most
\leq 1-\nicefrac{{\epsilon^{2}}}{{4}}
.
Denote by
\Pi_{0/1}
the POVM corresponding to their algorithm. Then in other words

\displaystyle\tr[\rho^{\otimes 6}\Pi_{1}]=\begin{cases}1\,,&\rho\in\mathrm{Stab}(n)\,,\\
c_{\rho}\leq 1-\nicefrac{{\epsilon^{2}}}{{4}}\,,&\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{Stab}}(2^{n})\,.\end{cases}

(7.86)

Thus, a single query to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{6}_{\tau}(\rho)
with
\tau<\nicefrac{{\epsilon^{2}}}{{8}}
suffices to decide whether
\rho
is a stabilizer or at least
\epsilon
-far.
∎

7.4.1
Haar Random States

We start by bounding the complexity of non-trivial learning Haar random states given access to multi-copy quantum statistical queries
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}(\rho)
in terms of the copies
k
and dimension
N
.

Theorem 7.19:
(Hardness of Learning Random States From
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}
)

Let
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tau>0
.
Let
\mathcal{S}(N)
be the set of
N
dimensional quantum states and denote by
U\sim\mathrm{U}(N)
the Haar measure.
Then, the query complexity
q_{\mathrm{nt}}
of non-trivial learning
\rho(U)\in\mathcal{S}(N)
with respect to
U\sim\mathrm{U}(N)
is bounded by

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity({\mb S^{N-1}}^{\odot k}, \tau)\geq\exp\quantity(\frac{N\tau^2}{9\pi^3k^2})\,.

(7.87)

Remark 7.3:

7.19
states that any non-trivial learning problem over Haar random states requires at least doubly exponentially many queries to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}
for any
k<\sqrt{N}=2^{\nicefrac{{n}}{{2}}}
.
By
7.17
this directly translates to the setting of quantum samples where one is given access to copies of the unknown state
\rho
.
In reverse, this implies that any efficient learning algorithm which works on all states must be trivial in the sense that
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z,\mu)
is exponentially close to one with respect to Haar random states.
One example for this is Aaronson’s shadow tomography
[
Aar18
]
.

The proof relies on Levy’s lemma which can be stated as follows (see for example
[
BCH+21
]
).

Lemma 7.20:
(Levy’s Lemma)

Let
f:\mathbb{S}^{N-1}\to\mathbb{R}
be a real function on the complex unit sphere with Lipschitz constant
L
with respect to the euclidean distance and denote by
z\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
the Haar measure on the sphere. Then it holds

\displaystyle\Pr_{z\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\abs{f(z) - \Ex_{w\sim\mb S^{N-1}}\qty[f(w)]}>\tau]>\tau\leq\exp\quantity(-\frac{4N\tau^2}{9\pi^3L^2})

\displaystyle\text{for any }\tau>0\,.

(7.88)

Proof of

7.19
:

For any
\ket{\psi_0}\in\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
the unitary Haar measure
U\sim\mathrm{U}(N)
induces the spherical Haar measure
U\ket{\psi_0}\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}
.
Now, let
K=N^{k}
and for an arbitrary
O\in\mathbb{H}^{K\times K}
define the function

\displaystyle f:\mathbb{S}^{N-1}\to\mathbb{R}::\ket{\psi}\mapsto\!\matrixelement*{\psi^{\otimes k}}{O}{\psi^{\otimes k}}\,.

(7.89)

To bound the Lipschitz constant we proceed as in
[
NIS+23
, Lemma 22]
.
First note that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{f(\psi)-f(\varphi)}=\absolutevalue{\tr[O\qty(\ketbra{\psi}^{\otimes k} - \ketbra{\varphi}^{\otimes k})]}\leq\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\norm{\ketbra{\psi}^{\otimes k} - \ketbra{\varphi}^{\otimes k}}_{\tr}

(7.90)

\displaystyle\leq 2\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\norm{\ket{\psi}^{\otimes k} - \ket{\varphi}^{\otimes k}}_{2}\,,

(7.91)

where the last inequality is due to Fuchs-van de Graaf

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\norm{\ketbra{\psi}^{\otimes k} - \ketbra{\varphi}^{\otimes k}}_{\tr}=\sqrt{1-\absolutevalue{\braket{\psi}{\varphi}}^{2k}}=\sqrt{1+\absolutevalue{\braket{\psi}{\varphi}}^{k}}\sqrt{1-\absolutevalue{\braket{\psi}{\varphi}}^{k}}

(7.92)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\sqrt{1-\mathrm{Re}(\innerproduct{\psi}{\varphi}^{k})}=\norm{\ket\psi^{\otimes k}-\ket\varphi^{\otimes k}}_{2}\,.

(7.93)

Next, observe that

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}^{\otimes k}-\ket{\varphi}^{\otimes k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\ket{\psi}^{\otimes i}\otimes\quantity(\ket\psi-\ket\varphi)\otimes\ket{\varphi}^{\otimes(k-i-1)}

(7.94)

such that by the triangle inequality it holds

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{f(\psi)-f(\varphi)}\leq 2k\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\norm{\ket\psi-\ket\varphi}_{2}\,.

(7.95)

Thus, the claim follows from setting the reference state
\sigma_{k}=\Ex_{U}[\rho(U)^{\otimes k}]
together with Levy’s
7.20

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity({\mb S^{N-1}}^{\odot k}, \tau)\geq\max_{O}\quantity(\max_O\Pr_{U\sim\U(N)}\qty[\abs{\tr[O\qty(\rho(U)^{\otimes k} - \sigma_k)]}>\tau])_{U\sim\mathrm{U}(N)}\quantity[\abs{\tr[O\qty(\rho(U)^{\otimes k} - \sigma_k)]}>\tau]^{-1}\geq\exp\quantity(\frac{N\tau^2}{9\pi^3k^2})\,,

(7.96)

where the
\max
is over all
O\in\mathbb{H}^{K\times K}
with
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
.
∎

7.4.2
Random Quantum Circuits: Part III

Let us now de-randomize the hardness result for learning Haar random states.

Theorem 7.21:
(Hardness of Learning
t
-Designs From
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}
)

Let
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
K=N^{k}
, let
\tau,\epsilon\in(0,1]
and let
\mu
be an additive
\epsilon
-approximate projective
2k
-design.
Then for every
O\in\mathbb{H}^{K\times K}
with
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
it holds

\displaystyle\Var_{\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon,2k}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\tr[\rho^{\otimes k}O]]\leq 8\frac{k^{2}}{N}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}+O\quantity(\frac{k^4}{N^2})\,.

(7.97)

In particular, for the query complexity of non-trivial learning from
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}
it holds

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu^{\odot k},\tau)=\begin{cases}\Omega\quantity(\frac{\tau^2 N}{k^2})&,\quad\epsilon=O\quantity(\frac{k^2}{N})\\[8.0pt]
\frac{\tau^{2}}{\epsilon}\quantity(1 - o\qty(\frac{k^2}{N\epsilon}))&,\quad\epsilon=\omega\quantity(\frac{k^2}{N})\,.\end{cases}

(7.98)

An immediate consequence of
7.21
is the following corollary.

Corollary 7.22:

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
\log k=o(n)
.
Then,
\epsilon
-learning depth

d=O(n\ln(k)^{5}k^{6})

brickwork quantum circuit states from entangled measurements and with success probability at least
1-\delta\geq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
requires, for every
\epsilon\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
and for every
n\geq 3
at least
k
copies.

Proof:
.

We apply
7.21
and show a simplified version of
7.17
. We refer to
Remark

7.4
for the direct application of
7.17
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the set of depth
d
brickwork quantum states on
n
qubits.
Note that brickwork random quantum circuits at sufficiently large depth
d
form a
2k
-design.
Let
N=2^{n}
,
K=N^{k}
and denote

\displaystyle\sigma_{\vee^{k}}=\Ex_{\rho\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\rho^{\otimes k}]=\binom{N+k-1}{k}^{-1}P_{\vee^{k}}\,.

(7.99)

Since every
\rho^{\otimes k}\in\mathcal{S}(N)^{\odot k}
is pure and because

\displaystyle\tr[\quantity(\rho^{\otimes k})\sigma_{\vee^{k}}]=\binom{N+k-1}{k}^{-1}=O(N^{-k})\,,

(7.100)

it holds that
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S}(N)^{\odot k},\sigma_{\vee^{k}})
and hence
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{Q}^{\odot k},\sigma_{\vee^{k}})
is non-trivial.

By
7.21
, solving
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{Q}^{\odot k},\sigma_{\vee^{k}})
requires strictly more than one query to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}
for
\tau\geq\sqrt{2\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
and
n
sufficiently large.

Now, for the sake of contradiction, let
\mathcal{A}
be an algorithm for
\epsilon
-learning from
k-1
copies and denote by
\quantity{\Gamma_\rho\midvert\rho\in\mc S(N)}
the corresponding POVM on
k-1
copies.
Define by
M
the following observable on
k
-copies: First measure
\Gamma
on
k-1
copies.
Given output
\rho
measure the projector
\Pi^{\mathrm{loc}}=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
onto
\rho
’s largest eigenvector on the
k
’th copy.
If the measurement returns
1
return “
\mathcal{Q}
” and else “
\quantity{\sigma_{\vee^k}}
”.

When acting on
k-1
copies of some
\varsigma\in\mathcal{Q}
the measurement
\Gamma
will measure, with probability at least
1-\delta
some
\rho
which is at most
\epsilon
far in trace distance from the true state
\varsigma
.
In this case it holds by the Fuchs-van de Graaf
Equation

6.3

\displaystyle\!\matrixelement{\psi}{\varsigma}{\psi}=1-\frac{1}{4}\norm{\varsigma-\ketbra\psi}_{\tr}^{2}\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4}\,.

(7.101)

Thus, when
M
acts on
k
copies of
\varsigma
we measure
1
with probability at least
p_{\mathcal{Q}}\geq(1-\delta)(1-(\frac{\epsilon}{2})^{2})
.

In contrast, since for the partial trace on the first
k-1
qubits
\tr_{[k-1]}[\sigma_{\vee^{k}}]=N^{-1}\mathds{1}
is the maximally mixed state, the final measurement will be
1
with probability at most
p_{\sigma}\leq\delta+\frac{1}{N}(1-\delta)
.
This implies that a single query with
M
to
\mathtt{MQStat}^{k}_{\tau}
suffices to distinguish between
\mathcal{Q}^{\odot k}
and
\sigma_{\vee^{k}}
provided
p_{\mathcal{Q}}-p_{\sigma}>2\tau
.
Setting
\tau=\nicefrac{{1}}{{10}}
this contradicts the hardness result from
7.21
, such that by
7.17
no such learning algorithm
\mathcal{A}
can exist.
∎

Remark 7.4:

Learning the best approximation with respect to an
\epsilon
-net implies an
\epsilon
-learner.
Similarly, an
\epsilon
-learner implies a learner with respect to an
\epsilon
-net with no additional queries (which may be computationally unfeasible).
Thus, we could also directly prove
7.22
by applying
7.17
to a learner with respect to an
\epsilon
-net:
The
\lceil\log{\abs{\mc Z(\mc S)}}\rceil
would then correspond to the size of the net which, by the Solovay-Kitaev theorem would induce a linear overhead for any polynomially sized circuit, which again contradicts the hardness result due to
7.21
.

Proof of

7.21
:

We first consider the case
\epsilon=0
and proceed analogous to the proof of
7.14
.
Let
K=N^{k}
, assume
\mu
is a unitary
2k
-design and let
O\in\mathbb{H}^{K\times K}
with
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
.
We write the variance as

\displaystyle\Var_{\rho\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[O\rho(U)^{\otimes k}]]=M_{2k}(O\otimes O)-M_{k}(O)^{2}

(7.102)

where we defined

\displaystyle M_{r}(A)=\tr[A\cdot\Ex_{\rho\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\rho(U)^{\otimes r}]]=\tr[A\cdot K_{\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}^{(r)}]=\binom{N+r-1}{r}^{-1}\tr[AP_{\vee^{r}}]\,,

(7.103)

and used
6.5
with
P_{\vee^{r}}
the projector onto the symmetric subspace with respect to the cyclic group on the copies of Hilbert space.

We then partition the cyclic group as
S_{2k}=S_{k}\times S_{k}\cup S_{2k}\setminus S_{k}\times S_{k}=S_{\otimes}\cup S_{\times}
where
S_{\otimes}
contains all permutations
\varsigma=(\varsigma_{1},\varsigma_{2})
that can be represented as
\Pi_{2k}(\varsigma)=\Pi_{k}(\varsigma_{1})\otimes\Pi_{k}(\varsigma_{2})
, whereas all permutations in
S_{\times}
contain crossings between the first and second
k
copies.
Then, we rewrite

\displaystyle M_{2k}(A)=\binom{N+2k-1}{2k}^{-1}(2k!)^{-1}\quantity(\tr[A\sum_{\varsigma\in S_\otimes}\Pi_{2k}(\varsigma)] +
\tr[A\sum_{\varsigma\in S_\times}\Pi_{2k}(\varsigma)])\eqcolon M_{2k}^{\otimes}(A)+M_{2k}^{\times}(A)\,.

(7.104)

We first focus on
M_{2k}^{\otimes}(O\otimes O)-M_{k}(O)^{2}
.
Denote by
(n)_{k}=\frac{n!}{(n-k)!}

and rewrite

\displaystyle\mathrm{diff}=\binom{D+2k-1}{2k}\frac{1}{(2k)!}-\quantity(\binom{D+k-1}{k}\frac{1}{k!})\frac{1}{k!}^{2}=(D+2k-1)_{2k}^{-1}-(D+k-1)_{k}^{-2}

(7.105)

\displaystyle=N^{-2k}\quantity(\qty(\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\qty(1+2\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N}))^{-1} - \qty(\prod_{i=1}^{ k}\qty(1+\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N}))^{-2})

(7.106)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2k}}\cdot\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}\quantity(1+\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N})^{2}-\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\quantity(1+2\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N})}{\prod_{i=1}^{k}\quantity(1+\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N})^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\quantity(1+2\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N})}\,.

(7.107)

For every
i\in[k]
it holds
1\leq(1+\frac{k}{N}-\frac{i}{k}\frac{k}{N})<1+\frac{k}{N}
. Similarly, for every
i\in[2k]
it holds
1\leq(1+2\frac{k}{N}-\frac{i}{k}\frac{k}{N})<1+2\frac{k}{N}
.
Thus, it holds for the denominator

\displaystyle 1\leq\prod_{i=1}^{k}\quantity(1+\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N})^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\quantity(1+2\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N})\,.

(7.108)

In order to bound the numerator first note that
1+\frac{k}{N}-\frac{i}{k}\frac{k}{N}\leq 1+2\frac{k}{N}-\frac{i}{k}\frac{k}{N}
and thus
\mathrm{diff}\leq 0
.
Then we rewrite

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{k}\quantity(1+\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N})^{2}\geq 1\,,

(7.109)

and

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\quantity(1+2\tfrac{k}{N}-\tfrac{i}{k}\tfrac{k}{N})\leq\quantity(1+2\tfrac{k}{N})^{2k}\leq 1+4\tfrac{k^{2}}{N}+O(\tfrac{k^{4}}{N^{2}})\,,

(7.110)

where we have used
\ln(1+x)\leq x
and thus

\displaystyle(1+x)^{l}=e^{l\ln(1+x)}\leq e^{lx}=1+lx+O\quantity(l^2x^2)\,.

(7.111)

Combining this yields
0\leq-\mathrm{diff}\leq 4\tfrac{k^{2}}{N^{2k+1}}+O\quantity(\tfrac{k^4}{N^{2k+2}})
,
which implies

\displaystyle 0\leq M_{k}(O)^{2}-M_{2k}^{\otimes}(O\otimes O)=-\mathrm{diff}\cdot\tr[(O\otimes O)\cdot(P_{\vee^{k}}\otimes P_{\vee^{k}})]\leq 4\tfrac{k^{2}}{N}+O\quantity(\tfrac{k^4}{N^2})\,.

(7.112)

It remains to bound
M_{2k}^{\times}(O\otimes O)
.
Note that for any
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
it holds

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{M_{2k}^\times(O\otimes O)}\leq\absolutevalue{M_{2k}^\times(\id_{2k})}\,.

(7.113)

Since
\Var_{\rho}\quantity[\tr[\rho^{\otimes k}\id_k]]=0
it thus holds

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{M_{2k}^\times(O\otimes O)}\leq\absolutevalue{M_{2k}^\times(\id_{2k})}\leq\absolutevalue{M_{2k}^\otimes(\id_{2k}) - M_k(\id_k)^2}\leq 4\tfrac{k^{2}}{N}+O\quantity(\tfrac{k^4}{N^2})\,.

(7.114)

We conclude the
\epsilon=0
case with

\displaystyle\Var_{\rho\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\tr[\rho^{\otimes k}O]]\leq 8\frac{k^{2}}{N}+O\quantity(\frac{k^4}{N^2})\,.

(7.115)

We now consider the case
\epsilon>0
.
By
6.6

we find

\displaystyle\Var_{\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon,2k}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\tr[O\rho(U)^{\otimes k}]]\leq\Var_{\rho\sim\mathbb{S}^{N-1}}\quantity[\tr[O\rho(U)^{\otimes k}]]+\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}\,.

(7.116)

The claim on
q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu^{\odot k},\tau)
then follows from expanding
(1+x)^{-1}
in the different regimes.
∎

8
Parametrized Learning Algorithms

subsection.8.1subsection.8.2subsubsection.8.2.1subsubsection.8.2.2subsubsection.8.2.3subsection.8.3subsubsection.8.3.1paragraph*.15subsubsection.8.3.2subsubsection.8.3.3subsubsection.8.3.4subsubsection.8.3.5subsubsection.8.3.6

8.1
Set-Up

We now shift our attention to what we refer to as parametrized algorithms.
Let
\Theta
be a parameter space.
In general, we assume
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
compact for some sufficiently large
m
.
Let
l:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
be a function that is minimized by some (potentially non-unique)
\vartheta_{\min}
7
7

7

The existence of such a minimizer
\vartheta_{\min}
is in general already due to the compactness of
\Theta
.
For any
l\in\mathcal{L}
there always exists a
\vartheta_{\min}
that minimizes
l
because
\Theta
is compact.
Practically however the minimum is often because
l
is the composition of a convex function and a parametrization map.

.

We refer to
l
as a
Loss Function
.
An oracle
\mathcal{O}(l)
that, when queried with some
\vartheta\in\Theta
returns the value
l(\vartheta)
, potentially up to some tolerance, is referred to as a
Loss Function Oracle
.
The task of the algorithm is then to find an estimate
\hat{\vartheta}
that satisfies an optimality criterion.
This optimality criterion is either defined with respect to the
Access Loss Function

l
,
or with respect to another loss function
l_{\mathrm{op}}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
referred to as the
Operational Loss Function
.
The access loss function encodes the access
\mathcal{A}
is given to the problem instance and the operational loss function encodes the actual task of the learning problem.
The optimality criterion can then be formalized in terms of a function
\mathrm{opt}
as

\displaystyle\vartheta\quad\text{such that}\quad l_{\mathrm{op}}(\vartheta)\leq\mathrm{opt}(\Theta,l_{\mathrm{op}},\epsilon)\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{opt}(\Theta,l_{\mathrm{op}},\epsilon)=\min_{\phi\in\Theta}l_{\mathrm{op}}(\phi)+\epsilon\,.

(8.1)

A parametrized algorithm
\mathcal{A}
then is one which can be written as an optimization routine
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}
that interacts with
\mathcal{O}(l)
and finds a
\hat{\vartheta}
that satisfies the optimality condition
Equation

8.1

\displaystyle\quantity(\A_\mrm{opt}\underset{\vartheta}{\overset{l(\vartheta)}{\circlearrowleft}} \mc O(l) )\longrightarrow\hat{\vartheta}\quad\text{with}\quad l_{\mathrm{op}}(\hat{\vartheta})\leq\mathrm{opt}(\Theta,l_{\mathrm{op}},\epsilon)\,.

(8.2)

If
\Theta
is continuous and if
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}
uses a variational optimization routine we refer to
\mathcal{A}
as a variational algorithm.

In practice, the loss function access often comes with two more properties:

L1:

The loss function is bounded
l:\Theta\to[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
.

L2:

The loss function oracle
\mathcal{O}(l)
, when queried with some
\vartheta\in\Theta
returns
l(\vartheta)
up to a tolerance
\widetilde{\tau}
.

L1
is often simply due to the fact that the numerical accuracy of any practical algorithm is bounded and, as such, the range is bounded to some compact interval to avoid numerical instabilities.
While the numerical inaccuracy also implies some inaccuracy as in
L2
, the dominant contribution to
\widetilde{\tau}
usually comes from statistical errors in the evaluation of the loss function.
Notably, if
L1
and
L2
are satisfied we can rescale the loss function to the interval
[0,1]
, such that the corresponding oracle
\mathcal{O}(l)
becomes an evaluation oracle
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(l)
for
l
with tolerance
\tau=\frac{\widetilde{\tau}}{b-a}
.

As such, we can employ the formalism from
Part

I
in order to obtain bounds on the complexity of parametrized algorithms.
We define the corresponding learning problem as follows.

Definition 8.1:
(Parametrized Learning)

Let
\epsilon>0
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a compact set referred to as the parameter space and let
\mathcal{L}
be a set of functions
l:\Theta\to[0,1]\subset\mathbb{R}
referred to as the
Set of Loss Functions
.
We refer to the elements
l\in\mathcal{L}
as
Access Loss Functions
.
For each
l\in\mathcal{L}
let
l_{\mathrm{op}}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
be the corresponding
Operational Loss Function
and let

\mathrm{opt}(\Theta,l_{\mathrm{op}},\epsilon)
define an
Optimality Criterion
as in
Equation

8.1
.
Then,
Parametrized Learning
is defined as the learning problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{P}(\Theta)::l\mapsto\quantity{\hat\vartheta\in\Theta\midvert l_\mrm{op}\qty(\hat\vartheta)\leq\mrm{opt}(\Theta, l_\mrm{op}, \epsilon)}\,.

(8.3)

Note that by the definition of the learning problem we implicitly defined a class of loss functions
\mathcal{L}
with respect to which the success of the algorithm is defined.
This is, here we apply the standard perspective from problem complexity where an algorithm is required to be successful on every problem contained in a class of instances.

As already argued in the introduction
Section

1.2.2
and further explained in
Section

8.3.1
, we only consider algorithms for which the optimization is deterministic and where any randomness is due to the evaluation of the loss function and a possible random initialization of the parameters.
By modelling stochastic evaluations by the evaluation oracle with a finite tolerance we assume that the model and its parameters are not fine-tuned to exploit the randomness due to the evaluation as in
[
AS20
]
.
Moreover, in
Section

8.3.1
we show how to analyze a random initialization in terms of deterministic bounds.

The deterministic average-case query complexity of parametrized learning
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
with respect to a measure
\mu
over
\mathcal{L}
from
\tau
accurate queries to
l
can then be lower bounded by
2.1
as

q\geq\frac{\beta-\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l_\mrm{op}(\vartheta)\leq\mrm{opt}(\Theta, l_\mrm{op}, \epsilon)]}{\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{l(\vartheta)-g(\vartheta)}>\tau]}\,,

(8.4)

where
g
is any function
g:\Theta\to[0,1]
.

In case
l=l_{\mathrm{op}}
and
\min_{\vartheta}l(\vartheta)=0
for all
l
, there is an interesting connection between the complexity of non-trivial learning and the fraction of trivial learning.
The complexity of non-trivial learning is usually bounded by Chebyshev’s inequality, setting
g(\vartheta)=\Ex_{l}[l(\vartheta)]
as

\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{l(\vartheta)-g(\vartheta)}>\tau]\leq\frac{\Var_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l(\vartheta)]}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(8.5)

Thus, if this quantity is small for all
\vartheta
, leading to a large complexity of non-trivial learning,
then
l(\vartheta)
concentrates around its mean
g(\vartheta)=\Ex_{l}[l(\vartheta)]
.
The fraction of non-trivial learning is therefore small if
g(\vartheta)
is sufficiently greater
\epsilon
for all
\vartheta
.
And conversely, if
g(\vartheta)
is in expectation over
\vartheta
sufficiently below
\epsilon
, the learning problem becomes average-case trivial.

Corollary 8.1:

Let
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
be a parametrized learning problem
with
l=l_{\mathrm{op}}
and such that for all
l\in\mathcal{L}
it holds
\min_{\vartheta}l(\vartheta)=0
.
Denote by
l_{\min}=\min_{\vartheta}\Ex_{l}[l(\vartheta)]
and by
\sigma^{2}=\max_{\vartheta}\Var_{l}[l(\vartheta)]
.
Then, if
l_{\min}>\epsilon
it holds

\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l(\vartheta) \leq\epsilon]\leq\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{l(\vartheta)-\Ex_l[l(\vartheta)]} \geq\Ex_l[l(\vartheta)]-\epsilon]\geq\Ex_{l}[l(\vartheta)]-\epsilon\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{(l_{\min}-\epsilon)^{2}}\,.

(8.6)

In particular, the query complexity for learning
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
can be lower bounded as

q\geq\sigma^{-2}\tau\cdot\quantity(\beta- \frac{\sigma^2}{(l_{\min}-\epsilon)^2})\,.

(8.7)

Similarly, for
l_{\min}\leq\Ex_{l}[l(\vartheta)]=o(\epsilon)
sufficiently small the average-case problem is trivial, and there exists a trivial algorithm with success probability at least

\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l(\vartheta)<\epsilon]\geq 1-\frac{\Ex_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l(\vartheta)]}{\epsilon}\,,

(8.8)

with respect to
l\sim\mu
.

8.2
Narrow Gorge and Barren Plateau

In this subsection we will connect the bounds we obtained from applying the formalism from
Part

I
to parametrized learning as defined in
8.1

with other complexity witnesses in learning.

Hardness of learning from loss functions is in practice often witnessed in terms of narrow gorges and barren plateaus.
While each of these phenomena focuses on different aspects of the loss function landscape,
they often come hand in had
[
AHC+22
]
.

Before going into details of narrow gorges and barren plateaus let us explain some strengths and weaknesses of the different approaches.
Our formalism as introduced in
Part

I
has the nice feature of yielding unconditional bounds. This is, the lower-bounds given hold for
any
learning algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, independent of the assumptions on
\mathcal{A}
.
However, in practice it is often either not clear, which set of loss functions
\mathcal{L}
one is actually interested in,
or those sets
\mathcal{L}
for which one can actually derive bounds on the relevant probabilities are not immediately connected to practically relevant sets at best, or way too large to realistically hope for an efficient learning algorithm, at worst.
The phenomena of barren plateau and narrow gorge thus come from a different, more practically motivated intuition.
Those phenomena consider only a single loss function
l
and analyze how generic certain properties of
l
are with respect to a probability measure
\vartheta\sim\nu
over
\Theta
.
As such, the presence of these phenomena does not per se rule out any learning algorithm, but emphasizes the need for careful initialization, updates or novel methods to stabilize the learning process with respect to a given loss function.

Simply put, a loss function admits a narrow gorge if the set of minimizers is concentrated in a subset of (exponentially) vanishing measure.
As such, the minimizers quite literally live in a “narrow gorge”.
More formally we define it as follows.

Definition 8.2:
(Narrow Gorge)

Let
n
be a scaling parameter,
l^{(n)}:\Theta^{(n)}\to\mathbb{R}
be a loss function,
\nu^{(n)}
a measure over
\Theta^{(n)}
and refer to
\Ex_{\vartheta\sim\nu^{(n)}}[l^{(n)}(\vartheta)]
as the mean loss.
We say that
l^{(n)}
admits a
Narrow Gorge
with respect to
\nu
if:

NG1:

The minimal loss
l^{(n)}(\vartheta_{\min})
is bounded away from the mean loss

\Delta(n)=\Ex_{\vartheta\sim\nu^{(n)}}\quantity[l^{(n)}(\vartheta)]-l^{(n)}(\vartheta_{\min})=\Omega\quantity(\overpoly)\,.

(8.9)

NG2:

The loss is sharply concentrated around its mean. In particular, for any
\delta>0
it holds

\Pr_{\vartheta\sim\nu^{(n)}}\quantity[\abs{l^{(n)}(\vartheta) - \Ex_{\vartheta\sim\nu^{(n)}}\qty[l^{(n)}(\vartheta)]}>\delta]>\delta\leq\frac{2^{-\Omega(n)}}{\delta^{2}}\,.

(8.10)

In particular, the valid solutions
\hat{\vartheta}
to
l
for a sufficiently small
\epsilon=\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
with
\Delta(n)-\epsilon=\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
are by
NG1
inverse polynomially bounded away from the expected loss.
Thus, the mass of this solution set with respect to
\nu
is by
NG2
exponentially small.

The similarity to the bounds as in
Equation

8.4
is immediate.

NG2
is a statement about the concentration of the loss function with respect to
\vartheta\sim\nu
just as the denominator in
Equation

8.4
,
which corresponds to the complexity of non-trivial learning,
is a statement about the concentration of the loss function with respect to
l\sim\mu
.
Similarly,
NG1
is a statement about the non-triviality of the loss function with respect to
\vartheta
,
just as the numerator in
Equation

8.4
is a statement about the non-triviality of the learning problem with respect to
l
.

8.2.1
Narrow Gorge and Hardness of Learning

Let us work out this relationship in more detail.
To begin with, let
n
be a scaling parameter and
\epsilon=\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
.
For
l\in\mathcal{L}
let
l_{\mathrm{reg}}(\vartheta)=l(\vartheta)-\min_{\phi}l(\phi)
be the regularized loss function.
Consider the case
l_{\mathrm{op}}=l_{\mathrm{reg}}
.
Set
g(\vartheta)=\Ex_{l\sim\mu}[l(\vartheta)]
and assume that for
\beta=1-\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
and any
\tau>0
the obtained lower bound from
Equation

8.4
yields
q=\nicefrac{{2^{\Omega(n)}}}{{\tau^{2}}}
.
Since the numerator in
Equation

8.4
is bounded by one, the complexity is due to the complexity of non-trivial learning as characterized by the denominator

\displaystyle\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{l(\vartheta)-\Ex_{l\sim\mu}[l(\vartheta)]}>\tau]>\tau\leq\frac{2^{-\Omega(n)}}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(8.11)

Due to the supremum this holds for any
\vartheta\in\Theta
.
As such, the expression in
Equation

8.11
can be understood as dual to
NG2
.
While
NG2
states that a given loss function
l
concentrates around its mean,

Equation

8.11
states that the loss of a given parameter
\vartheta
concentrates around its mean with respect to the ensemble of loss functions
l\sim\mu
.

Similarly, from
q=\nicefrac{{2^{\Omega(n)}}}{{\tau^{2}}}
and
\beta=1-\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
we deduce

\displaystyle\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Pr_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l_\mrm{reg}(\vartheta)\leq\epsilon]=1-\Omega\quantity(\overpoly)\,.

(8.12)

This implies that for any
\vartheta\in\Theta
there is a subset of loss functions
\mathcal{L}^{\vartheta}
of size at least
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
with respect to
\mu
such that for any
l\in\mathcal{L}^{\vartheta}
the loss is not within optimality by
\vartheta
.
The previous equation implies

\displaystyle\Ex_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l_\mrm{reg}(\vartheta)]\geq\epsilon\Omega\quantity(\overpoly)

(8.13)

and hence

\displaystyle\Ex_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l_\mrm{reg}(\vartheta)]-\min_{l}l_{\mathrm{reg}}(\vartheta)=\epsilon\Omega\quantity(\overpoly)=\Omega\quantity(\overpoly)\,.

(8.14)

Thus,
Equation

8.14
is dual to
NG1
.
In particular, where
NG1
states that the mean loss for fixed
l
is bounded away from the minimal loss with respect to
l
,
Equation

8.14
states that the mean regularized loss of
\vartheta
under
l\sim\mu
is bounded away from the minimal regularized loss of
\vartheta
.
We summarize this as follows.

Corollary 8.2:
(Hardness of Learning Implies Dual Narrow Gorge)

Let
n
be a scaling parameter,

\epsilon=\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
,
\tau>0
and let
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
be as in
8.1
with
l_{\mathrm{op}}=l_{\mathrm{reg}}=l-\min_{\phi}l(\phi)
for all
l\in\mathcal{L}
and assume that
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
is a surjective learning problem.
Denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\epsilon}
the dual learning problem and identify any
\vartheta\in\Theta
with the
Dual Loss Function

\vartheta^{\star\star}:\mathcal{L}\to[0,1]::l\mapsto\vartheta^{\star\star}(l)=l(\vartheta)\,.

(8.15)

We can interpret
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\epsilon}
as a parametrized learning problem with loss function set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}=\Theta^{\star\star}=\{\vartheta^{\star\star}\mid\vartheta\in\Theta\}\simeq\Theta
.
Let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathcal{L}
.
If the query complexity for learning
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
with probability
\beta=1-\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
over
\mu
from
\tau
accurate evaluation queries is, by
Equation

8.4
lower bounded by
q=\nicefrac{{2^{\Omega(n)}}}{{\tau^{2}}}
,
then every dual loss function
\vartheta^{\star\star}
in the dual parametrized learning problem
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\epsilon}
satisfies
NG2
and an
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
fraction of dual loss functions admits a narrow gorge.

8.2.2
Barren Plateau and Hardness of Learning

Let us now turn towards barren plateaus.
The phenomenon of a barren plateau is phrased in a similarly scenic language as the narrow gorge.
A barren plateau refers to the phenomenon when the gradient of the loss function is concentrated around zero.
This is much in spirit with the vanishing gradient problem and the shattered gradient problem in deep learning
[
BFL+17
]
.
If the loss landscape is dominated by a flat plateau, or more generally multiple flat plateaus optimization is hard for any gradient based algorithm.
Due to the flatness any such algorithm, once inside such a plateau will in general not be able to exit the plateau without a more violent intervention.
We use the following definition to formalize barren plateaus.

Definition 8.3:
(Barren Plateau)

Let
n
be a scaling parameter,
\vartheta\in\Theta^{(n)}
be a vector of
m=O(\mathrm{poly}(n))
continuous variables,
l^{(n)}:\Theta^{(n)}\to\mathbb{R}
be a loss function, let
\nu^{(n)}
be a measure over
\Theta^{(n)}
and for
i\in[m]
denote by
\partial_{i}l
the partial derivative of
l
with respect to the
i
’th component of
\vartheta
.
We say that
l^{(n)}
admits a
Barren Plateau
with respect to
\nu
if:

BP:

Any partial derivative of the loss function is concentrated around zero. In particular, for any
i\in[m]
and any
\tau>0
it holds

\Pr_{\vartheta\sim\nu}\quantity[\abs{\partial_i l(\vartheta)}>\tau]=\frac{2^{-\Omega(n)}}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(8.16)

Interestingly, it was shown in
[
AHC+22
]
that for most variational quantum-classical algorithms the two phenomena, the barren plateau and the narrow gorge, do coincide.
In particular, they have shown that
NG2
implies
BP
and vice versa.
While their proof used slightly different definitions of the two phenomena the logic, applied to our definitions, is identical.
Moreover, we would like to point out that their result can be easily extended to an even broader class of loss functions, such as QAOA
by applying the results from
[
WIW+22
]
in the proof of
[
AHC+22
, Lemma 2]
.
As such, we can directly re-use the discussion from
Section

8.2.1
and arrive at the following observation.

Observation 8.1:
(Hardness of Learning Implies a Barren Plateau)

Let
n,\epsilon,\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\epsilon}
be as in
8.2
and make the same identification between
\vartheta
and
\vartheta^{\star\star}
.
Let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathcal{L}
and assume that, for any
\tau>0
, the query complexity of non-trivial learning
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
with respect to
\mu
is lower bounded by the inverse of
Equation

8.11
.
If all
l\in\mathcal{L}
can be written as a (sum of) quantum loss function, for which the gradient can be evaluated by the generalized parameter shift rule from polynomially many circuit evaluations as in
[
WIW+22
]
,
then every dual loss function
\vartheta^{\star\star}
admits a barren plateau.

8.2.3
Self Learning

The previous sections have shed light on the connection between the hardness bounds as introduced in this work,
and practically relevant phenomena such as the barren plateau and the narrow gorge.
In particular, we found that hardness due to our bounds often directly implies a narrow gorge in many instances and, for most quantum loss functions, a barren plateau
in any instance of the dual problem.
This motivates the study of learning problems that are self-dual as defined in
2.2
.
A natural example of this is what we define as
Self-Learning
.

Definition 8.4:
(Self-Learning)

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a class of objects endowed with a metric
\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{M}}
.
The radius of
\mathcal{M}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{M}}
is given by

\displaystyle R=\frac{1}{2}\max_{o,w\in\mathcal{M}}\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{M}}(o,w)\,,

and we assume that

\displaystyle L:[0,2R]\to[0,1]

(8.17)

is a monotonous function.
For
m\in\mathbb{N}
let
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
and let

\displaystyle\Pi:\Theta\to\mathcal{M}::\vartheta\mapsto\Pi(\vartheta)=o_{\vartheta}

(8.18)

be a surjective map referred to as the parametrization of
\mathcal{M}
.
To every
\phi\in\Theta
we assign the loss function

\displaystyle l^{\phi}:\Theta\to[0,1]::\vartheta\mapsto l^{\phi}(\vartheta)=L\circ\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{M}}\quantity(\Pi(\vartheta),\Pi(\phi))\,.

(8.19)

By

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(\Theta,\Pi,L,\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{M}})=\quantity{l^\phi\midvert\phi\in\Theta}

(8.20)

we reffer to the corresponding set of loss functions.
The parametrized learning problem
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
with parameter space
\Theta
corresponding to
\mathcal{L}
with
l_{\mathrm{op}}=l
as defined in
8.1
is then referred to as
Self-Learning
.

It is immediate to see that self-learning is self-dual:
To see this, note that, by the symmetry of the metric
\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{M}}
, for any
\vartheta\in\Theta
it holds
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(\vartheta)=\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}_{\vartheta}
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(\vartheta)=\quantity{\phi\in\Theta\midvert L\qty(\met_\mc M(o_\phi, o_\vartheta))\leq\epsilon}=\quantity{\phi\in\Theta\midvert L\qty(\met_\mc M(o_\vartheta, o_\phi))\leq\epsilon}=\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}_{\vartheta}\,.

(8.21)

Thus, the dual learning problem
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\epsilon}
is, in fact, equal to
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
.
This implies, by
Sections

8.2.1
and
8.2.2
,
that the bound as in
Equation

8.4
has direct implications on the existence of a narrow gorge and often also a barren plateau in every instance of
\mathcal{L}
.

8.3
Examples

In this section we will exemplify our formalism when applied to parametrized learning problems.

8.3.1
Working with Random Initialization
