As already stated, the results considered where all in terms of deterministic algorithms.
While most practically used routines for the parameter optimization are deterministic, randomness often is still present in terms of random initialization and in terms of stochastic evaluation of the loss function.
We assume that common algorithms are not fine-tuned to be able to exploit the stochastic evaluation in the sense of
[
AS20
]
.
To be precise, we assume that the tolerance in the evaluation oracle sufficiently models stochastic evaluations.
On the other hand, as we will show in this section, random initialization only has a mild effect on our bounds.

We model a random initialization as follows.
First, we assume that the optimizer is independent of the random initialization. That is, the optimizer does depend on the random initial value only through the results to the queries made.
For a loss function class
\mathcal{L}
with elements
l:\Phi\times\Theta\to[0,1]
we assume that
\phi\sim\nu
is a random assignment for the first variable
\phi\in\Phi
.
A measure
\mu
on
\mathcal{L}
hence induces a measure

\mu^{\phi}
on
\mathcal{L}^{\phi}=\quantity{l(\phi,\cdotspace)\midvert l\in\mc L}
.
If an algorithm, that initializes
\phi\sim\nu
can solve
\mathcal{L}^{\phi}
query efficiently with probability
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
over
\nu
, then the algorithm can be boosted to solve it with overwhelming probability by running
r=O(n)
independent runs with polynomially many queries and returning the best
\vartheta
obtained from these runs.
In order to show hardness one therefore needs to show that the probability of successfully solving
\mathcal{L}^{\phi}
with respect to
\nu
is negligible.

The same holds for the average-case setting with respect to a measure
\mu
over
\mathcal{L}
.
In order to show hardness of learning a
\beta
fraction of
\mathcal{L}
it suffices to show that the probability of learning a
\beta
fraction of
\mathcal{L}^{\phi}
with respect to
\mu^{\phi}
is negligible with respect to
\nu
.
In particular, for every polynomial
q
it needs to hold

\displaystyle\Pr_{\phi\sim\nu}\quantity[\qty(\beta-\triv[\mc Z_\phi^\epsilon,\tau])\qnt[\mu^\phi,\tau]\leq q]=\mathtt{neg}(n)

(8.22)

where
n
is the scaling parameter,
\mathtt{neg}(n)
is a negligible function in
n
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{\epsilon}
denotes the parametrized learning problem induced by
\mathcal{L}^{\phi}
for some
\epsilon>0
.

We first make the following assumption.

Assumption 1:

For every
l\in\mathcal{L}
it holds that for every
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi
and every
\vartheta\in\Theta
there exists a
\vartheta^{\prime}\in\Theta
such that
l(\phi,\vartheta)=l(\phi^{\prime},\vartheta^{\prime})
.

This is a natural assumption in many settings.
It holds for example if
\Theta=\Phi=[0,2\pi)^{m}
and
l:\Phi\times\Theta\to[0,1]
is defined by
l(\phi,\vartheta)=l^{\prime}(\phi+\vartheta\mod 2\pi)
with
l^{\prime}:\Theta\to[0,1]
.

1
implies that

\displaystyle\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z_\phi^\epsilon)=\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z_{\phi'}^\epsilon)=\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z^\epsilon)

(8.23)

is independent of
\phi
.

Equation

8.22
can then be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Pr_{\phi\sim\nu}\quantity[\qnt[\mu^\phi,\tau]\leq q \qty(\beta-\triv[\mc Z^\epsilon,\tau])^{-1}]=\mathtt{neg}(n)\,.

(8.24)

One way to bound this probability is as follows.
Using the variance bound on
q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu^\phi,\tau)
from
2.5

together with Markov’s inequality we then find that it suffices to show

\displaystyle q\frac{\Ex_{\phi\sim\nu}\max_{\vartheta}\quantity[\max_\vartheta\Var_{l\sim\mu}\qty[l(\phi,\vartheta)]]_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l(\phi,\vartheta)]}{\tau^{2}\quantity(\beta-\triv[\mc Z^\epsilon,\tau])}\leq\mathtt{neg}(n)\,,

(8.25)

in order to prove
Equation

8.22
.

We now extend this analysis to the general case, in which
1
does not necessarily hold.
This is the case if, for example, the random initialization fixes a subset of parameters which effectively only allows the algorithm to discover a strict subset of parameters which depends on
\phi\sim\nu
.
We make the weaker assumption:

Assumption 2:

Let
t
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\Pr_{\phi\sim\nu}\quantity[\triv[\mc Z_\phi^\epsilon]>t]\leq\delta\,.

(8.26)

This intuitively states that the trivial fraction is, with probability at least
1-\delta
over
\phi\sim\nu
is upper bounded by
t
.

Under
2
we can almost repeat the previous reasoning.
The left-hand side (LHS) of
Equation

8.22
can be upper-bounded by

\displaystyle\text{LHS}\leq(1-\delta)\Pr_{\phi\sim\nu}\quantity[\qty(\beta-t)\qnt[\mu^\phi,\tau]\leq q]+\delta\leq\frac{q(1-\delta)}{\beta-t}\Ex_{\phi\sim\nu}\max_{\vartheta}\quantity[\max_\vartheta\Var_{l\sim\nu}\qty[l(\phi,\vartheta)]]_{l\sim\nu}\quantity[l(\phi,\vartheta)]+\delta\,.

(8.27)

Thus, hardness is again implied for a sufficiently small expected variance and
\beta>t
,
\delta\leq\mathtt{neg}(n)
.

Remark 8.1:

To summarize this we remark, that the query complexity of deterministic algorithms with random initializations crucially depends on the expected variance

\displaystyle\Ex_{\phi\sim\nu}\max_{\vartheta}\quantity[\max_\vartheta\Var_{l\sim\mu}\qty[l(\phi,\vartheta)]]_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[l(\phi,\vartheta)]=\max_{\vartheta}\Ex_{l\sim\mu}\quantity[\Ex_{\phi\sim\nu}\qty[l(\phi,\vartheta)^2]]_{\phi\sim\nu}\quantity[l(\phi,\vartheta)^2]-\Ex_{l,l^{\prime}\sim\mu}\quantity[\Ex_{\phi\sim\nu}[l(\phi,\vartheta)l'(\phi,\vartheta)]]_{\phi\sim\nu}[l(\phi,\vartheta)l^{\prime}(\phi,\vartheta)]\,,

(8.28)

and the “randomized fraction of trivial learning” which, for
t\in(0,1)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathtt{rtriv}\quantity(\mc Z^\epsilon,\mu,\nu,t)=\Pr_{\phi\sim\nu}\quantity[\triv[\mc Z^\epsilon_\phi,\mu^\phi]>t]\,.

(8.29)

In particular, a simple bound on the query complexity for average-case learning by deterministic algorithms with random initialization and
\mathtt{rtriv}
sufficiently bounded away from
\beta
is given by

\displaystyle q=\Omega\tau^{2}\min_{\vartheta,l,l^{\prime}}\quantity(\tau^2 \min_{\vartheta, l, l'}\qty(\Ex_{\phi\sim\nu}\qty[l(\phi,\vartheta)(l(\phi-\vartheta) - l'(\phi-\vartheta))]^{-1}))\,.

(8.30)

Example:

(Random Initialized VQEs) Let us consider the following popular example related to the typical incarnation of barren plateaus
[
MBS+18
]
.
In variational quantum eigensolvers (VQE) the aim is to find a state
\rho(\vartheta)
parametrized by
\vartheta\in\Theta=[0,2\pi)^{m}
that minimizes the energy of a Hamiltonian.
We write
\rho(\phi,\vartheta)=\rho(\phi+\vartheta\mod 2\pi)
and assume that
\rho(\phi,\vartheta)
forms a
2
-deisng for
\phi\sim\nu
and for all
\vartheta
.
Note, that
1
holds.
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
N=2^{n}
and
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
be a class of traceless bounded Hamiltonians
\norm{H}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq c
.
The corresponding set of loss functions consists of functions

\displaystyle l_{H}:\Theta\times\Theta\to[0,1]::(\phi,\vartheta)\mapsto\frac{1}{c}\tr[\rho(\phi,\vartheta)H]+\frac{1}{2}\,.

(8.31)

In order to lower bound the query complexity with respect to random initialization
\phi\sim\nu
and some measure
\mu
over
\mathcal{H}
we upper-bound for every
\vartheta\in\Theta

\displaystyle\Ex_{\phi\sim\nu}\quantity[\Var_{H\sim\mu}\qty[l(\phi,\vartheta)-\frac 12]]_{H\sim\mu}\quantity[l(\phi,\vartheta)-\frac 12]=\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Ex_{\phi\sim\nu}\quantity[
\Ex_{H\sim\mu}\qty[
\tr[\rho(\phi,\vartheta)H]^2
]
-
\Ex_{H\sim\mu}\qty[
\tr[\rho(\phi,\vartheta)H]
]^2
]_{H\sim\mu}\quantity[
\tr[\rho(\phi,\vartheta)H]^2
]-\Ex_{H\sim\mu}\quantity[
\tr[\rho(\phi,\vartheta)H]
]^{2}

(8.32)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Ex_{H\sim\mu}\quantity[
\Ex_{\phi\sim\nu}\qty[
\tr[\rho(\phi,\vartheta)H]^2
]
]_{\phi\sim\nu}\quantity[
\tr[\rho(\phi,\vartheta)H]^2
]=\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Ex_{H\sim\mu}\quantity[
\frac{\tr[H]^2+\tr[H^2]}{N(N+1)}
]\leq\frac{1}{N+1}\,,

(8.33)

where the last inequality is due to the tracelessness and the boundedness of
H
.

Remark 8.2:

Since the above derivation did not impose any assumption on
\mathcal{H}
but tracelessness, it follows immediately that learning must be hard in this model.
To see this, note that learning
\mathcal{H}=\quantity{H_1,H_2}
for traceless Hamiltonians
H_{1/2}
is exponentially hard, even if
H_{1/2}
are very simple Hamiltonians with a non-zero trivial fraction.
The latter is implied for example if
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
have well separated ground states and which can be approximated sufficiently well by the parametrization.
This type of hardness result is the typical implication due to barren plateaus and narrow gorges: Optimization with a random initialization due to the measure measuring the barren plateau must be hard.

8.3.2
Learning Basis States and Pauli Operators

As a warm-up we now show that self-learning computational basis states is hard.
To this end, let

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{(n)}=\quantity{\rho\midvert\rho=\ketbra z\,,\; z\in\mb F_2^n}\,,

(8.34)

be the set of computational basis states and let
\mathcal{B}
be endowed with the trace distance
\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(\rho,\sigma)=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\rho-\sigma}_{\tr}
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the corresponding self-learning problem for
\mathcal{B}
with
L
and
\Pi
defined as

\displaystyle L:[0,1]\to[0,1]::x\mapsto 1-\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\,,

and

\displaystyle\Pi:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}::z\mapsto\outerproduct{z}{z}\,.

(8.35)

Theorem 8.3:
(Self-Learning Basis States)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon\in[0,1),\beta>0,\tau\in[0,1]
and let
\mu
be the uniform measure over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
denoted as
z\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
.
Then, self-learning a
\beta
-fraction of
\mathcal{B}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{\tr}
and
L
as in
Equation

8.35

requires at least
(\beta-2^{-n})2^{n}
many queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(l^{z})
with
l^{z}=L\circ\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(\,\cdot\,,\outerproduct{z}{z})\in\mathcal{L}
the unknown loss function.
In particular, every loss function
l^{z}
, with
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
admits a narrow gorge.

Remark 8.3:

The problem of learning basis states from loss function queries is equivalent to the problem of finding the ground state of the Hamiltonian
H=\mathds{1}-\outerproduct{z}{z}
from “energy queries”
E(y)=\!\matrixelement{y}{H}{y}=1-\delta_{xy}
.
This problem is about as trivial as it can be when given a proper description of the problem in terms of the bit string
z
.
However, when accessing the problem instance only through a poorly chosen loss function, the problem renders information theoretically as complex as it can be.
As such, this basic theorem highlights the care that has to be taken in the choice of loss function.

Proof of

8.3
:

We begin with the following observation.
By the Fuchs-van de Graaf equality it holds for any
\rho=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi},\sigma=\outerproduct{\phi}{\phi}
in terms of the fidelity
F(\rho,\sigma)=\tr[\sqrt{\sqrt{\rho}\sigma\sqrt{\rho}}]^{2}

\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(\rho,\sigma)=\sqrt{1-F(\rho,\sigma)}=\sqrt{1-\absolutevalue{\braket{\psi}{\phi}}^{2}}\,.

(8.36)

Thus, since
\mathcal{B}
only contains pure state we find

L\circ\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(\rho,\sigma)=1-\absolutevalue{\braket{\psi}{\phi}}\,.

(8.37)

We now analyze the probability of trivial learning
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z^\epsilon,\mu)
.
For every
z,y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
it holds
\innerproduct{z}{y}=\delta_{zy}
.
Hence, for every
0\leq\epsilon<1
and every
y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
we find

\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z^\epsilon,\mu)=\sup_{y}\Pr_{z\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\quantity[l^z(y)<\epsilon]=\Pr_{z\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\quantity[\smash{\underbrace{\abs{\braket{z}{y}}}_{=\delta_{zy}}}>1-\epsilon]=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\,.

(8.38)

Next, we analyze the complexity of non-trivial learning
q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)
.
For every
y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
and
\tau\in[0,1]
it holds similar as above that

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)^{-1}=\max_{x}\Pr_{z\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\quantity[\abs{\braket{z}{x}}>\tau]=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\,,

(8.39)

where we have set
g(z)=0
for all
z
.

Thus, learning a
\beta
fraction of
\mathcal{B}
with respect to the uniform measure requires by
Equation

8.4
at least

\displaystyle q\geq(\beta-\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z^\epsilon,\mu))q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)=(\beta-2^{-n})2^{n}

(8.40)

queries.

The remainder follows due to
8.2
.
∎

Leaving the realm of self-learning we can also extend the prior result to more general access to the loss function.

Corollary 8.4:
(Learning Basis States)

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
with
1-\epsilon=\Omega(1)
,
\tau>0
,
\beta=2^{-o(n)}
,
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
for some
m>0
, let
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{S}(2^{n})
be a set of
n
-qubit quantum states and let
\Pi:\Theta\to\mathcal{Q}
be a parametrization of
\mathcal{Q}
.
Let
L
as before and denote by
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
the parametrized learning problem over

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{(n)}=\quantity{l:\Theta\to[0,1]\doublecolon\vartheta\mapsto l(\vartheta)\midvert l(\vartheta)=l^z(\vartheta)=L\circ\met_{\tr}(\Pi(\vartheta), \ketbra z)}\,,

(8.41)

with
l_{\mathrm{op}}=l
.
Then, learning
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
requires at least
\tau^{2}2^{\Omega(n)}
many
\tau
-accurate evaluation queries of the unknown loss function
l^{z}
.

Proof:
.

The proof is similar to that of
8.3
.
Note that for any
\vartheta

\Pr_{z\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\quantity[\abs{\braket{\psi(\vartheta)}{z}}>\tau]\leq\frac{\Var_{z\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}[\innerproduct{\psi(\vartheta)}{z}]}{\tau^{2}}\leq\frac{\Ex_{z\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}[\innerproduct{\psi(\vartheta)}{z}\innerproduct{z}{\psi(\vartheta)}]}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(8.42)

The second moment can then be bounded as

\displaystyle\Ex_{z\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\quantity[\braket{\psi(\vartheta)}{z}\braket{z}{\psi(\vartheta)}]=\sum_{x,y,z}\frac{\overline{c}_{x}c_{y}}{2^{n}}\delta_{xz}\delta_{zy}=\sum_{z}\frac{\absolutevalue{c_z}^{2}}{2^{n}}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\,,

(8.43)

which yields
\Pr_{z}[\absolutevalue{\braket{\psi(\vartheta)}{x}}>\tau]\leq 2^{-n}\tau^{-2}
.

In fact, we can use the same bound for the fraction of non-trivial learning to obtain

\Pr_{z\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}[\absolutevalue{\braket{\psi(\vartheta)}{x}}>1-\epsilon]\leq\frac{2^{-n}}{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}\,.

(8.44)

Thus, at least

q\geq 2^{n}\tau^{2}\quantity(\beta- \frac{2^{-n}}{1-\epsilon})=\tau^{2}2^{\Omega(n)}

(8.45)

queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(l^{z})
are necessary to learn the unknown
z
.
∎

Note that we can identify the basis state projector in terms of the Pauli
Z
operator as

\displaystyle\outerproduct{z}{z}=\bigotimes_{i}\outerproduct{z_i}{z_i}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}(\mathds{1}+(-1)^{z_{i}}Z)\,.

(8.46)

This motivates another reformulation of the above results in terms of Pauli operators which is tightly related to Cerezo et al.’s work on barren plateaus and global cost functions
[
CSV+21
]
.

Theorem 8.5:
(Hardness of Learning Global Cost Functions)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
\mathcal{G}
the set of length
n
Pauli strings.
For any parametrization of
n
-qubit quantum states
\ket{\psi}
and every
P\in\mathcal{G}
let
l^{P}(\psi)=\frac{1}{2}(\!\expectationvalue{P}{\psi}+1)
be the associated loss function,
\mathcal{L}=\quantity{l^P\midvert P\in\mc G}
and denote by
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
the corresponding parametrized learning problem with
l=l_{\mathrm{op}}
.
We assume that for every
P\in\mathcal{G}
there exists a state in the parametrization such that
\!\expectationvalue{P}{\psi}=-\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})

Then, learning any
\beta
fraction of
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
with respect to the uniform distribution over
\mathcal{G}
requires at least
q\geq\beta\tau^{2}2^{n+1}
many queries.

Note that the assumption on the parametrization is automatically fulfilled if the parametrization has an
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
fidelity with the states
\ket{z}
with
z\in\{0,1,+,-,i,-i\}^{\times n}
.

Proof:
.

For any
x=(a,b)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}
represent the corresponding Pauli string in its Weyl representation

\displaystyle W_{x}=i^{a\cdot b}X^{a_{1}}Z^{b_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{a_{n}}Z^{b_{n}}\,.

(8.47)

Note, that

\displaystyle\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}=2^{-n}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}}\!\expectationvalue{W_x}{\psi}W_{x}\,,

(8.48)

and hence

\displaystyle 1=\tr[\ket{\psi}\!\!\innerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\!\!\bra{\psi}]=2^{-2n}\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}}\!\expectationvalue{W_x}{\psi}\!\!\!\expectationvalue{W_y}{\psi}\tr[W_{x}W_{y}]=2^{-n}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}}\!\expectationvalue{W_x}{\psi}^{2}\,.

(8.49)

This implies

\displaystyle\Var_{P\sim\mathcal{G}}\quantity[\ev{P}{\psi}]\leq\Ex_{P\sim\mathcal{G}}\quantity[\ev{P}{\psi}^2]=\Ex_{x\sim\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}}\quantity[\ev{W_x}{\psi}^2]=2^{-2n}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}}\!\expectationvalue{W_x}{\psi}^{2}=2^{-n}\,.

(8.50)

In particular, for every
\ket{\psi}
the loss
l^{P}(\psi)=\frac{1}{2}(\!\expectationvalue{P}{\psi}+1)
concentrates exponentially close to
\frac{1}{2}
for
P\sim\mathcal{G}
with
\Var_{P}[l^{P}(\psi)]\leq 2^{n+1}
.
This directly implies that
q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mc G,\tau)\geq\tau^{2}2^{n}
.
Moreover, it follows that
\mathtt{triv}\quantity(\mc Z^\epsilon,\mc G)=O(2^{-n})
for every
\epsilon=\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
by the assumption implies that the parametrization contains for every
P
a state with loss inverse polynomially smaller than
\frac{1}{2}
.
Thus, the claim follows.
∎

Remark 8.4:

Thinking about
\!\expectationvalue{P}{\psi}=\tr[P\rho]=\left\langle P,\rho\right\rangle_{HS}
in terms of the Hilbert Schmidt inner product, noting that the Pauli strings form a basis for Hermitian operators, makes the connection to learning basis states even more apparent.
The hardness of learning basis states holds for computational basis states and even any exponentially sized subset thereof.
In contrast, for Pauli strings, it is crucial to consider
\mathds{1},Z
as well as the non-diagonal
X
and
Y
operators to show hardness of learning.
This can be easily seen by observing that any
\mathds{1},Z
string can be directly read out from computational basis states.
Similarly, below, we show the less obvious insight that Pauli strings composed of
Z
and
X
operators only can be learned efficiently,
which implies that non-commutativity of the local Pauli variables alone is no sufficient condition for hardness of learning.

Theorem 8.6:
(Learning
ZX
-Pauli Strings)

Let
\mathcal{G}_{ZX}=\quantity{Z,X}^{\otimes n}
be the set containing all length
n
strings composed of
Z
and
X
operators only and let
\ket{\psi}
be a parametrization that contains the set of stabilizer states.
Then, there is an algorithm for learning an unknown
P\in\mathcal{G}_{ZX}
from
n+1
evaluation queries to
l^{P}(\psi)=\frac{1}{2}(\!\expectationvalue{P}{\psi}+1)
with tolerance
\tau\leq\frac{1}{5}
.

Proof:
.

For any
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
denote

\displaystyle P_{x}=A_{1}\otimes A_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{ZX}\quad\text{with}\quad A_{i}=\begin{cases}Z,&x_{i}=0\,,\\
X,&x_{i}=1\,.\end{cases}

(8.51)

To begin with, let
\ket{S}
be the stabilizer state corresponding to the generating set (omitting identity tensor factors)

\displaystyle S=\quantity{Z_1\otimes Z_2\otimes\cdots\otimes Z_n, Y_1\otimes Y_2,\dots, Y_{n-1}\otimes Y_n}\,.

(8.52)

We then find that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\ev{P_x}{S}}=\begin{cases}1,&\absolutevalue{x}=0\,,\\
0,&\absolutevalue{x}=1\,,\end{cases}

(8.53)

where
\absolutevalue{x}
denotes the parity of
x
.
We now define the generating sets

\displaystyle S_{i}^{e}

\displaystyle=\quantity{Z_1\otimes Z_2\otimes\cdots\otimes Z_n, Y_1\otimes Y_2,\dots, Y_{i-1}\otimes X_i, X_i\otimes Y_{i+1}, Y_{i+1}\otimes Y_{i+2},\dots,Y_{n-1}\otimes Y_n}\,,

\displaystyle S_{i}^{o}

\displaystyle=\quantity{Z_1\otimes\cdots\otimes Z_i\otimes X_{i+1}\otimes Z_{i+2}\otimes\cdots\otimes Z_n, Y_1\otimes Y_2,\dots, Y_{i-1}\otimes X_i, X_i\otimes Y_{i+1}, Y_{i+1}\otimes Y_{i+2},\dots,Y_{n-1}\otimes Y_n}\,.

Using
[X\otimes Y,X\otimes A]\neq[X\otimes Y,Z\otimes A]=0
for
A\in Z,X
, we find for
\absolutevalue{x}=0
that it holds

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\ev{P_x}{S_i^e}}=\begin{cases}0,&x_{i}=1\,,\\
1,&x_{i}=0\,.\end{cases}

(8.54)

Similarly, for
\absolutevalue{x}=1
we find

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\ev{P_x}{S_i^o}}=\begin{cases}0,&x_{i}=1\,,\\
1,&x_{i}=0\,.\end{cases}

(8.55)

Thus, the algorithm first queries
l^{P}(S)
and then
l^{P}(S_{i}^{a})
for
i\in[n]
and with
a=e,o
depending on the output of the first query.
The tolerance is sufficiently fine because each exact evaluation is either
0,\frac{1}{2}
or
1
, such that at worst we need to distinguish the case
0.3\leq v\leq 0.7
from
v\leq 0.2\lor 0.8\leq v
.
∎

8.3.3
Parametrized Quantum States

Let us now use the formalism from this work to derive a simple proof of hardness of self-learning for parametrized quantum states.
While worst-case hardness is implied by hardness of learning basis states, this section shows average-case hardness.
More, since parametrized quantum circuits are a continuous class that usually admits a parameter shift rule, hardness of self-learning is directly connected to the existence of a barren plateau.

Definition 8.5:
(Quantum Circuit Architecture)

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
A
k
-local
Quantum Circuit Architecture
(QCA)
is a family of maps that, for every system size
n
and depth
d
maps from a parameter space
\Theta
to the space of
n
-qubit unitaries that can be decomposed as a depth
d
quantum circuits composed of
k
-local gates.
In particular, the QCA is defined in terms of, for every
n
and
d
, a parametrization map

U:\Theta\to\mathrm{U}\quantity(2^n)\,.

(8.56)

Using the
Sections

8.2.1
and
8.2.2
we then reproduce as a corollary the main result of
[
MBS+18
]
.
For any
n,k
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
k
-local quantum circuit architecture with parametrization
U:\Theta\to\mathrm{U}(n)
as defined in
8.5
let

\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{QCA}}^{(n)}=\quantity{\rho\midvert\rho=\ketbra{ \psi(\vartheta)}{ \psi(\vartheta)}\,,\; \ket\psi(\vartheta)=U(\vartheta)\ket 0\,,\; \vartheta\in\Theta}

(8.57)

be the set of
n
-qubit states with respect to the architecture
\mathrm{QCA}
.
We endow
\mathcal{Q}
with the trace distance
\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(\rho,\sigma)=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\rho-\sigma}_{\tr}
and
denote by
\ket{\psi(\cdotspace)}:\Theta\to\mathcal{Q}
the parametrization on
\mathcal{Q}
induced by
U
.
Then, let
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
be the corresponding self-learning problem with
L
defined as

L:[0,1]\to[0,1]::x\mapsto 1-\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\,.

(8.58)

Then it holds.

Theorem 8.7:

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
with
1-\epsilon=\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
,
\tau>0
,
\beta=2^{-o(n)}
,
\mu
be a measure over
\Theta
and assume that
\mu
and
U
are such that
\ket{\psi(\vartheta)}
with
\vartheta\sim\mu
forms a complex spherical
(1,1)
-design.
Then, self-learning a
\beta
fraction of
\mathcal{Q}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{\tr}
and
L
requires at least
\Omega(\tau^{2}2^{n})
many
\tau
-accurate evaluation queries.
In particular, for an
\Omega(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
fraction of
\phi\in\Theta
with respect to
\mu
it holds that the loss function
l^{\phi}=L\circ\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(U^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,),U^{\prime}(\phi))
does admit a narrow gorge.
Further, if
\mathrm{QCA}
admits an efficient parameter shift rule, then
l^{\phi}
admits a barren plateau for every
\phi\in\Theta
.

As the proof will use
(1,0)
- and
(1,1)
-moments only, we remark that the above claim immediately carries over to additive
\delta
-approxiamte spherical
(1,1)
-designs when
\delta=O(2^{-n})
.
Moreover, we remark that most common quantum circuit architectures form a
(1,1)
-design already at depth
d=1
.

Proof:
.

Recall the first observation in the proof of
8.3

L\circ\mathrm{d}_{\tr}(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi},\outerproduct{\phi}{\phi})=1-\absolutevalue{\braket{\psi}{\phi}}

(8.59)

As such, by
Equations

8.4
,
8.2
and
8.1
it suffices to find good upper bounds for these two quantities:

•

The fraction of trivial learning

\max_{\vartheta}\Pr_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{\braket{\psi(\phi)}{\psi(\vartheta)}}>1-\epsilon]\,.

(8.60)

•

The complexity of non-trivial learning

\max_{\vartheta}\Pr_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{\braket{\psi(\phi)}{\psi(\vartheta)}}>\tau]\,,

(8.61)

where we have set
g=1
.

Note that, for the first claim, it suffices to prove

\max_{\vartheta}\Pr_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{\braket{\psi(\phi)}{\psi(\vartheta)}}>\tau]\leq\frac{2^{-n}}{\tau^{2}}\,,

(8.62)

since
Equation

8.62
directly implies

\max_{\vartheta}\Pr_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{\braket{\psi(\phi)}{\psi(\vartheta)}}>1-\epsilon]\leq\frac{2^{-n}}{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}\,,

(8.63)

which then, by
Equation

8.4
, yields the first claim

q\geq 2^{n}\tau^{2}\quantity(2^{-o(n)} - \frac{2^{-n}}{(1-\epsilon)^2})=\Omega\quantity(\tau^2 2^n)\,.

(8.64)

We now prove
Equation

8.62
. Let
\vartheta\in\Theta
arbitrary.
Then, by Chebyshev’s inequality

\Pr_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{\braket{\psi(\phi)}{\psi(\vartheta)}}>\tau]\leq\frac{\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}[\innerproduct{\psi(\phi)}{\psi(\vartheta)}]}{\tau^{2}}=\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\quantity(M_2-\abs{M_1}^2)\,,

(8.65)

where we introduce the first and second moments

\displaystyle M_{1}=\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}[\innerproduct{\psi(\phi)}{\psi(\vartheta)}]

and

\displaystyle M_{2}=\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}[\innerproduct{\psi(\phi)}{\psi(\vartheta)}\innerproduct{\psi(\vartheta)}{\psi(\phi)}]\,.

(8.66)

By the
(1,1)
-design assumption we can easily bound these using the corresponding Haar moments
(see
6.5
and text below)
and obtain

\displaystyle M_{1}=\Ex_{\psi\sim\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\quantity[\bra{\psi}]\ket{\chi}=0\,,

\displaystyle M_{2}=\bra{\chi}\Ex_{\psi\sim\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\quantity[\ketbra{\psi}]\ket{\chi}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\innerproduct{\chi}{\chi}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\,,

(8.67)

where we have set
\ket{\chi}=\ket{\psi(\vartheta)}
.
We plug this into
Equation

8.65
and obtain

\Pr_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\abs{\braket{\psi(\phi)}{\psi(\vartheta)}}>\tau]\leq\frac{1}{\tau^{2}2^{n}}\,.

(8.68)

The claim on the narrow gorge and the barren plateau then follows immediately from
8.2
and
8.1
.
∎

8.3.4
Linear and Data Re-Uploading QML Models

Let us now turn towards self-learning of variational quantum machine learning (vQML) models.
We will consider a very generic architecture that contains most current vQML models, in particular data re-uploading models.
For a detailed discussion of current vQML models, we refer to e.g.
[
JFN+22
]
.

Let
n,D,k,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
k>1
, let
\ket{\psi_0}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\ket{\psi_{0i}}
be an
n
-qubit tensor product state and
O
an
n
-qubit observable with operator norm
\norm{O}_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
,
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{m\cdot D\cdot\lfloor\nicefrac{{n}}{{k}}\rfloor}
a compact set and let
\mathcal{X}
be some set.

\mathcal{X}
deals as the data set, so for example
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{b}
for some
b
if we consider functions over length
b
bit strings,
or,
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
in case of real functions.
Consider the vQML architecture represented by the following function class

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{O,U,W,\psi_{0}}^{(n,D)}=\quantity{f:\mc X\to[-1,1]\doublecolon x\mapsto f(x) \midvert f=f_\vartheta\,,\; \vartheta\in\Theta}\,,

(8.69)

where for any
\vartheta\in\Theta
the function
f_{\vartheta}
is defined as

f_{\vartheta}(x)=\!\matrixelement{\psi_0\;}{
\qty(\prod_{l=1}^D U_l(\vartheta_l)^\dagger W_l(x)^\dagger) O \qty(\prod_{l=D}^1 W_l(x)U_l(\vartheta_l))
}{\;\psi_0}\,.

(8.70)

Here, each
U_{l}(\vartheta_{l})
is one layer of
k
-local unitaries

U_{l}(\vartheta_{l})=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\lfloor\nicefrac{{n}}{{k}}\rfloor}U_{li}(\vartheta_{li})\,,

(8.71)

where each
\vartheta_{li}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Moreover, each
W_{l}(x)
is an encoding layer, which we assume to consist of on-site unitaries

W_{l}(x)=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}W_{li}(x)\,.

(8.72)

Note that, depending on the choice
U_{li}
and the
W_{li}
,
most linear, as well as data re-uploading models can be phrased in terms of this architecture.

For a data generating distribution
x\sim\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{X}

we then define the
\mathcal{D}
-inner product on
\mathcal{F}
as

\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\to[-1,1]::(f,g)\mapsto\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\mathcal{D}}=\Ex_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\quantity[f(x)g(x)]\,,

(8.73)

and the corresponding
L^{2}
-distance as

\mathrm{d}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\to[0,1]::(f_{\phi},f_{\vartheta})\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\norm{f_\phi-f_\vartheta}^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}=\frac{1}{2}\Ex_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\quantity[\qty(f_\phi(x)-f_\vartheta(x))^2]\,.

(8.74)

In the following, we will consider the task of
\epsilon
-self-learning
\mathcal{F}
with respect to
L=\mathrm{id}
and
\mathrm{d}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}
.
In particular, the corresponding set of loss functions is given by

\mathcal{L}=\quantity{l^\phi:\Theta\to[0,1]\doublecolon\vartheta\mapsto l^\phi(\vartheta)=L\circ\met_{L^2(\mc D)}(f_\vartheta,f_\phi) \midvert\phi\in\Theta}\,.

(8.75)

To start with let us derive the following useful lemma.

Lemma 8.8:

Let
\gamma>0
,
\mathcal{F}
be a class of functions
f_{\vartheta}:\mathcal{X}\to[-1,1]
with parameter space
\vartheta\in\Theta
,
\mathcal{D}
a probability distribution over
\mathcal{X}
and
\mu
a probability measure over
\Theta
.
Denote by
\mathcal{L}
the class of loss functions corresponding to self-learning
\mathcal{F}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}
.
Then, for every
\vartheta\in\Theta
it holds

\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[l^\phi(\vartheta)]\leq 4\cdot\sqrt{\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[f_\phi]}\leq 4\cdot\sqrt{\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[f_\phi(x)^2]}\,,

(8.76)

where we used the variance with respect to the
L^{2}(\mathcal{D})
inner product

\displaystyle\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[f_\phi]=\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\phi}_{\mc D}]-\left\langle\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[f_\phi],\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[f_\phi]\right\rangle_{\mathcal{D}}\,.

(8.77)

Remark 8.5:

Informally, the lemma states that concentration of
f_{\phi}
with respect to
\phi\sim\mu
implies concentration of the loss function
l^{\phi}(\vartheta)
with respect to
\phi\sim\mu
.
As such, function classes, for which non-trivially learning is hard,
are often average-case trivial.

Proof of

8.8
:

To begin with, note that

\displaystyle l^{\phi}(\vartheta)=\frac{1}{2}\Ex_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\quantity[\qty(f_\phi(x) - f_\vartheta(x))^2]=\frac{1}{2}\Ex_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\quantity[\qty(\tilde f_\phi(x) - \tilde f_\vartheta(x))^2]=\tilde{l}^{\phi}(\vartheta)\,,

(8.78)

with
\tilde{f}_{\vartheta}(x)=f_{\vartheta}(x)-\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}[f_{\phi}(x)]
the “centered” version of
f_{\vartheta}
.
Thus,
\Var_{\phi}[l^{\phi}(\vartheta)]=\Var_{\phi}[\tilde{l}^{\phi}(\vartheta)]
.

Using

\displaystyle l^{\phi}(\vartheta)=\frac{1}{2}\norm{f_\vartheta}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\norm{f_\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}^{2}-\left\langle f_{\phi},f_{\vartheta}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{D}}\,,

(8.79)

and that for constant
y
it holds
\Var[X+y]=\Var[X]
, we bound

\displaystyle\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[l^\phi(\vartheta)]

\displaystyle=\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[
\frac{1}{2}\norm{f_\phi}^2_{L^2(\mc D)}
-\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_{L^2(\mc D)}
]

(8.80)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\norm{f_\phi}^2_{L^2(\mc D)}]+\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_{\mc D}]-\Cov_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\norm{f_\phi}_{L^2(\mc D)}^2,\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_{\mc D}]\,.

(8.81)

As for all
\phi
and
x
it holds
f_{\phi}(x)\leq 1
we bound

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu} \qty[\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_{\mc D}^2]}_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_{\mc D}^2]\leq\absolutevalue{\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu} \qty[\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_{\mc D}]}_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_{\mc D}]\leq\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\norm{f_\phi}_{L^1(\mc D)}]=\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[\abs{f_\phi(x)}]\,,

(8.82)

as well as

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu} \qty[\norm{f_\phi}^4_{L^2(\mc D)}]}_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\norm{f_\phi}^4_{L^2(\mc D)}]\leq\absolutevalue{\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu} \qty[\norm{f_\phi}^2_{L^2(\mc D)}]}_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\norm{f_\phi}^2_{L^2(\mc D)}]\leq\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\norm{f_\phi}_{L^1(\mc D)}]=\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[\abs{f_\phi(x)}]\,,

(8.83)

and similarly

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\qty[\norm{f_\phi}^2_{L^2(\mc D)}\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_{\mc D}]}_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\norm{f_\phi}^2_{L^2(\mc D)}\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_{\mc D}]\leq\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\norm{f_\phi}^2_{L^2(\mc D)}]\leq\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[\abs{f_\phi(x)}]\,.

(8.84)

Which can be bound by Jensen’s inequality
\Ex[\absolutevalue{X}]^{2}\leq\Ex[\absolutevalue{X}^{2}]
as

\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[\abs{f_\phi(x)}]\leq\sqrt{\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[f_\phi(x)^2]}\,.

(8.85)

Now, using
\Var[X]\leq\Ex[X^{2}]
and
\absolutevalue{\Cov[X,Y]}[X,Y]\leq\absolutevalue{\Ex[XY]}[XY]+\absolutevalue{\Ex[X]\Ex[Y]}[X]\Ex[Y]
together with the triangle inequality and
Equations

8.78
,
8.82
,
8.83
and
8.84
we conclude

\displaystyle\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[l^\phi(\vartheta)]=\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\tilde l^\phi(\vartheta)]\leq 4\cdot\sqrt{\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[\tilde f_\phi(x)^2]}=4\cdot\sqrt{\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[f_\phi]}\,,

(8.86)

which completes the claim.
∎

Let us now focus on two specific vQML models.
To begin with, we define linear models.

Definition 8.6:
(Linear Models)

Let
D_{E},D_{P}\in\mathbb{N}
and let
D=D_{E}+D_{P}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be as in
Equation

8.69
where
U_{l}(\vartheta_{l})=U_{l}
are independent of
\vartheta
for all
1\leq l\leq D_{E}
,
and where all
W_{l}(x)=\mathds{1}
for all
D_{E}+1\leq l\leq D
.
Then, we refer to
\mathcal{F}
as a
Linear Variational Quantum Machine Learning Model
, or, in short, a linear model.
We refer to
D_{E}
as the
Encoding Depth
and
D_{P}
as the
Parametrization Depth
.
Defining the
Encoding Unitary

W(x)
and the
Variational Unitary

U(\vartheta)
as

\displaystyle W(x)=\prod_{l=D_{E}}^{1}W_{l}(x)U_{l}

and

\displaystyle U(\vartheta)=\prod_{l=D}^{D_{E}+1}U_{l}(\vartheta)\,,

(8.87)

we can then write every
f\in\mathcal{F}
as
f_{\vartheta}(x)=\tr[\rho_{x}O_{\vartheta}]
, where

\displaystyle\rho_{x}=W(x)^{\dagger}\outerproduct{\psi_0}{\psi_0}W(x)

and

\displaystyle O_{\vartheta}=U(\vartheta)OU(\vartheta)^{\dagger}\,.

(8.88)

In particular,
f_{\vartheta}(x)=\left\langle\rho_{x},O_{\vartheta}\right\rangle_{HS}
can be written in terms of the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product.

Theorem 8.9:
(Linear Models and Barren Plateaus: Part I)

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the function class corresponding to a linear model,
\delta=2^{-\Omega(n)}
, assume that
U(\vartheta)\sim U\circ\mu
forms an additive
\delta
-approximate 2-desing and let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, the query complexity for non-trivial self-learning
\mathcal{F}
from
\tau
-accurate loss function queries with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}
and wit respect to te measure on
\mathcal{F}
induced by
\mu
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)=\frac{2^{\Omega(n)}}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(8.89)

In particular, if the
U_{li}(\vartheta_{li})
have equidistant spectrum, then the loss function
l^{\phi}
admits a barren plateau for every
\phi\in\Theta
.

Proof:
.

We can write the observable as
O=N^{-1}\tr[O]\mathds{1}+O_{\times}
with
\tr[O_{\times}]=0
.
By
8.8
it suffices to show

\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[f_\phi]=2^{-\Omega(n)}\,.

(8.90)

By assumption
U(\phi)\sim U\circ\mu
is an additive
\delta
-approximate unitary 2-design.
For
\delta=0
and denoting
2^{n}=N
it holds

\displaystyle\Ex_{\phi,\vartheta\sim\mu}\quantity[\lrangle{f_\phi,f_\vartheta}_\mc D]=\Ex_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\quantity[\Ex_{\phi}[f_\phi(x)],\Ex_{\phi}[f_\phi(x)]]_{\phi}[f_{\phi}(x)],\Ex_{\phi}[f_{\phi}(x)]=\frac{\tr[O]^{2}}{N^{2}}\,

(8.91)

and

\displaystyle\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[f_\phi(x)^2]=\Ex_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\quantity[\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu} \qty[\tr[U(\phi)^\dagger\rho(x)U(\phi)O]]^2]_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[U(\phi)^\dagger\rho(x)U(\phi)O]]^{2}=\frac{\tr[O^{2}]+\tr[O]^{2}}{N(N+1)}\,.

(8.92)

Using
\tr[O^{2}]\leq N
and
\tr[O]\leq N
this yields

\displaystyle\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[f_\phi]=\frac{\tr[O^{2}]N-\tr[O]^{2}}{N^{2}(N+1)}\leq\frac{1}{N+1}\,.

(8.93)

The claim for
\delta>0
then follows due to
6.6

while the claim about the barren plateau is because te equidistant spectrum implies by
[
WIW+22
]
an efficient parameter-shift rule.
∎

Similarly, the same holds if the encoding forms an additive approximate
2-design
.

Corollary 8.10:
(Linear Models and Barren Plateaus: Part II)

Let
\delta=2^{-\Omega(n)}
and let
\mathcal{F}
be the function class corresponding to a linear model, where
W(x)\sim W\circ\mathcal{D}
forms an additive
\delta
-approximate 2-design where
\mathcal{D}
is the distribution over
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, the query complexity for non-trivial self-learning
\mathcal{F}
from
\tau
-accurate loss function queries with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)\geq\frac{2^{\Omega(n)}}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(8.94)

In particular, if the
U_{li}(\vartheta_{li})
have equidistant spectrum, then the loss function
l^{\phi}
admits a barren plateau for every
\phi\in\Theta
.

Proof:
.

To see this observe that due to the linearity and for
\delta=0

\displaystyle\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}[f_{\phi}(x)]=\tr[\rho_{x}\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}[O_{\phi}]]=\tr[\rho_{x}\tilde{O}]\,.

(8.95)

Without loss of generality, assume that
\tr[O]=0
and hence
\tr[\tilde{O}]=\tr[O_{\phi}]=0
for all
\phi
(else, shift
O\mapsfrom O-N^{-1}\tr[O]\mathds{1}
which leaves the variance unchanged).
Then, we find

\displaystyle\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}[f_{\phi}]=\Ex_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi\sim\mu}}{{x\sim\mathcal{D}}}}\quantity[\tr[\rho_x O_\phi]^2]-\Ex_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi,\vartheta\sim\mu}}{{x\sim\mathcal{D}}}}\quantity[\tr[\rho_x O_\phi]\tr[\rho_x O_\vartheta]]

(8.96)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N(N+1)}\quantity(\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\qty[\tr[O_\phi^2]+\tr[O_\phi]^2] - \Ex_{\phi,\vartheta\sim\mu}\qty[\tr[O_\phi O_\vartheta] + \tr[O_\phi]\tr[O_\vartheta]])_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[O_\phi^2]+\tr[O_\phi]^2]-\Ex_{\phi,\vartheta\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[O_\phi O_\vartheta] + \tr[O_\phi]\tr[O_\vartheta]]

(8.97)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N(N+1)}\quantity(\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\qty[\tr[O^2]] - \tr[\tilde O^2])_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[O^2]]-\tr[\tilde{O}^{2}]\leq\frac{2}{N(N+1)}

(8.98)

The claim for
\delta>0
follows from
6.6

and the claim on the barren plateau follows the same argument as in the previous proof.
∎

Next, we investigate data re-uploading models in general.

Definition 8.7:
(Data Re-Uploading Models)

Let
D\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathcal{F}
be as in
Equation

8.69
.
Then we refer to
\mathcal{F}
as a
Data Re-Uploading Model
over
\mathcal{X}
of depth
D
with observable
O
.

Theorem 8.11:
(Data Re-Uploading Models and Barren Plateaus)

Let
D\geq 1
and
\delta=2^{-\Omega(n)}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a data re-uploading model of depth
D
with observable
O
. Let
\mu=\bigotimes_{l,i}\mu_{li}
be independently distributed over the local variables and
assume that locally
U_{li}(\vartheta_{li})
forms a
2
-design and either

•

O
is a Pauli string with
\Omega(n)
support,
or

•

O
is traceless and the unitary
U(\vartheta)=\prod_{l=D}^{1}U_{l}(\vartheta_{l})
forms an additive
\delta
approximate 2-design for
\vartheta\sim\mu
.

Then, the query complexity of non-trivial self-learning
\mathcal{F}
from
\tau
-accurate loss function queries with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)=\frac{2^{\Omega(n)}}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(8.99)

In particular, if the
U_{li}(\vartheta_{li})
have equidistant spectrum, then the loss function
l^{\phi}
admits a barren plateau for every
\phi\in\Theta
.

Proof:
.

The proof is analogous to that of
8.9
.
Since each
k
-local unitary
U_{li}(\vartheta_{li})
forms a
2
-design the average is invariant under the transformation
O\mapsto X^{\otimes n}OX^{\otimes n}
.
Therefore, for every
x
it holds
\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}[f_{\phi}(x)]=0
, which implies

\displaystyle\Var_{\phi\sim\mu}[f_{\phi}]=\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}[f_{\phi}(x)^{2}]\,.

(8.100)

Next, we note

\displaystyle\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[f_\phi(x)^2]=\Ex_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\sim\mu\\
x\sim\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\quantity[\mel**{\psi_0}{\prod_{l=1}^DU_l(\phi_l)^\dagger O \prod_{l=D}^1 U_l(\phi_l)}{\psi_0}^2]=\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[\rho(\phi)\otimes\rho(\phi) O\otimes O ]]\,,

(8.101)

where the local unitaries
W_{li}(x)
have been canceled by the 2-design property of the local
U_{li}(\vartheta_{li})
and where

\rho(\phi)=\prod_{l=D}^{1}U_{l}(\phi_{l})\outerproduct{\psi_0}{\psi_0}\prod_{l=1}^{D}U_{l}(\phi_{l})^{\dagger}=U(\phi)\outerproduct{\psi_0}{\psi_0}U(\phi)^{\dagger}\,.

(8.102)

The case where the
U(\phi)
form a
\delta
approximate 2-design then follows immediately from
8.9
.

We now consider the case that
O
is a Pauli string with support
\Omega(n)
.
To upper bound the second moment of
f_{\phi}(x)
consider the last layer of unitaries together with the observable
O\otimes O
in
Equation

8.101
.
Let
U_{li}
be a local unitary in the last layer that is connected to a non-trivial element in the Pauli string and let
P_{li}
be the corresponding Pauli substring.
By
6.1
we find

\displaystyle\Ex_{U_{li}\sim\mu_{li}}\quantity[U_{li}^{\otimes 2} P_{li}^{\otimes 2} U_{li}^{\otimes 2\,\dagger}]=\frac{2K}{(K-1)(K+1)}\mathbb{F}+\frac{2}{(K-1)(K+1)}\mathds{1}=\Theta\quantity(\tfrac{1}{K})\mathbb{F}+\Theta\quantity(\tfrac{1}{K^2})\mathds{1}=M

(8.103)

where we have used and defined
\tr[P_{li}^{\otimes 2}]=0
and
\tr[P_{li}^{\otimes 2}\mathbb{F}]=2^{k}=K
, defined
M=\Theta(\frac{1}{K})\mathbb{F}+\Theta(\frac{1}{K^{2}})\mathds{1}
and hence
\norm{M}\mathrm{op}\leq\frac{2K}{(K-1)(K+1)}
and where
\mathds{1}
and
\mathbb{F}
refer to the identity, respectively flip on
\mathbb{C}^{K}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{K}
.

By assumption, there exist
\Omega(n)
many
U_{li}
’s connected to non-trivial Pauli elements.
Hence, we can simplify
Equation

8.101
as

\displaystyle\Ex_{\phi\sim\mu}\quantity[\tr[\rho(\phi)^{\otimes 2} O^{\otimes 2}]]=\tr[\sigma M^{\otimes\Omega(n)}]\leq\norm{M}_{\mathrm{op}}^{\Omega(n)}=2^{-\Omega(n)}\,,

(8.104)

with
\sigma
the averaged state on the first
D-1
layers and
M
the observable averaged over the last layer as above.

We conclude with
q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)=2^{\Omega(n)}
in either case.

The claim about the barren plateau follows from the same arguments as in the previous proofs.
∎

8.3.5
A (Very) Simple Hard Class

In this section we compare our method to Anschuetz and Kiani’s statistical dimension due to their SQ framework
[
AK22a
]
.

We will significantly strengthen their Proposition 4 in
[
AK22a
, Supplementary Note 3]
.
We restrict ourselves to Proposition 4 and remark that
similar statements can be made for all other concept classes considered in
[
AK22a
, Supplementary Note 3]
by invoking our
8.10
.

Consider the function class
\mathcal{F}_{S}
consisting of functions
f_{U}:\mathcal{X}\to[-1,1]
,
with
\mathcal{X}
a set of
n
-qubit quantum states,
defined as

\displaystyle f_{U}(\rho)=\tr[\rho\quantity(\bigotimes_{i=1}^n U_i^\dagger)S\quantity(\bigotimes_{i=1}^n U_i)]=\tr[\rho U^{\dagger}SU]

(8.105)

where the vector of unitaries
U=(U_{1},\dots,U_{n})\in\mathcal{U}
is from some set of single qubit unitaries and
S
is a fixed signed non-identity Pauli string
S=\lambda P_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes P_{n}
with
\lambda\in\quantity{-1,1}
and
P_{i}\in\quantity{\id, X, Y, Z}
, with
(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\neq(\mathds{1},\dots,\mathds{1})
.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over
\mathcal{X}
such that
\rho\sim\mathcal{D}
forms a projective 2-design.

Note that this class is closely related to linear models as in
8.6
.

\mathcal{F}_{S}
can be understood as a linear model where the encoding is such that
W(x)
forms a
2
-design.
Then it holds.

Theorem 8.12:

Let
S
be a non-identity stabilizer and let
\mathcal{F}_{S}
be as defined in
Equation

8.105
for any
\mathcal{U}
with
\absolutevalue{\mc U}\geq 2
and let
\mathcal{D}
as above.
Denote by
\mathcal{L}_{S}
the set of loss functions corresponding to self-learning
\mathcal{F}_{S}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}
.
Then, for every probability measure
\mu
over
\mathcal{U}
it holds that the query complexity of non-trivial self-learning
\mathcal{F}_{S}
from
\tau
-accurate loss function queries with respect to
\mu
and
\mathrm{d}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)=\frac{2^{\Omega(n)}}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(8.106)

In particular, if
\mathcal{U}
is continuous and every
U_{i}
has an equidistant spectrum, every loss function
l^{U}\in\mathcal{L}
admits a barren plateau.

Proof:
.

The claim follows immediately from
8.10
with
\delta=0
.
∎

Remark 8.6:

Note that by
Equation

8.98

any
f_{U}
is exponentially concentrated around
\Ex_{U}[f_{U}]
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{D})
-norm.
Thus, we immediately find
\Var_{U}[\langle f_{U},f_{Q}\rangle]\leq 2^{-\Omega(n)}
,
which
in turn implies the same lower bounds on learning
\mathcal{F}_{S}
from quantum correlational queries of the form
Q^{\dagger}SQ
to
\mathtt{QCSQ}_{\tau}(U^{\dagger}SU)
.
This then corresponds to applying the parameter shift perspective in
[
AK22a
]
to parametrized learning (see their Eq. (16) and below).

Remark 8.7:

By
8.12
learning an unknown
U\in\mathcal{U}
requires at least
\nicefrac{{2^{2n}}}{{\tau^{2}}}
many queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}
for any non-trivial
\mathcal{U}
.
Thus, let
\mathcal{U}=\quantity{\id, X^{\otimes n}}
.
Then we immediately find deciding whether
U=\mathds{1}
or
X^{\otimes n}
requires exponentially many queries.
In contrast, a similar bound can, due to the set size of
\mathcal{U}
, never be obtained from the statistical dimension in
[
AK22a
]
.

8.3.6
Remark on Optimizing Local Hamiltonians

In the example about random initializations in
Section

8.3.1
we considered variational quantum eigensolvers (VQE).
VQEs aim at finding low energy states of a given Hamiltonian, where a class of Hamiltonians
\mathcal{H}
induces a class of loss functions
\mathcal{L}
.
A practically relevant and often considered special case is that where
\mathcal{H}
consists of local Hamiltonians
H=\sum_{i}h_{i}
only, with terms
h_{i}=\mathds{1}_{[n-k]}\otimes h_{i}^{(\mathrm{loc})}
that act non-trivially only on
k
qubits.
It is easy to see that from any parametrization that contains the computational basis states in every Pauli basis

\displaystyle\quantity{\ketbra{a_1,\dots,a_n}\midvert a\in\set{0,1,+,-,i,-i}^{\times n}}\subseteq\quantity{\rho(\vartheta)\midvert\vartheta\in\Theta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(N)

(8.107)

one efficiently learn
H
by estimating the
4^{k}\binom{n}{k}
many non-trivial coefficients in the Pauli decomposition of
H
.
In particular, for constant
k
one can from polynomially many queries to
\mathtt{Eval}_{\tau}(l_{H})
learn each
h_{i}^{(\mathrm{loc})}
to accuracy
O(\tau)
in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm and, hence, for
\tau=O(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mathrm{poly}(n)}})
sufficiently small, in any norm.
Therefore, our framework can only yield polynomial lower bounds for VQE optimization of local Hamiltonians, independent of the measure
H\sim\mu
.

Appendix

appendix.Aappendix.Bappendix.C

Appendix A
Boostable Learning Problems

Boostable learning problems are, in short, learning problems, for which a weak learner can query efficiently be boosted to a strong learner.
Since weak learning is only defined for specific learning problems – specifically PAC learning as in
2
with
\epsilon=1-\gamma
for a small
\gamma>0
– we formalize boosting via the following notion.

Definition A.1:
(Boostable Learning Problems and Exact Learning)

Let
0\leq\epsilon_{0}\leq\epsilon_{1}\leq\infty
and let
\mathcal{I}=[\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}]
(respectively
\mathcal{I}=[\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1})
if
\epsilon_{1}=\infty
).
A
Parameter Family of learning Problems
with parameter
\epsilon
and parameter set
I
is a family of learning problems
\quantity{\mc Z^\epsilon:\mc S\to\mc\mc P(\mc T) \midvert\epsilon\in\mc I}
.
We say
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
is
Boostable
if there exists a
c\geq 1
, such that for any
\epsilon\in\mathcal{I}
and any

s,r\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(s)\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(r)\neq\varnothing

it holds that

\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(r)\cup\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(s)\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{c\bar{\epsilon}}(s)\,,

(A.1)

where
\bar{\epsilon}=\min\quantity{\epsilon, c^{-1}\epsilon_1}
.
The special cases
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{1}
and equivalently
c=1
, are referred to as
Exact Learning Problems
.

The intuition of this condition is that the solutions of related source objects become increasingly similar at a sufficient scale.
In particular, let
s,r
be any two source objects with related solutions in the sense of
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(s)\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(r)\neq\varnothing
.
Then, the solutions to
r
at scale
\epsilon
are also valid solutions to
s
at scale
c\bar{\epsilon}
.

Example A.1:

For any
\mathcal{S}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
and
s^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}\setminus\mathcal{S}
the decision problem
\mathtt{Dec}(\mathcal{S},s^{*})
from
3.1
is an exact learning problem.
Similarly, any learning problem that can be defined by a parameter
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, a map
f:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
and a metric
m
on
\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{Z}(s)=\{t\in\mathcal{T}\;|\;m(t,f(s))<\epsilon\}
is, by the triangle inequality for
m
boostable.
This implies that PAC learning is boostable.

The following lemma will make the naming clear.

Lemma A.1:
(Boosting)

Let
\quantity{\mc Z^\epsilon\midvert\epsilon\in\mc I}
be a boostable learning problem and denote by
\bar{\epsilon}=\min\quantity{\epsilon,c^{-1}\epsilon_1}
.
Assume that, for
\epsilon_{0}\leq\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{1}
there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
with
oracle access
\mathcal{O}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
with probability
\alpha=1/2+\gamma
from
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}
.
Then there exists an algorithm that
learns
\mathcal{Z}^{c\bar{\epsilon}}
with probability
\alpha^{\prime}=1-\delta^{\prime}
from

\displaystyle q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{2\gamma^{2}}\cdot\ln\quantity(\frac{1}{\delta'})

(A.2)

many queries to
\mathcal{O}
. In particular, there exists an algorithm with success probability
1-\exp(-n)
from
\frac{qn}{2\gamma^{2}}
queries.

Proof:
.

Assume
\mathcal{A}
learns
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
from
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}
with probability at least
\alpha=1/2+\gamma
. For any boosting parameter
b>0
we define the following boosting algorithm
\mathcal{B}
.

Run
\mathcal{A}

b
times. This results in
b
solutions
t_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
,
i=1,\dots,b
.
Search for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
such that at least
\left\lceil\frac{b}{2}\right\rceil
solutions are contained in
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(s)
.
While this step is computationally inefficient it does not involve any further queries to
\mathcal{O}
.
If there exists such an
s
return any
t\in\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(s)
.

We now claim that With at most
b\cdot q
queries to
\mathcal{O}
algorithm
\mathcal{B}
successfully learns
\mathcal{Z}^{\bar{\epsilon}}

with probability at least
1-\exp(-2b\gamma^2)
.

To see this first observe that by construction
\mathcal{B}
makes no more than
b\cdot q
queries.
To verify the second part of the claim assume we run
\mathcal{B}
on an unknown
r\in\mathcal{S}
via access to
\mathcal{O}(r)
.
Define the random variable
X_{i}
as the event “
t_{i}\not\in\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(r)
”.
In particular,
X_{i}=1
if
t_{i}
is not a valid solution to
r
.
Then
\Pr[X_{i}=1]=\Ex[X_{i}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-\gamma=\overline{\alpha}
.
Defining the random variable
S_{b}=\sum_{i=1}^{b}X_{i}
we obtain from Höffding’s Theorem

\displaystyle\Pr_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}}\quantity[\frac{S_b}{b}\geq\frac 12]=\Pr_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}}\quantity[\frac{S_b}{b}-\overline\alpha\geq\gamma]=\Pr_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}}\quantity[S_b-\overline\alpha\cdot b\geq\gamma\cdot b]\leq\exp\quantity(-2b\gamma^2)\,.

(A.3)

Therefore, with probability
1-\exp(-2b\gamma^2)
for at least half the solutions it holds
t_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(r)
.
This implies that for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(s)
contains at least
\left\lceil\nicefrac{{b}}{{2}}\right\rceil
solutions it must hold
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(s)\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(r)\neq\varnothing
.
By
Equation

A.1
we then find
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}(r)\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{\bar{\epsilon}}(s)
, which proves the claim.
In particular,

\displaystyle b=\ln(\frac{1}{\delta'})\cdot\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}

(A.4)

suffices to obtain a failure probability of at most
\delta^{\prime}
proving
Equation

A.2
.
∎

With the previous
A.1
, we arrive at the following corollary.

Corollary A.2:
(Random Average-Case Lower Bound for Boostable Problems)

Assume a given boostable learning problem
\quantity{\mc Z^\epsilon\midvert\epsilon_0\leq\epsilon\leq\epsilon_1}
with
c\geq 1
and denote
\bar{\epsilon}=\min\quantity{\epsilon,c^{-1}\epsilon_1}
.
Let
\mu
be a measure over
\mathcal{S}
and suppose for some
\epsilon
there is a random algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}
that learns
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
with probability
\alpha
over
\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}
’s randomnes and probability
\beta
with respect to
s\sim\mu
from
q
many
\tau
accurate evaluation queries. Then for any
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
and any
\delta^{\prime}<1-\alpha
it must hold

\displaystyle q\geq\frac{2(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})^{2}}{\ln\quantity(\sfrac{1}{\delta'})}\cdot\frac{(1-\delta^{\prime})\cdot\beta-\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z^{c\bar\epsilon}_t]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(A.5)

Proof:
.

If
0<\alpha,\beta,\gamma
with
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}+\gamma=\alpha
and
\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}
is an algorithm such that

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\Pr_\A\qty[\A_\epsilon^{\eval_\tau(s)}\to\mc Z^\epsilon(s) \;\text{using at most $q$ queries}]\geq\alpha]_{\mathcal{A}}\quantity[\A_\epsilon^{\eval_\tau(s)}\to\mc Z^\epsilon(s) \;\text{using at most $q$ queries}]\geq\alpha\geq\beta\,,

(A.6)

then by the previous
A.1
for any
\delta^{\prime}<1-\alpha
and
\bar{\epsilon}=\min\quantity{\epsilon, c^{-1}\epsilon_1}
there is an algorithm
\mathcal{B}
such that

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[\Pr_\mc B\qty[\mc B^{\eval_\tau(s)}\to\mc Z^{c\bar\epsilon}(s) \;\text{using at most $\frac{q}{2(\alpha-\sfrac 12)^2}\cdot\ln\qty(\sfrac{1}{\delta'})$ queries}]\geq 1-\delta']_{\mathcal{B}}\quantity[\mc B^{\eval_\tau(s)}\to\mc Z^{c\bar\epsilon}(s) \;\text{using at most $\frac{q}{2(\alpha-\sfrac 12)^2}\cdot\ln\qty(\sfrac{1}{\delta'})$ queries}]\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}\geq\beta\,.

(A.7)

This implies

\displaystyle\Pr_{s\sim\mu,\mathcal{B}}\quantity[\mc B^{\eval_\tau(s)}\to\mc Z^{c\bar\epsilon}(s) \;\text{using at most $\frac{q}{2(\alpha-\sfrac 12)^2}\cdot\ln\qty(\sfrac{1}{\delta'})$ queries}]\geq(1-\delta^{\prime})\cdot\beta\,.

(A.8)

By
2.3
for any
f\in M^{\mathcal{X}}
it must hold

\displaystyle\frac{q}{2(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})^{2}}\cdot\ln\quantity(\sfrac{1}{\delta'})\geq\frac{(1-\delta^{\prime})\cdot\beta-\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z^{c\bar\epsilon}_t]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}\,,

(A.9)

or equivalently

\displaystyle q\geq\frac{2(\alpha-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})^{2}}{\ln\quantity(\sfrac{1}{\delta'})}\cdot\frac{(1-\delta^{\prime})\cdot\beta-\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Pr_{s\sim\mu}\quantity[s\in\mc Z^{c\bar\epsilon}_t]}{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\quantity[\met\qty(s(x),f(x))>\tau]}\,.

(A.10)

∎

Appendix B
Omitted Definitions for Mirror Descent

As in
Section

5.1
we denote by
\mathcal{V}
a linear space over
\mathbb{K}
where
\mathbb{K}
is either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
,
and denote by
\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle
the inner product in
\mathcal{V}
with the convention of anti-linearity in the first argument.
Let
\norm{\cdotspace}
be a norm on
\mathcal{V}
,
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\star}
its dual norm and
let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be some open convex set.
We refer to
\mathcal{W}
as the primal space.

Definition B.1:
(
\alpha
-Regularizer)

A function
R:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{K}
is called an
\alpha
-
regularizer
if it is strictly
\alpha
-convex with respect to
\norm{\cdotspace}
. In particular, for all
t\in[0,1]
and
x,y\in\mathcal{W}
it holds

R(tx+(1-t)y)\leq tR(x)+(1-t)R(y)-\frac{\alpha t(1-t)}{2}\norm{x-y}^{2}\,.

(B.1)

We say that
R
is a
0
-regularizer if
R
is strictly convex, this is
R(tx+(1-t)y)<tR(x)+(1-t)R(y)
.

Definition B.2:
(Bregman Divergence)

Let
\overline{\mathcal{W}}
be the closure of
\mathcal{W}
and let
R:\overline{\mathcal{W}}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuously differentiable. Then, the map

D_{R}:\overline{\mathcal{W}}\times\overline{\mathcal{W}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}::(x,y)\mapsto R(x)-R(y)-\left\langle\nabla R(x),x-y\right\rangle

(B.2)

is called the
Bregman divergence
of
R
.

Let us introduce the following regularizers and corresponding Bregman divergences on
\mathbb{R}^{N},\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
\mathbb{H}^{N\times N}
respectively:

\displaystyle R_{S}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}\ln(x_i)\,,

\displaystyle D_{S}(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\quantity(x_i\ln\tfrac{x_i}{y_i} -x_i +y_i)

(B.3)

\displaystyle R_{2}(x)=\tfrac{\norm{x}_{2}^{2}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle D_{2}(x,y)=R_{2}(x-y)=\tfrac{\norm{x-y}_{2}^{2}}{2}

(B.4)

\displaystyle R_{N}(X)=\tr[X\ln X]\,,

\displaystyle D_{N}(X,Y)=\tr[X\ln X-X\ln Y]+\tr[X-Y]\,.

(B.5)

Here,
R_{S}
is the negative Shannon entropy and
D_{S}
is the Kullback-Leibler divergence, or relative entropy and
R_{N}
denotes the negative von Neumann entropy and
D_{N}
corresponds to the quantum relative entropy.
A function
f
is
\alpha
-strictly convex with respect to a norm
\norm{\cdotspace}
, if its Bregman divergence satisfies

\displaystyle D_{f}(x,y)\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}\norm{x-y}^{2}\,.

(B.6)

Thus, by Pinsker’s inequlity it holds that
\zeta_{S}=1
with respect to
\norm{\cdotspace}_{1}
, trivially
\zeta_{2}=1
with respect to
\norm{\cdotspace}_{2}
and by the quantum Pinsker inequality
\zeta_{N}=\frac{1}{\ln 2}
with respect to
\norm{\cdotspace}_{\tr}
.

Definition B.3:
(Mirror Map)

A map
R:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
is called a
mirror map
if the following hold:

•

R
is an
\alpha
-regularizer

•

R
is differentiable on
\mathcal{W}

•

\quantity{\nabla R(x)\midvert x\in\mc W}=\mathcal{V}
, we say the
dual space
of
R
is all of
\mathcal{V}
.

•

The gradient diverges on the boundary
\lim_{x\to\partial\mathcal{W}}\norm{\nabla R(x)}=\infty
.

Definition B.4:
(Constraint Set)

A set
\mathcal{K}
is a
constraint set
with respect to
\mathcal{W}
if the following hold:

•

\mathcal{K}
is closed convex

•

\mathcal{K}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{W}}

•

\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{W}\neq\varnothing
.

Definition B.5:
(Bregman Projection)

The
Bregman projection
with respect to the mirror map
R
over primal space
\mathcal{W}
and with respect to constraint set
\mathcal{K}
is defined as

\Pi^{R}_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{W}::y\mapsto\argmin_{x\in\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{W}}D_{R}(x,y)\,.

(B.7)

In general the
\argmin
in
B.5
might not be well-defined as
\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{W}
is not a closed set.
However,
D_{R}(\,\cdot\,,y)
is
\alpha
-convex.
Moreover, due to the convexity of
R
and as
\nabla R
diverges on
\partial W
the minimum cannot be attained at

\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{K}

such that the
\argmin
will indeed be attained in
\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{W}
. See also Lemma 3.11.3 in
[
PC19
]
.

Appendix C
Metric Variance

The metric mean and variance generalize the mean and variance to general metric spaces.
This is necessary in spaces, in which convex combination, or addition and scalar multiplication more generally, is not defined, or not defined everywhere.
For all cases we have considered in this work the conventional definitions suffice and the metric mean and variance will reduce to the conventional notions
\Var[X]=\Ex[X^{2}-\Ex[X]^{2}]
.
However, for the sake of generality – and simply because of the beauty of this perspective on the mean and variance – we present here the more general framework for the metric mean and variance.

Definition C.1:
(Metric Variance and Metric Mean)

Let
(M,\mathrm{d})
be a metric space and let
X
be an
M
-valued random variable which is distributed according to
\lambda\in\Delta(M)
.

•

We define the
Metric Variance
of
X
as

\displaystyle\Var_{X\sim\lambda}[X]=\frac{1}{2}\Ex_{X_{1},X_{2}\sim\lambda}\quantity[\met(X_1,X_2)^2]\,.

(C.1)

•

Similarly, by

\displaystyle\Ex_{X\sim\lambda}[X]=\argmin_{v\in M}\Ex_{X\sim\lambda}[\mathrm{d}(v,X)^{2}]\,,

(C.2)

we denote the
Metric Mean
of
X
, if the
\argmin
uniquely exists.

In the above definition we have borrowed the definition of the variance in metric spaces from
[
Lei17
]
which is related by a factor of
2
to the
2
-diameter as defined in
[
Mém11
, Definition 5.2]
.
Crucially, for
M\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
\mathrm{d}(a,b)=\norm{a-b}_{2}
, the metric variance reduces to the conventional variance.
The metric mean is not necessarily well-defined: it may be non-unique or non-existent.
As such, it is astonishing that the metric variance does exist in any metric space.

Crucially, the connection between the variance and concentration represented by Chebyshev’s inequality – probably the most practical interpretation of the variance – does carry over to the metric variance as the following lemma shows.

Lemma C.1:
(Chebyshev’s Inequality for the Metric Variance)

Let
X
be a random variable over a metric space
(M,\mathrm{d})
distributed according to
\mu
. Then it holds

\displaystyle\Pr_{X_{1},X_{2}\sim\mu}\quantity[\met(X_1,X_2)>2\tau]\leq\frac{\Var[X]}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(C.3)

Proof:
.

The claim follows immediately from Markov’s inequality

\displaystyle\Pr_{X_{1},X_{2}\sim\mu}\quantity[\met(X_1,X_2)>2\tau]=\Pr_{d\sim\mathrm{d}\circ(\mu\otimes\mu)}\quantity[d^2>(2\tau)^2]\leq\frac{\Ex_{d\sim\mathrm{d}\circ(\mu\otimes\mu)}[d^{2}]}{\tau^{2}}=\frac{\Var[X]}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(C.4)

∎

Now,
2.5
can be straightforwardly generalized by Chebyshev’s inequality.

Corollary C.2:
(Variance Bound for
q_{\mathtt{nt}}
)

It holds

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)\geq\frac{\tau^{2}}{4}\cdot\Var_{s\sim\mu}[s(x)]^{-1}\,.

(C.5)

Further, if the metric mean
\overline{X}=\Ex_{s\sim\mu}[s(x)]
exists for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and it holds
\mathrm{d}(v,\overline{X})^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Ex_{X}[\mathrm{d}(v,X)^{2}]
for all
v\in M
, this can be improved to

\displaystyle q_{\mathtt{nt}}\quantity(\mu,\tau)\geq\tau^{2}\cdot\Var_{s\sim\mu}[s(x)]^{-1}\,.

(C.6)

Note that
\mathrm{d}(v,\overline{X})^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Ex_{X}[\mathrm{d}(v,X)^{2}]
holds in any convex metric space, and in words it merely states, that the deviation from the mean is, taking into account the weight due to
\mu
, symmetric around the mean.

Proof:
.

By Chebyshev’s inequality
C.1
, for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
most
s\sim\mu
are
2\tau
close to
v\in M
. Thus, there is a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to M
such that for every
x
the value
s(x)
as
s\sim\mu
will concentrate
2\tau
close to
f(x)
and hence
\mathrm{frac}(\mu,f,\tau)\leq\tau^{-2}\Var[s(x)]
.

The second claim follows from

\displaystyle\Pr_{X}\quantity[\met(X,\overline{X})>\tau]=\Pr_{X}\quantity[\met(X,\overline{X})^2>\tau^2]\leq\tfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\Ex_{X}\quantity[\met(X,\overline{X})^2]\leq\tfrac{1}{2\tau^{2}}\Ex_{X,Y}\quantity[\met(X,Y)^2]=\tfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\Var[X]\,.

(C.7)

∎
